% Right: residuals against fitted values.
\begin{scope}[shift={(4.2,0)}]
\draw[-Stealth] (0,-2.6) -- (5.0,-2.6) node[right] {\small $\hat y_i$};
\draw[-Stealth] (0,-2.6) -- (0,3.0) node[above] {\small $e_i$};
\draw[-,dashed,gray] (0,0) -- (4.7,0);
\foreach \v [count=\k] in {5.7,7.2,8.7,10.2,11.7} \draw[-] ({0.8*\k},-2.54) -- ({0.8*\k},-2.66) node[below] {\scriptsize $\v$};
\foreach \t in {-2,-1,0,1,2} \draw[-] (0.06,\t) -- (-0.06,\t) node[left] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\draw[red!70!black,thick] plot[smooth] coordinates {(0.8,-1.2) (1.6,0.3) (2.4,1.8) (3.2,0.3) (4.0,-1.2)};
\foreach \p in {(0.8,-1.7),(0.8,-0.7),(1.6,-0.2),(1.6,0.8),(2.4,1.3),(2.4,2.3),(3.2,-0.2),(3.2,0.8),(4.0,-1.7),(4.0,-0.7)}
  \fill[blue] \p circle (2.2pt);
\node[font=\small] at (2.4,-3.5) {Residuals against fitted values};
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Residual plots for the catalyst data of Example~\ref{exa:catalyst}. The normal plot
looks satisfactory; the plot against $\hat y$ has a clear arch, traced in red through the
mean residual at each concentration.}
\label{fig:catalyst-plots}
\end{figure}

\subhead{Residuals against fitted values}
A plot of the residuals $e_i$ (or $r_i$, or $t_i$) against the fitted values $\hat y_i$ is
the most useful single plot. Theorem~\ref{thm:resid-var}(iii) is the reason to plot
against $\hat y_i$: residuals and fitted values are uncorrelated, so when the model is
right the plot should show no trend at all, just a horizontal band of points centred on
zero. Plotting against the observed $y_i$ instead would be a mistake; $\mathbf{e}$ and
$\mathbf{y}$ \emph{are} correlated, and the plot would show a rising trend even for a
perfect model (Problem~\ref{prob:e-vs-y}).

The common patterns are in Figure~\ref{fig:resid-patterns}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item a \emph{horizontal band}: no evidence of inadequacy;
\item a \emph{funnel} opening outwards: the variance grows with the mean, as it often does
for counts, or when the errors are a percentage of the response;
\item a \emph{double bow}, wider in the middle: typical of proportions between $0$ and $1$,
whose variance $\pi(1 - \pi)$ is largest near $\pi = 0.5$;
\item a \emph{curve}: the model has the wrong shape; terms such as $x^2$, or a
transformation, or another regressor, are needed.
\end{enumerate}
Patterns (2) and (3) are violations of the constant-variance assumption A2. The usual
remedies, transforming $y$ or fitting by weighted least squares, are the subject of a later
section. Pattern (4) is a failure of the linear form itself, and is the most serious of the
three, since then the fitted model is systematically wrong.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\py/\col/\num/\name/\verdict/\pts in {
 0/0/green/1/{Band}/{satisfactory}/{(0.15,0.43),(0.29,-0.56),(0.42,0.50),(0.56,-0.23),(0.70,0.27),(0.84,-0.55),(0.97,0.12),(1.11,-0.52),(1.25,0.51),(1.38,-0.35),(1.52,0.27),(1.66,-0.26),(1.79,0.25),(1.93,-0.34),(2.07,0.37),(2.21,-0.39),(2.34,0.61),(2.48,-0.51),(2.62,0.42),(2.75,-0.60),(2.89,0.23),(3.03,-0.20),(3.16,0.42),(3.30,-0.14),(3.44,0.14),(3.58,-0.37),(3.71,0.35),(3.85,-0.57)},
 5.2/0/orange/2/{Funnel}/{variance grows with mean}/{(0.15,0.10),(0.29,-0.17),(0.42,0.19),(0.56,-0.10),(0.70,0.14),(0.84,-0.33),(0.97,0.08),(1.11,-0.39),(1.25,0.41),(1.38,-0.31),(1.52,0.26),(1.66,-0.26),(1.79,0.27),(1.93,-0.40),(2.07,0.46),(2.21,-0.52),(2.34,0.84),(2.48,-0.74),(2.62,0.65),(2.75,-0.97),(2.89,0.38),(3.03,-0.35),(3.16,0.77),(3.30,-0.27),(3.44,0.27),(3.58,-0.76),(3.71,0.74),(3.85,-1.24)},

\label{fig:resid-patterns}

For the catalyst data the right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:catalyst-plots} is pattern (4):
the residuals are negative at both ends and positive in the middle. The yield rises with
concentration and then levels off, and a straight line, forced to compromise, runs above
the data at the ends and below it in the middle. The normal plot did not detect this, nor
did $R^2$ or the $F$-test. Subsection~\ref{ssec:lof} gives a formal test that confirms
it.

\subhead{Residuals against the regressors}
In multiple regression, also plot the residuals against each regressor $x_j$ in turn. The
patterns mean what they did before: a funnel indicates non-constant variance, a curve that
$x_j$ enters the model non-linearly (perhaps needing $x_j^2$, or a transformation of
$x_j$). It is also worth plotting the residuals against variables \emph{not} in the model.
Any visible trend means the omitted variable carries information about $y$ that the model
is missing.

\subhead{Residuals in time order}
If the observations were collected one after another, plot the residuals in the order of
collection. Under A3 the plot should be patternless. Long runs of residuals of the same
sign indicate \emph{positive autocorrelation}, successive errors that tend to be alike;
rapid alternation of sign indicates negative autocorrelation. Correlated errors are common
when data are recorded over time, and they invalidate the variance formulas on which every
test so far depends. A formal test, and models for correlated errors, are given in the
section on autocorrelated errors.

\subhead{Partial regression and partial residual plots}
A plot of $e_i$ against $x_j$ shows whether $x_j$ is correctly represented, but it does not
show the effect of $x_j$ itself, because $x_j$ is correlated with the other regressors.
Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable} gives a plot that does.

\begin{defn}
Let $\mathbf{X}_1$ be the columns of $\mathbf{X}$ other than the one for $x_j$, with hat
matrix $\mathbf{H}_1$. The \textbf{partial regression plot} (or \emph{added-variable plot})
for $x_j$ plots
\[
\mathbf{v} = (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{y}
\quad\text{against}\quad
\mathbf{u} = (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{x}_j ,
\]
the residuals from regressing $y$ on the other regressors against the residuals from
regressing $x_j$ on them.
\end{defn}

\begin{thm}\label{thm:added-variable-plot}
In the partial regression plot for $x_j$, the least-squares line through the origin has
slope $\hat\beta_j$, the coefficient of $x_j$ in the full model, and its residuals are the
residuals $\mathbf{e}$ of the full model.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The slope of the line through the origin is $\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{v}/\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u}$.
Since $\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1$ is symmetric and idempotent,
$\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{x}_j^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{y}$,
so the slope is $\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{y}/\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u} = \hat\beta_j$ by
Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable}. The proof of that theorem showed
$\mathbf{H} = \mathbf{H}_1 + \mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^\top/\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u}$, so the
residuals of the plot's line are
\[
\mathbf{v} - \hat\beta_j\mathbf{u}
= (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{y} - \frac{\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u}}
= (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{e} . \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

(Both $\mathbf{u}$ and $\mathbf{v}$ are residuals from a fit with an intercept, so both add
to zero, and the least-squares line with an intercept is the same as the line through the
origin.) So the partial regression plot is a scatter plot that does for multiple regression
what the ordinary scatter plot does for simple regression: its slope is the estimated
effect of $x_j$, its scatter about the line is the residual scatter of the whole model, and
a curve in it shows that $x_j$ needs a non-linear term. Points that dominate the slope are
easy to spot.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:crop-avp}
Construct the partial regression plot for rainfall $x_2$ in the crop data.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{Regress $x_2$ on $x_1$.} $\bar x_1 = 3$, $\bar x_2 = 2.5$,
$\sum(x_{i1} - 3)^2 = 4 + 1 + 1 + 0 + 1 + 9 = 16$ and
$\sum(x_{i1} - 3)x_{i2} = -4 - 1 - 3 + 0 + 4 + 9 = 5$. The slope is $5/16 = 0.3125$, the
intercept $2.5 - 0.3125 \times 3 = 1.5625$, and $\mathbf{u}$ is $x_2$ minus the fitted values.

\emph{Regress $y$ on $x_1$.} $\bar y = 11.5$ and
$\sum(x_{i1} - 3)y_i = -12 - 9 - 11 + 0 + 15 + 54 = 37$. The slope is $37/16 = 2.3125$, the
intercept $11.5 - 2.3125 \times 3 = 4.5625$, and $\mathbf{v}$ is $y$ minus the fitted values.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrr}
Plot & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 \\
\hline
$u_i$ & $0.125$ & $-1.188$ & $0.813$ & $-0.500$ & $1.188$ & $-0.438$ \\
$v_i$ & $-0.875$ & $-0.188$ & $1.813$ & $-1.500$ & $1.188$ & $-0.438$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
(All the entries are exact multiples of $1/16$; they are rounded here.) Then
$\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u} = 3.9375$, so the slope is $1 = \hat\beta_2$,
and $v_i - u_i = -1, 1, 1, -1, 0, 0$, the residuals of the full fit, as
Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable-plot} says. Note also that
$\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u} = 3.9375 = 1/C_{22}$, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable}.
\end{solution}

A related plot uses the full model only.

\begin{defn}
The \textbf{partial residuals} for $x_j$ are $e_i + \hat\beta_j x_{ij}$, and the
\textbf{partial residual plot} (or \emph{component-plus-residual plot}) plots them against
$x_{ij}$.
\end{defn}

The partial residual adds back to the residual the part of the fit due to $x_j$. Its plot
against $x_j$ also has least-squares slope $\hat\beta_j$ (Problem~\ref{prob:partial-resid}),
and, because the horizontal axis is $x_j$ itself rather than a residual, it is the better
plot for seeing \emph{what shape} the dependence on $x_j$ has, and so for choosing a
transformation of $x_j$.

\subsection{Outliers}
An \textbf{outlier} is an observation that is unlike the rest: in a regression, one whose
response is far from where the model fitted to the other observations would put it. It
shows up as a large scaled residual, conventionally three or four standard deviations from
zero.

Outliers have causes, and the cause decides what is done. A \emph{bad value}, a reading
recorded wrongly, a faulty instrument, a unit that does not belong to the population, should
be corrected if possible, and otherwise removed. But an outlier may be a perfectly genuine
observation, telling us that the model is wrong in some region, or that the errors have
heavier tails than the normal. Such points should never be deleted merely to improve the
fit; they are often the most informative points in the data. The safe course is to fit the
model with and without the point, report both, and look for an explanation.

\subhead{A test for an outlier}
Theorem~\ref{thm:rstudent}(ii) turns R-student into a test. If observation $i$ was singled
out in advance, compare $|t_i|$ with $t_{\alpha/2, n - p - 1}$. Usually it was not: we look
at all $n$ residuals and test the largest. The chance that \emph{at least one} of $n$
statistics exceeds the critical value is then far above $\alpha$. The \emph{Bonferroni}
correction allows for this. Testing each at level $\alpha/n$, the probability of any false
alarm is at most $n \times \alpha/n = \alpha$ (the probability of a union of events is at
most the sum of their probabilities). So
\[
\text{declare observation } i \text{ an outlier if } |t_i| > t_{\alpha/(2n),\, n - p - 1} .
\]
The test is conservative, but critical values of this size are what a single extreme
residual among $n$ should be measured against.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:yield-outlier}
In the chemical-yield data of Example~\ref{exa:yield}, suppose the yield at $40^\circ$C had
been recorded as $80$ instead of $70$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|cccccc}
Temperature $x$ & 10 & 20 & 30 & 40 & 50 & 60 \\
\hline
Yield $y$ & 52 & 58 & 63 & 80 & 74 & 81 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit the line and look for an outlier.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
As before, $\bar x = 35$ and $S_{xx} = 1750$. Now $\sum y_i = 408$, $\bar y = 68$, and
\begin{align*}
S_{xy} = \sum(x_i - 35)y_i &= -1300 - 870 - 315 + 400 + 1110 + 2025 \\
&= 1050 ,
\end{align*}
so $\hat\beta_1 = 1050/1750 = 0.6$, $\hat\beta_0 = 68 - 0.6 \times 35 = 47$ and
$\hat y = 47 + 0.6x$. By Example~\ref{exa:slr-leverage},
$h_{ii} = 1/6 + (x_i - 35)^2/1750$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrr}
$x_i$ & 10 & 20 & 30 & 40 & 50 & 60 \\
\hline
$\hat y_i$ & 53 & 59 & 65 & 71 & 77 & 83 \\
$e_i$ & $-1$ & $-1$ & $-2$ & $9$ & $-3$ & $-2$ \\
$h_{ii}$ & 0.524 & 0.295 & 0.181 & 0.181 & 0.295 & 0.524 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 1 + 1 + 4 + 81 + 9 + 4 = 100$ and $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 100/4 = 25$. For the
fourth point,
\begin{align*}
r_4 &= \frac{9}{\sqrt{25 \times 0.819}} = \frac{9}{4.525} = 1.989, \\
S^2_{(4)} &= \frac{100 - 81/0.819}{3} = \frac{100 - 98.895}{3} = 0.368, \\
t_4 &= \frac{9}{\sqrt{0.368 \times 0.819}} = \frac{9}{0.549} = 16.39 .
\end{align*}
The other $|t_i|$ are all below $0.7$.

The studentized residual $r_4 = 1.99$ looks entirely unremarkable. But here $n - p = 4$,
so no $r_i$ can exceed $2$ in size: $r_4$ is as large as a studentized residual can be.
R-student shows what is happening. With the fourth point removed, the other five lie very
close to a line ($S^2_{(4)} = 0.37$, against $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 25$ with it), and the
fourth point is $16$ standard errors from that line. The Bonferroni critical value at
$\alpha = 0.05$ is $t_{0.05/12,\,3} = 6.23$, and $16.39$ is far beyond it: observation~4 is
an outlier. Checking the laboratory record would reveal the transcription error.
\end{solution}

\subsection{The PRESS statistic}
$R^2$ and $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ measure how well a model fits the data used to fit it. A model
always fits its own data better than it will fit new data, because the estimates have
adapted to the particular errors in the sample. A fairer measure predicts each observation
from a fit that did not use it.

\begin{defn}
The \textbf{prediction error sum of squares} is
\[
\mathrm{PRESS} = \sum_{i=1}^n \bigl(y_i - \hat y_{(i)}\bigr)^2
= \sum_{i=1}^n \left(\frac{e_i}{1 - h_{ii}}\right)^2 ,
\]
and the \textbf{$R^2$ for prediction} is
\[
R^2_{\mathrm{pred}} = 1 - \frac{\mathrm{PRESS}}{SS_T} .
\]
\end{defn}

The second form of PRESS is Theorem~\ref{thm:deletion}(iii); it means that PRESS, although
it is defined by $n$ separate fits, is computed from one. Since $0 < 1 - h_{ii} \le 1$,
every PRESS residual is at least as large as the ordinary one, so
$\mathrm{PRESS} \ge SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ and $R^2_{\mathrm{pred}} \le R^2$. A large gap between
them warns that the model will predict new observations much less well than its fit
suggests. PRESS is also useful for comparing models: other things equal, prefer the model
with the smaller PRESS. Points with high leverage contribute most, because their residuals
are divided by a small $1 - h_{ii}$.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:crop-press}
Compute PRESS and $R^2_{\mathrm{pred}}$ for the crop data.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The PRESS residuals were found in Example~\ref{exa:crop-scaled}. Exactly, from the
leverages $53/126$, $37/63$, $25/63$, $29/126$,
\[
e_{(1)} = -\frac{126}{73}, \quad e_{(2)} = \frac{63}{26}, \quad
e_{(3)} = \frac{63}{38}, \quad e_{(4)} = -\frac{126}{97},
\]
and $e_{(5)} = e_{(6)} = 0$. So
\[
\mathrm{PRESS} = 2.979 + 5.871 + 2.749 + 1.687 = 13.29 .
\]
From Example~\ref{exa:crop-anova}, $SS_T = 93.5$, so
\[
R^2_{\mathrm{pred}} = 1 - \frac{13.29}{93.5} = 0.858 ,
\]
against $R^2 = 0.957$. We may expect the model to explain about $86\%$ of the variability
in predicting new plots. PRESS is more than three times $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 4$, a reminder
that with six observations and three parameters the fit has adapted a good deal to its
data. Plot~2 alone contributes $44\%$ of PRESS: its leverage is high, and the other five
plots predict it badly.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Lack of fit and pure error}\label{ssec:lof}
The residual plots of Figure~\ref{fig:catalyst-plots} suggested that a straight line has the
wrong shape for the catalyst data. When the data contain \textbf{replicates}, repeated
observations of $y$ at the same value of $x$, the suspicion can be tested formally.

The idea is this. The residual sum of squares measures two things at once: the random
scatter of $y$ about its true mean, and any systematic failure of the line to pass through
the true means. Replicates measure the first on its own, because the scatter among
observations at the \emph{same} $x$ can owe nothing to the shape of the model. If what is
left over is much larger than replicate scatter would explain, the model has the wrong
shape.

\subhead{The decomposition}
Suppose there are $m$ distinct values $x_1, \dots, x_m$ of the regressor, with $n_i$
observations $y_{i1}, \dots, y_{in_i}$ at $x_i$, and $n = \sum n_i$. Let $\bar y_i$ be the
mean of the observations at $x_i$, and $\hat y_i$ the fitted value there. The residual of
$y_{ij}$ splits as
\[
y_{ij} - \hat y_i = (y_{ij} - \bar y_i) + (\bar y_i - \hat y_i) .
\]

\begin{thm}\label{thm:lof-identity}
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = SS_{\mathrm{PE}} + SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$, where
\[
SS_{\mathrm{PE}} = \sum_{i=1}^m \sum_{j=1}^{n_i}(y_{ij} - \bar y_i)^2 ,
\qquad
SS_{\mathrm{LOF}} = \sum_{i=1}^m n_i(\bar y_i - \hat y_i)^2 .
\]
$SS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ is the \textbf{pure-error} sum of squares, with $n - m$ degrees of
freedom, and $SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$ the \textbf{lack-of-fit} sum of squares, with $m - 2$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Square the split residual and add over $i$ and $j$. The cross term is
\[
2\sum_{i=1}^m (\bar y_i - \hat y_i)\sum_{j=1}^{n_i}(y_{ij} - \bar y_i) = 0 ,
\]
since deviations from a mean add to zero. The degrees of freedom are those of the
distribution theory below: $\sum_i (n_i - 1) = n - m$ for pure error, and
$(n - 2) - (n - m) = m - 2$ for lack of fit.
\end{proof}

$SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$ is a weighted sum of squared distances between the fitted line and the
means at each $x$: it is small when the line passes close to the group means. At least one
$x$ must be replicated for there to be any pure error ($n > m$), and there must be more
distinct $x$ values than parameters ($m > 2$) for lack of fit to be measurable.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:lof-test}
Under A1--A4 with $E(y_{ij}) = \mu_i$, where the means $\mu_i$ need not lie on a line,
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $SS_{\mathrm{PE}}/\sigma^2 \sim \chi^2_{n - m}$, so $MS_{\mathrm{PE}} = SS_{\mathrm{PE}}/(n - m)$
is unbiased for $\sigma^2$ whatever the true means;
\item $SS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ and $SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$ are independent;
\item if the straight-line model is correct, $\mu_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_i$, then
$SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}/\sigma^2 \sim \chi^2_{m - 2}$, and
\[
F_0 = \frac{SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}/(m - 2)}{SS_{\mathrm{PE}}/(n - m)} \sim F_{m - 2,\, n - m} .
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathbf{Z}$ be the $n \times m$ matrix of group indicators, with $z_{(ij),k} = 1$ if
$k = i$ and $0$ otherwise, and let $\mathbf{P} = \mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^\top$
be its hat matrix. Regressing $y$ on $\mathbf{Z}$ fits a separate mean to each group, so
$\mathbf{Py}$ replaces each $y_{ij}$ by $\bar y_i$, and $\mathbf{y}^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P})\mathbf{y} = SS_{\mathrm{PE}}$.
Like any hat matrix, $\mathbf{P}$ is symmetric and idempotent with trace $m$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:hat}).

Both columns of $\mathbf{X}$, the ones and the $x$ values, are constant within groups, so
they are combinations of the columns of $\mathbf{Z}$; hence $\mathbf{PX} = \mathbf{X}$ and,
taking transposes and using symmetry, $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{P} = \mathbf{X}^\top$. Then
$\mathbf{PH} = \mathbf{H}$ and $\mathbf{HP} = \mathbf{H}$, and so $\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H}$
is symmetric, with
\[
(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})^2 = \mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H} - \mathbf{H} + \mathbf{H} = \mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H},
\qquad
(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P})(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H}) = \mathbf{0} .
\]
Its trace is $m - 2$, and
$\mathbf{y}^\top(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{y}^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{y}^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P})\mathbf{y}
= SS_{\mathrm{Res}} - SS_{\mathrm{PE}} = SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$.

Write $\mathbf{y} = \boldsymbol\mu + \boldsymbol\varepsilon$, where $\boldsymbol\mu$ has entry
$\mu_i$ for each observation in group $i$; it is a combination of the columns of
$\mathbf{Z}$, so $(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P})\boldsymbol\mu = \mathbf{0}$.

(i) $SS_{\mathrm{PE}} = \boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P})\boldsymbol\varepsilon$,
and Lemma~\ref{lem:idempotent-chisq}(ii) applies, with $\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P}) = n - m$.

(ii) $SS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ is a function of $(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P})\mathbf{y}$ and
$SS_{\mathrm{LOF}} = \bigl((\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y}\bigr)^\top(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y}$
of $(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y}$. These are jointly normal with covariance
$\sigma^2(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{P})(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H}) = \mathbf{0}$, so independent by (F1).

(iii) If $\boldsymbol\mu = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta$ then
$(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\boldsymbol\mu = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta - \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf{0}$,
so $SS_{\mathrm{LOF}} = \boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\boldsymbol\varepsilon$
and Lemma~\ref{lem:idempotent-chisq}(ii) gives $\chi^2_{m - 2}$. The $F$ distribution
follows from (i), (ii) and (F3).
\end{proof}

When the line is wrong, $(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\boldsymbol\mu \neq \mathbf{0}$, and a
calculation like that of Lemma~\ref{lem:quad-form} gives
\[
E(SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}) = (m - 2)\sigma^2 + \boldsymbol\mu^\top(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H})\boldsymbol\mu ,
\]
where the second term is $\sum_i n_i(\mu_i - \tilde\mu_i)^2 > 0$, $\tilde\mu_i$ being the
value at $x_i$ of the least-squares line fitted to the true means. So $MS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$
estimates $\sigma^2$ when the model is right and something larger when it is wrong, while
$MS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ estimates $\sigma^2$ in either case. A large $F_0$ is evidence of lack of
fit.

\subhead{The lack-of-fit test}
To test $H_0$: the straight-line model is adequate, against $H_1$: it is not, compute
\[
F_0 = \frac{MS_{\mathrm{LOF}}}{MS_{\mathrm{PE}}}
\]
and reject $H_0$ at level $\alpha$ if $F_0 > F_{\alpha, m - 2, n - m}$. The calculations go in an extension of the analysis of
variance table:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
Source & SS & df & MS & $F_0$ \\
\hline
Regression & $SS_R$ & $1$ & $MS_R$ & \\
Residual & $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ & $n - 2$ & & \\
\quad Lack of fit & $SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$ & $m - 2$ & $MS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$ & $MS_{\mathrm{LOF}}/MS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ \\
\quad Pure error & $SS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ & $n - m$ & $MS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ & \\
\hline
Total & $SS_T$ & $n - 1$ & & \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
If $H_0$ is not rejected, there is no evidence against the model, and $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$,
which pools both sources and has more degrees of freedom, is used to estimate $\sigma^2$
in the tests and intervals that follow. If $H_0$ is rejected, the model should be revised
before any inference is drawn from it.

Everything extends to multiple regression, where the replicates are repeated observations
at the same \emph{combination} of regressor values. With $m$ distinct combinations and $p$
parameters, $SS_{\mathrm{LOF}}$ has $m - p$ degrees of freedom; the proof is unchanged, with
$\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{P} - \mathbf{H}) = m - p$.

\begin{remark}
Replicates must be genuine: separate runs of the process at the same settings, each
subject to all the sources of variation that affect any other run. Measuring the same
sample twice, or splitting one batch, captures only measurement error, which is smaller
than the true run-to-run variation. $MS_{\mathrm{PE}}$ is then too small, and the test finds
lack of fit that is not there.
\end{remark}

\begin{exa}\label{exa:catalyst-lof}
Test the straight-line model for the catalyst data of Example~\ref{exa:catalyst} for lack of
fit, at $\alpha = 0.05$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
There are $m = 5$ distinct concentrations, each with $n_i = 2$ observations, $n = 10$.

\emph{Pure error.} Each pair differs by $1$, so each pair contributes
$(\tfrac12)^2 + (\tfrac12)^2 = 0.5$, and $SS_{\mathrm{PE}} = 5 \times 0.5 = 2.5$ on
$n - m = 5$ degrees of freedom.

\emph{Lack of fit.} From Example~\ref{exa:catalyst}, $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 15.1$, so
$SS_{\mathrm{LOF}} = 15.1 - 2.5 = 12.6$ on $m - 2 = 3$ degrees of freedom. As a check, directly,
with group means $4.5, 7.5, 10.5, 10.5, 10.5$ and fitted values $5.7, 7.2, 8.7, 10.2, 11.7$,
\[
SS_{\mathrm{LOF}} = 2\bigl[(-1.2)^2 + 0.3^2 + 1.8^2 + 0.3^2 + (-1.2)^2\bigr] = 2 \times 6.3 = 12.6 .
\]
\emph{The table.} $SS_R = \hat\beta_1 S_{xy} = 1.5 \times 30 = 45$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
Source & SS & df & MS & $F_0$ \\
\hline
Regression & 45.0 & 1 & 45.0 & \\
Residual & 15.1 & 8 & & \\
\quad Lack of fit & 12.6 & 3 & 4.2 & 8.40 \\
\quad Pure error & 2.5 & 5 & 0.5 & \\
\hline
Total & 60.1 & 9 & & \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$F_{0.05,3,5} = 5.41$, and $F_0 = 8.40 > 5.41$ (the $P$-value is $0.021$). We reject $H_0$: the
straight line does not adequately describe how yield depends on concentration.

This confirms what the plot of residuals against fitted values suggested. The means rise
from $4.5$ to $10.5$ over the first three concentrations and then stay level, and a
straight line cannot follow that. Notice too that the line's $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 1.89$ is
nearly four times the pure-error estimate $0.5$ of $\sigma^2$, because the lack of fit has
inflated it. A curved model, for instance one with an $x^2$ term, should be tried.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:yield-resid}
For the original chemical-yield data of Example~\ref{exa:yield}, with fitted line
$\hat y = 46.3333 + 0.5714x$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item find the residuals, $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ and the leverages;
\item find the studentized residuals and the R-student residuals, and say whether any
observation is an outlier at $\alpha = 0.05$;
\item compute PRESS and $R^2_{\mathrm{pred}}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) With $\hat\beta_1 = 4/7$ exactly, the fitted values are $46.3333 + 0.5714x_i$, giving
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrr}
$x_i$ & 10 & 20 & 30 & 40 & 50 & 60 \\
\hline
$\hat y_i$ & 52.048 & 57.762 & 63.476 & 69.190 & 74.905 & 80.619 \\
$e_i$ & $-0.048$ & $0.238$ & $-0.476$ & $0.810$ & $-0.905$ & $0.381$ \\
$h_{ii}$ & 0.524 & 0.295 & 0.181 & 0.181 & 0.295 & 0.524 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In exact terms the residuals are $-1/21$, $5/21$, $-10/21$, $17/21$, $-19/21$, $8/21$; they
add to zero, and $\sum(x_i - 35)e_i = 0$. Then $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 840/441 = 1.905$ and
$MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 1.905/4 = 0.476$. The leverages are
$h_{ii} = 1/6 + (x_i - 35)^2/1750$, from Example~\ref{exa:slr-leverage}.

(b) With $r_i = e_i/\sqrt{0.476(1 - h_{ii})}$ and $S^2_{(i)} = (1.905 - e_i^2/(1 - h_{ii}))/3$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrr}
$x_i$ & 10 & 20 & 30 & 40 & 50 & 60 \\
\hline
$r_i$ & $-0.100$ & $0.411$ & $-0.763$ & $1.296$ & $-1.562$ & $0.800$ \\
$t_i$ & $-0.087$ & $0.364$ & $-0.714$ & $1.474$ & $-2.165$ & $0.756$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The largest $|t_i|$ is $2.165$, far below the Bonferroni critical value
$t_{0.05/12,\,3} = 6.23$. No observation is an outlier.

(c) The PRESS residuals $e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$ are $-0.100$, $0.338$, $-0.581$, $0.988$,
$-1.284$, $0.800$, so
\[
\mathrm{PRESS} = 0.010 + 0.114 + 0.338 + 0.977 + 1.648 + 0.640 = 3.727 .
\]
$SS_T = 26974 - 398^2/6 = 573.33$, so $R^2_{\mathrm{pred}} = 1 - 3.727/573.33 = 0.9935$, very
close to $R^2 = 0.9967$. The line should predict new runs about as well as it fits these.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:e-vs-y}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that under A1--A3, $\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{e}, \mathbf{y}) = \sigma^2(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})$.
\item For a model with an intercept, show that the sample correlation between the
residuals and the observed responses is $\sqrt{1 - R^2}$.
\item Explain why residuals are plotted against $\hat y_i$ and not against $y_i$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $\mathbf{e} = (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y}$ and $\operatorname{Var}(\mathbf{y}) = \sigma^2\mathbf{I}$, so
$\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{e}, \mathbf{y}) = (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\sigma^2\mathbf{I} = \sigma^2(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})$.
In particular $\operatorname{Cov}(e_i, y_i) = \sigma^2(1 - h_{ii}) > 0$ whenever $h_{ii} < 1$.

(b) With an intercept, $\sum e_i = 0$, so $\bar e = 0$ and
\[
\sum (e_i - \bar e)(y_i - \bar y) = \sum e_i y_i = \mathbf{e}^\top\mathbf{y} .
\]
Since $\mathbf{y} = \hat{\mathbf{y}} + \mathbf{e}$ and $\mathbf{e}^\top\hat{\mathbf{y}} = 0$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:hat}), this is $\mathbf{e}^\top\mathbf{e} = SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$. The sums of
squares about the means are $\sum e_i^2 = SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ and $SS_T$. So the correlation is
\[
\frac{SS_{\mathrm{Res}}}{\sqrt{SS_{\mathrm{Res}}\, SS_T}} = \sqrt{\frac{SS_{\mathrm{Res}}}{SS_T}} = \sqrt{1 - R^2} .
\]

(c) The correlation in (b) is positive for every data set, whether the model is right or
not. A plot of $e_i$ against $y_i$ would always show a rising trend, which would be a
property of least squares and not of the data. Against $\hat y_i$ the sample correlation is
exactly zero (since $\mathbf{e}^\top\hat{\mathbf{y}} = 0$ and $\bar e = 0$), so any pattern seen
there is genuine. For the catalyst data, $\sqrt{1 - 0.749} = 0.50$: a plot of $e$ against
$y$ would have shown a misleading upward drift.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:partial-resid}
Show that the least-squares line, with intercept, fitted to the partial residual plot for
$x_j$ has slope $\hat\beta_j$. Verify this for $x_1$ in the crop data.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The slope of the least-squares line of $w_i = e_i + \hat\beta_j x_{ij}$ on $x_{ij}$ is
\[
\frac{\sum (x_{ij} - \bar x_j) w_i}{\sum (x_{ij} - \bar x_j)^2}
= \frac{\sum (x_{ij} - \bar x_j) e_i + \hat\beta_j \sum (x_{ij} - \bar x_j) x_{ij}}{\sum (x_{ij} - \bar x_j)^2} .
\]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:hat}(iii), $\sum x_{ij}e_i = 0$ and $\sum e_i = 0$, so the first sum in the
numerator vanishes. The second sum, $\sum(x_{ij} - \bar x_j)x_{ij}$, equals
$\sum(x_{ij} - \bar x_j)^2$, so the slope is $\hat\beta_j$.

For the crop data, $\hat\beta_1 = 2$ and the residuals are $-1, 1, 1, -1, 0, 0$, so the
partial residuals $e_i + 2x_{i1}$ are $1, 5, 5, 5, 8, 12$ at $x_{i1} = 1, 2, 2, 3, 4, 6$. With
$\bar x_1 = 3$ and $\sum(x_{i1} - 3)^2 = 16$,
\[
\frac{-2(1) - 1(5) - 1(5) + 0(5) + 1(8) + 3(12)}{16} = \frac{32}{16} = 2 .
\]
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:mean-shift}
(The mean-shift outlier model.) Let $\mathbf{d}_i$ be the vector with $1$ in place $i$ and
$0$ elsewhere, and suppose $h_{ii} < 1$. Fit the model
$\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta + \gamma\mathbf{d}_i + \boldsymbol\varepsilon$,
which allows observation $i$ alone to have its mean shifted by $\gamma$. Show that
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $\hat\gamma = e_i/(1 - h_{ii}) = e_{(i)}$, the PRESS residual;
\item the residual mean square of this model is $S^2_{(i)}$;
\item the $t$ statistic for $H_0\colon \gamma = 0$ is the R-student residual $t_i$.
\end{enumerate}
So the R-student test for an outlier is the $t$-test of whether observation $i$ has a
mean of its own.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable} with $\mathbf{d}_i$ as the added column and
$\mathbf{X}$ as the others. Then
$\mathbf{u} = (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{d}_i$, column $i$ of $\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}$,
and since $\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}$ is symmetric and idempotent,
\[
\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{d}_i^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{d}_i = 1 - h_{ii},
\qquad
\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{d}_i^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{y} = e_i .
\]
(a) $\hat\gamma = \mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{y}/\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u} = e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$.

(b) The extra sum of squares due to $\gamma$ is
$(\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{y})^2/\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u} = e_i^2/(1 - h_{ii})$, so the
enlarged model has residual sum of squares $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} - e_i^2/(1 - h_{ii})$, on
$n - p - 1$ degrees of freedom. By Theorem~\ref{thm:deletion}(iv) its mean square is
$S^2_{(i)}$. (This is no coincidence: with $\gamma$ free, observation $i$ is fitted exactly
and the other $n - 1$ determine $\boldsymbol\beta$.)

(c) The coefficient $\gamma$ has $C_{\gamma\gamma} = 1/\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u} = 1/(1 - h_{ii})$,
so
\[
t_0 = \frac{\hat\gamma}{\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}/(1 - h_{ii})}}
= \frac{e_i/(1 - h_{ii})}{\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}/(1 - h_{ii})}}
= \frac{e_i}{\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}(1 - h_{ii})}} = t_i .
\]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:t-mlr} it has the $t_{n - p - 1}$ distribution when $\gamma = 0$, which
is a second proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:rstudent}(ii).
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:hii-one}
Show that if $h_{ii} = 1$ then $e_i = 0$ for every possible response vector $\mathbf{y}$.
What does this say about the role of observation $i$, and about its PRESS residual? Give a
simple model in which it happens.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
From the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:hat}(v),
$h_{ii} = h_{ii}^2 + \sum_{j \neq i}h_{ij}^2$. If $h_{ii} = 1$ this gives
$\sum_{j \neq i}h_{ij}^2 = 0$, so $h_{ij} = 0$ for $j \neq i$, and row $i$ of $\mathbf{H}$
is $\mathbf{d}_i^\top$. Then $\hat y_i = \sum_j h_{ij}y_j = y_i$, so $e_i = 0$ whatever
$\mathbf{y}$ is.

The fitted surface is forced through observation $i$: one parameter's worth of the model is
determined by that observation alone, so the data contain no check of it. Its residual is
zero not because it fits but because it cannot fail to. The PRESS residual
$e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$ is $0/0$, undefined, and correspondingly the fit without observation $i$
cannot be computed. With $\mathbf{v} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_i \neq \mathbf{0}$,
$\mathbf{Xv}$ is column $i$ of $\mathbf{H}$, which is $\mathbf{d}_i$; so every row of
$\mathbf{X}_{(i)}$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf{v}$, $\mathbf{X}_{(i)}\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{0}$, and
$\mathbf{X}_{(i)}$ does not have full column rank. Such a point is maximally influential, and it is studied in the next section.

An example: $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x + \varepsilon$ with every observation at $x = 0$ except
one, at $x = 1$. Then $\beta_0$ is estimated by the mean of the others and the slope is
determined by the single point at $x = 1$, which the line must pass through. Indeed
$\bar x = 1/n$, $S_{xx} = 1 - 1/n$, and
$h = 1/n + (1 - 1/n)^2/(1 - 1/n) = 1/n + 1 - 1/n = 1$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:lof2}
Nine runs of a process gave
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|ccccccccc}
$x$ & 1 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 5 \\
\hline
$y$ & 3 & 4 & 5 & 5 & 8 & 9 & 11 & 11 & 12 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Fit a straight line.
\item Carry out the lack-of-fit test at $\alpha = 0.05$, setting out the analysis of variance
table.
\item Estimate $\sigma^2$ in the light of your conclusion.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $n = 9$, $\sum x_i = 25$, $\sum y_i = 68$, $\sum x_i^2 = 91$, $\sum x_i y_i = 232$,
$\sum y_i^2 = 606$. Then
\begin{align*}
S_{xx} &= 91 - \frac{625}{9} = \frac{194}{9}, \\
S_{xy} &= 232 - \frac{25 \times 68}{9} = \frac{388}{9},
\end{align*}
so $\hat\beta_1 = 388/194 = 2$ and $\hat\beta_0 = 68/9 - 2 \times 25/9 = 18/9 = 2$:
\[
\hat y = 2 + 2x .
\]
(b) The fitted values at $x = 1, \dots, 5$ are $4, 6, 8, 10, 12$, and the residuals are
$-1$, $0$, $1$, $-1$, $0$, $1$, $1$, $-1$, $0$, so $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 6$ on $7$ degrees of freedom.
There are $m = 5$ distinct values of $x$. Pure error comes from the replicated levels:
at $x = 1$, mean $4$, $SS = 1 + 0 + 1 = 2$; at $x = 3$, mean $8.5$, $SS = 0.5$; at $x = 5$,
mean $11.5$, $SS = 0.5$. So $SS_{\mathrm{PE}} = 3$ on $n - m = 4$ degrees of freedom, and
$SS_{\mathrm{LOF}} = 6 - 3 = 3$ on $m - 2 = 3$. Also
\[
SS_T = 606 - \frac{68^2}{9} = 92.22, \qquad SS_R = \hat\beta_1 S_{xy} = \frac{2 \times 388}{9} = 86.22 ,
\]
and indeed $86.22 + 6 = 92.22$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
Source & SS & df & MS & $F_0$ \\
\hline
Regression & 86.22 & 1 & 86.22 & \\
Residual & 6.00 & 7 & & \\
\quad Lack of fit & 3.00 & 3 & 1.00 & 1.33 \\
\quad Pure error & 3.00 & 4 & 0.75 & \\
\hline
Total & 92.22 & 8 & & \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$F_0 = 1.00/0.75 = 1.33 < F_{0.05,3,4} = 6.59$ ($P = 0.38$). There is no evidence of lack of
fit.

(c) With no evidence against the line, the pooled estimate is used:
$\hat\sigma^2 = MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 6/7 = 0.857$, on $7$ degrees of freedom rather than the $4$ of
$MS_{\mathrm{PE}}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:catalyst-rstudent}
For the catalyst data of Example~\ref{exa:catalyst}, compute the R-student residual of the
largest residual, $e = 2.3$ at $x = 3$, and test whether it is an outlier at $\alpha = 0.05$.
Comment, in the light of Example~\ref{exa:catalyst-lof}.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Here $h = 0.1$, $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 15.1$, $n - p = 8$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:deletion}(iv),
\[
S^2_{(6)} = \frac{15.1 - 2.3^2/0.9}{7} = \frac{15.1 - 5.878}{7} = 1.317 ,
\]
\[
t_6 = \frac{2.3}{\sqrt{1.317 \times 0.9}} = \frac{2.3}{1.089} = 2.11 .
\]
The Bonferroni critical value is $t_{0.05/20,\,7} = 4.03$, so the observation is not an
outlier. That is the right conclusion: no single observation is out of line. The two
largest positive residuals both sit at $x = 3$, and the two runs there agree closely with
each other. The fault is in the model, not in any observation, and it is the lack-of-fit
test, not the outlier test, that detects it. A residual analysis should always look at the
pattern of the residuals as a whole, not only at the largest one.
\end{solution}

\section{Test for influential observations}
A least-squares fit treats every observation alike in its criterion, but the observations
do not have equal say in the result. A few points, usually ones at the edge of the region
covered by the regressors, can determine the slope almost on their own. If they are sound,
the model is still at their mercy; if they are wrong, so is the model. This section
measures how much each observation drives the fit.

Two ideas must be kept apart. An observation has high \textbf{leverage} if its regressor
values are unusual, far from the bulk of the data in $x$-space. Leverage depends only on
$\mathbf{X}$, not on $y$. An observation is \textbf{influential} if deleting it would change
the fitted model substantially. High leverage gives a point the \emph{potential} to be
influential; whether it is depends on where its response lies. Figure~\ref{fig:influence-types}
shows the three cases.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\col/\num/\name/\verdict/\sx/\sy/\yb in {
 0/orange/1/{Outlier}/{line barely moves}/2.0/2.6/0.25,
 4.9/green/2/{High leverage}/{precision gained}/4.0/2.76/0.0,
 9.8/red/3/{Influential}/{line is dragged}/4.0/0.45/0.0} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,0)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-0.35,-1.05) rectangle (4.35,3.75);
  \node[circle,fill=\col!60!black,text=white,inner sep=1.5pt,font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (0,3.4) {\num};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.25,3.4) {\name};
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (4.2,0);
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (0,3.0);
  % The bulk of the data, the same in each panel.
  \foreach \p in {(0.3,0.5),(0.5,0.75),(0.7,0.6),(0.9,0.95),(1.1,0.85),(1.3,1.2),(1.5,1.05),(1.7,1.4)}
    \fill[gray!70!black] \p circle (1.8pt);
  % Line without the special point (dashed) and with it (solid).
  \draw[-,dashed,gray!70!black] (0.1,0.42) -- (4.1,2.82);
  \ifnum\num=1 \draw[-,thick,\col!60!black] (0.1,0.6) -- (4.1,2.85); \fi
  \ifnum\num=2 \draw[-,thick,\col!60!black] (0.1,0.42) -- (4.1,2.82); \fi
  \ifnum\num=3 \draw[-,thick,\col!60!black] (0.1,0.95) -- (4.1,0.65); \fi
  \fill[\col!60!black] (\sx,\sy) circle (2.6pt);
  \node[font=\scriptsize,text=\col!50!black] at (2.0,-0.65) {\verdict};
 \end{scope}
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Leverage and influence: (1) an outlier with low leverage, (2) a high-leverage
point on the trend, (3) a high-leverage point off the trend. The grey points are the same in
each panel; the coloured point is the one in question. The dashed line is fitted without it, the solid line with it.}
\label{fig:influence-types}
\end{figure}

In panel~1 the point has a large residual but sits in the middle of the $x$-range; it raises
the line a little and leaves the slope almost unchanged. In panel~2 the point is far out in
$x$ but lies on the trend of the others; it has high leverage, yet the fit is the same with
or without it, and because it extends the range of $x$ it makes the slope \emph{more}
precise. In panel~3 the point is far out in $x$ and off the trend. It pulls the line round
towards itself, and since the line passes close to it, its own residual is small. This last
point is the dangerous one, and it is the one that residual plots are worst at revealing.

\subsection{Leverage}
The leverages $h_{ii}$ are the diagonal entries of the hat matrix. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:hat}, $0 \le h_{ii} \le 1$ and $\sum h_{ii} = p$, so the average leverage
is $p/n$. The next result shows what $h_{ii}$ measures.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:centred-leverage}
Suppose the model has an intercept. Let $\mathbf{Z}$ be the $n \times k$ matrix of
regressor values with each column centred at its mean, and let $\mathbf{z}_i^\top$ be its
row $i$, the vector of deviations $x_{ij} - \bar x_j$. Then
\[
h_{ii} = \frac{1}{n} + \mathbf{z}_i^\top(\mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{z}_i .
\]
In particular $h_{ii} \ge 1/n$, with equality exactly when $\mathbf{x}_i$ is at the centroid
$(\bar x_1, \dots, \bar x_k)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Subtracting $\bar x_j$ times the column of ones from column $j$ of $\mathbf{X}$ does not change
the column space, so the matrix $\mathbf{W} = [\mathbf{1} \;\; \mathbf{Z}]$ has the same column
space as $\mathbf{X}$. Projection onto a subspace does not depend on which basis is used to
describe it, so $\mathbf{H}$ is also the hat matrix of $\mathbf{W}$. (Directly: $\mathbf{W} = \mathbf{XA}$
for an invertible $\mathbf{A}$, and
$\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{W}^\top\mathbf{W})^{-1}\mathbf{W}^\top = \mathbf{XA}\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^\top)^{-1}\mathbf{A}^\top\mathbf{X}^\top = \mathbf{H}$.)
The columns of $\mathbf{Z}$ add to zero, so $\mathbf{1}^\top\mathbf{Z} = \mathbf{0}^\top$ and
\[
\mathbf{W}^\top\mathbf{W} = \begin{bmatrix} n & \mathbf{0}^\top \\ \mathbf{0} & \mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z} \end{bmatrix},
\qquad
\mathbf{H} = \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^\top + \mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^\top .
\]
Take the $(i,i)$ entry. $\mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z}$ is positive definite (it is invertible, and
$\mathbf{v}^\top\mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{v} = \|\mathbf{Zv}\|^2$), and so is its inverse,
so the second term is $\ge 0$ and is zero only when $\mathbf{z}_i = \mathbf{0}$.
\end{proof}

So $h_{ii}$ is $1/n$ plus a measure of the distance of $\mathbf{x}_i$ from the centre of the
data, a distance that takes account of the spread of each regressor and of the correlation
between them. With one regressor, $\mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z} = S_{xx}$ and we recover
$h_{ii} = 1/n + (x_i - \bar x)^2/S_{xx}$ (Example~\ref{exa:slr-leverage}). With two or more
regressors, the points of equal leverage lie on ellipses (ellipsoids) centred at the
centroid, so a point can have high leverage even when each of its coordinates, taken alone,
is ordinary: it is the \emph{combination} that is unusual.

The usual rule calls observation $i$ a \textbf{high-leverage point} if
\[
h_{ii} > \frac{2p}{n} ,
\]
twice the average. The rule needs $2p/n < 1$ to be of use, and is best used to pick out
points for a closer look rather than as a test.

\subsection{Cook's distance}
The most direct measure of influence asks how far the estimates move when observation $i$
is deleted. A natural scale for movements of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ comes from its
confidence region.

\begin{lem}[Joint confidence region]\label{lem:conf-region}
Under A1--A4,
\[
\frac{(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta)^\top\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta)}{p\,MS_{\mathrm{Res}}} \sim F_{p,\, n - p} ,
\]
so the set of $\boldsymbol\beta$ for which this quantity is at most $F_{\alpha, p, n - p}$ is a
$100(1 - \alpha)\%$ confidence region for $\boldsymbol\beta$. It is an ellipsoid centred at
$\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
$\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}^\top\boldsymbol\varepsilon$, so
$\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta) = \mathbf{H}\boldsymbol\varepsilon$ and the
numerator is $\boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top\mathbf{H}^\top\mathbf{H}\boldsymbol\varepsilon = \boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top\mathbf{H}\boldsymbol\varepsilon$.
$\mathbf{H}$ is symmetric and idempotent with trace $p$, and $\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}) = \mathbf{0}$,
so by Lemma~\ref{lem:idempotent-chisq} the numerator over $\sigma^2$ is $\chi^2_p$,
independent of $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}/\sigma^2 = \boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\boldsymbol\varepsilon/\sigma^2 \sim \chi^2_{n - p}$.
By (F3) the ratio of each to its degrees of freedom is $F_{p, n - p}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{defn}
\textbf{Cook's distance} for observation $i$ is
\[
D_i = \frac{(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)} - \hat{\boldsymbol\beta})^\top\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)} - \hat{\boldsymbol\beta})}{p\,MS_{\mathrm{Res}}} ,
\qquad i = 1, \dots, n .
\]
\end{defn}

$D_i$ is the quantity of Lemma~\ref{lem:conf-region} with $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)}$ in place
of $\boldsymbol\beta$: it says which confidence ellipsoid about $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ the
deleted estimate lands on. (It is not itself $F$-distributed, since $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)}$
is not a fixed parameter; the comparison is a yardstick, not a test.) The median of
$F_{p, n - p}$ is close to $1$ for most $p$ and $n$, so $D_i = 1$ means that deleting the point
moves the estimate to the edge of a $50\%$ confidence region, a large move. The usual rule is
to regard points with
\[
D_i > 1
\]
as influential.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:cook}
Suppose $h_{ii} < 1$. Then
\[
D_i = \frac{r_i^2}{p}\cdot\frac{h_{ii}}{1 - h_{ii}}
= \frac{\|\hat{\mathbf{y}} - \hat{\mathbf{y}}_{(i)}\|^2}{p\,MS_{\mathrm{Res}}} ,
\]
where $\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{(i)} = \mathbf{X}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)}$ is the vector of all $n$
fitted values from the fit without observation $i$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The second form holds because
$\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)}) = \hat{\mathbf{y}} - \hat{\mathbf{y}}_{(i)}$
and $\mathbf{v}^\top\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}\mathbf{v} = \|\mathbf{Xv}\|^2$. For the first, by
Theorem~\ref{thm:deletion}(ii), $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)} = \mathbf{C}\mathbf{x}_i e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$,
so, using $\mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}\mathbf{C} = \mathbf{C}$ and $\mathbf{x}_i^\top\mathbf{C}\mathbf{x}_i = h_{ii}$,
\[
(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)})^\top\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)})
= \frac{e_i^2\,\mathbf{x}_i^\top\mathbf{C}\mathbf{x}_i}{(1 - h_{ii})^2} = \frac{e_i^2 h_{ii}}{(1 - h_{ii})^2} .
\]
Divide by $p\,MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ and recall $r_i^2 = e_i^2/\bigl(MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(1 - h_{ii})\bigr)$:
\[
D_i = \frac{e_i^2}{MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(1 - h_{ii})}\cdot\frac{h_{ii}}{p(1 - h_{ii})} = \frac{r_i^2}{p}\cdot\frac{h_{ii}}{1 - h_{ii}} . \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

The theorem is the formal statement of the three panels of
Figure~\ref{fig:influence-types}. Cook's distance is a product of two factors: $r_i^2$, which
measures how badly the point fits, and $h_{ii}/(1 - h_{ii})$, which grows without bound as
$h_{ii} \to 1$ and measures how much leverage the point has. A large $D_i$ needs both, or an
extreme amount of one. A point with zero residual has $D_i = 0$ whatever its leverage; a point
with leverage $1/n$ at the centroid has $h_{ii}/(1 - h_{ii}) = 1/(n - 1)$ and so can have a
large $D_i$ only with a very large residual.

\subsection{DFFITS and DFBETAS}
Cook's distance summarises the effect of a deletion on all of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ at once.
Two further measures look at particular effects: on the fitted value at the deleted point,
and on each coefficient separately. Both are scaled by the deleted estimate $S^2_{(i)}$, for
the reason given in the discussion of R-student: a bad point should not be allowed to
inflate the yardstick used to judge it.

\begin{defn}
\[
\mathrm{DFFITS}_i = \frac{\hat y_i - \hat y_{(i)}}{\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}\,h_{ii}}} ,
\qquad
\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i} = \frac{\hat\beta_j - \hat\beta_{j(i)}}{\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}\,C_{jj}}} ,
\]
where $\hat y_{(i)} = \mathbf{x}_i^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)}$ and $\hat\beta_{j(i)}$ is
entry $j$ of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)}$.
\end{defn}

$\mathrm{DFFITS}_i$ is the change in the fitted value at $\mathbf{x}_i$, in units of (an estimate
of) the standard deviation of $\hat y_i$, which is $\sigma\sqrt{h_{ii}}$. Similarly
$\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i}$ is the change in $\hat\beta_j$, in units of its standard error. Both
can be computed from the full fit.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:dffits}
Suppose $h_{ii} < 1$, and let $\mathbf{R} = \mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}^\top$, a $p \times n$ matrix with
entries $\rho_{ji}$. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\mathrm{DFFITS}_i = t_i\sqrt{\dfrac{h_{ii}}{1 - h_{ii}}}$;
\item $\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i} = \dfrac{\rho_{ji}}{\sqrt{C_{jj}}}\cdot\dfrac{t_i}{\sqrt{1 - h_{ii}}}$,
and $\sum_{i=1}^n \rho_{ji}^2 = C_{jj}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(i) By Theorem~\ref{thm:deletion}(iii), $\hat y_i - \hat y_{(i)} = (y_i - \hat y_{(i)}) - (y_i - \hat y_i)$
$= e_i/(1 - h_{ii}) - e_i = h_{ii}e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$. So
\begin{align*}
\mathrm{DFFITS}_i &= \frac{h_{ii}e_i}{(1 - h_{ii})\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}h_{ii}}}
= \frac{e_i}{\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}(1 - h_{ii})}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{h_{ii}}{1 - h_{ii}}} \\
&= t_i\sqrt{\frac{h_{ii}}{1 - h_{ii}}} .
\end{align*}
(ii) Column $i$ of $\mathbf{R}$ is $\mathbf{C}\mathbf{x}_i$, so by Theorem~\ref{thm:deletion}(ii),
$\hat\beta_j - \hat\beta_{j(i)} = \rho_{ji}e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$. Hence
\[
\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i} = \frac{\rho_{ji}}{\sqrt{C_{jj}}}\cdot\frac{e_i}{(1 - h_{ii})\sqrt{S^2_{(i)}}}
= \frac{\rho_{ji}}{\sqrt{C_{jj}}}\cdot\frac{t_i}{\sqrt{1 - h_{ii}}} .
\]
Finally $\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^\top = \mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}\mathbf{C} = \mathbf{C}$, whose
$(j,j)$ entry is $\sum_i \rho_{ji}^2 = C_{jj}$.
\end{proof}

DFFITS has the same structure as Cook's distance, a residual times a leverage factor; indeed
$\mathrm{DFFITS}_i^2 = p\,D_i\,MS_{\mathrm{Res}}/S^2_{(i)}$ (Problem~\ref{prob:dffits-cook}).
The difference is that it uses R-student, which is not bounded, so DFFITS reacts more
strongly to a gross outlier. In DFBETAS, the factor $\rho_{ji}/\sqrt{C_{jj}}$ is the share of
observation $i$ in the estimate $\hat\beta_j = \sum_i \rho_{ji}y_i$: by part (ii) the squares of
these shares add up to $1$ over $i$.

The usual cut-offs, which allow for the fact that every measure shrinks as $n$ grows, are
\[
|\mathrm{DFFITS}_i| > 2\sqrt{p/n} ,
\qquad
|\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i}| > 2/\sqrt{n} .
\]

\subsection{The effect on precision: COVRATIO}
The measures so far look at the estimates. An observation can also matter through the
\emph{precision} of the estimates: a sound point at high leverage reduces the variances of
the coefficients (panel~2 of Figure~\ref{fig:influence-types}), while a wild point inflates
$MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ and so the standard errors. A single number for the precision of
$\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ is the determinant of its estimated covariance matrix,
$\det(MS_{\mathrm{Res}}\mathbf{C})$, the \emph{generalised variance}. It is proportional to the
squared volume of the confidence ellipsoid of Lemma~\ref{lem:conf-region}.

\begin{defn}
\[
\mathrm{COVRATIO}_i = \frac{\det\bigl(S^2_{(i)}(\mathbf{X}_{(i)}^\top\mathbf{X}_{(i)})^{-1}\bigr)}{\det\bigl(MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}\bigr)} .
\]
\end{defn}

$\mathrm{COVRATIO}_i > 1$ means the estimates are less precise without observation $i$: the
point improves precision. $\mathrm{COVRATIO}_i < 1$ means they are more precise without it: the
point degrades precision. The computation needs one fact about determinants.

\begin{lem}[Matrix determinant lemma]\label{lem:det}
If $\mathbf{A}$ is an invertible $p \times p$ matrix and $\mathbf{u}$ a $p$-vector, then
$\det(\mathbf{A} - \mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^\top) = \det(\mathbf{A})\,(1 - \mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u})$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Factorise the $(p + 1) \times (p + 1)$ matrix
$\mathbf{M} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{A} & \mathbf{u} \\ \mathbf{u}^\top & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ in two
ways, by eliminating below the top-left block and above the bottom-right entry:
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{M} &= \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I} & \mathbf{0} \\ \mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{A}^{-1} & 1 \end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{A} & \mathbf{u} \\ \mathbf{0}^\top & 1 - \mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u} \end{bmatrix} , \\
\mathbf{M} &= \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I} & \mathbf{u} \\ \mathbf{0}^\top & 1 \end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{A} - \mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^\top & \mathbf{0} \\ \mathbf{u}^\top & 1 \end{bmatrix} .
\end{align*}
Each factor is block triangular, and the determinant of a block-triangular matrix is the
product of the determinants of its diagonal blocks. The first line gives
$\det\mathbf{M} = \det(\mathbf{A})(1 - \mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u})$ and the second
$\det\mathbf{M} = \det(\mathbf{A} - \mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^\top)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}\label{thm:covratio}
Suppose $h_{ii} < 1$. Then
\[
\mathrm{COVRATIO}_i = \left(\frac{S^2_{(i)}}{MS_{\mathrm{Res}}}\right)^p\frac{1}{1 - h_{ii}}
= \left(\frac{n - p - r_i^2}{n - p - 1}\right)^p\frac{1}{1 - h_{ii}} .
\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For a $p \times p$ matrix, $\det(c\mathbf{B}) = c^p\det\mathbf{B}$ and
$\det(\mathbf{B}^{-1}) = 1/\det\mathbf{B}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:det} with
$\mathbf{A} = \mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}$ and $\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{x}_i$,
$\det(\mathbf{X}_{(i)}^\top\mathbf{X}_{(i)}) = \det(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X} - \mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}_i^\top) = \det(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})(1 - h_{ii})$.
So
\[
\mathrm{COVRATIO}_i = \frac{(S^2_{(i)})^p\big/\bigl[\det(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})(1 - h_{ii})\bigr]}{MS_{\mathrm{Res}}^p\big/\det(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})} ,
\]
which is the first form. The second follows from
$(n - p - 1)S^2_{(i)}/MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = n - p - r_i^2$, shown in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:rstudent}(iii).
\end{proof}

The two factors pull in opposite directions. High leverage makes $1/(1 - h_{ii})$ large,
pushing COVRATIO above $1$: the point extends the design and sharpens the estimates. A large
residual makes $S^2_{(i)}/MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ small, pushing COVRATIO below $1$. The usual rule
looks closer at observation $i$ if
\[
\mathrm{COVRATIO}_i > 1 + \frac{3p}{n} \quad\text{or}\quad \mathrm{COVRATIO}_i < 1 - \frac{3p}{n} ,
\]
the lower limit being of use only when $n > 3p$.

\subsection{Applying the measures}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
Measure & What it measures & Flag if \\
\hline
$h_{ii}$ & remoteness in $x$-space & $h_{ii} > 2p/n$ \\
$t_i$ & poor fit (outlier) & $|t_i| > t_{\alpha/(2n), n - p - 1}$ \\
$D_i$ & movement of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ & $D_i > 1$ \\
$\mathrm{DFFITS}_i$ & change in own fitted value & $|\mathrm{DFFITS}_i| > 2\sqrt{p/n}$ \\
$\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i}$ & change in $\hat\beta_j$ & $|\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i}| > 2/\sqrt{n}$ \\
$\mathrm{COVRATIO}_i$ & change in precision & outside $1 \pm 3p/n$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The cut-offs are rules of thumb, not tests, and different measures answer different
questions: a point can move one coefficient a great deal and leave the others alone, so that
DFBETAS flags it and Cook's distance does not. In practice all of them are computed, and any
point that stands out on any of them is examined.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:shops}
A chain has eight branches. For each, the floor area $x$ (hundreds of m$^2$) and the
average weekly sales $y$ (thousands of kwacha) are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|cccccccc}
Branch & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 & 8 \\
\hline
Area $x$ & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 & 20 \\
Sales $y$ & 3 & 5 & 5 & 8 & 8 & 9 & 11 & 9 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Branch~8 is a large warehouse store on the edge of town. Fit a straight line and examine the
influence of each branch.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{The fit.} $\sum x_i = 48$, $\sum y_i = 58$, so $\bar x = 6$ and $\bar y = 7.25$. Then
\begin{align*}
S_{xx} &= \sum(x_i - 6)^2 = 25 + 16 + 9 + 4 + 1 + 0 + 1 + 196 = 252, \\
S_{xy} &= \sum(x_i - 6)y_i = -15 - 20 - 15 - 16 - 8 + 0 + 11 + 126 = 63,
\end{align*}
so $\hat\beta_1 = 63/252 = 0.25$, $\hat\beta_0 = 7.25 - 0.25 \times 6 = 5.75$, and
\[
\hat y = 5.75 + 0.25x .
\]
The residuals are $y_i - 5.75 - 0.25x_i$, and $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 33.75$, so
$MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 33.75/6 = 5.625$. Also $SS_T = 49.5$ and $R^2 = 0.318$.

\emph{Leverage and residuals}, with $h_{ii} = 1/8 + (x_i - 6)^2/252$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrr}
Branch & $e_i$ & $h_{ii}$ & $r_i$ & $S^2_{(i)}$ & $t_i$ \\
\hline
1 & $-3.00$ & 0.224 & $-1.436$ & 4.430 & $-1.618$ \\
2 & $-1.25$ & 0.188 & $-0.585$ & 6.365 & $-0.550$ \\
3 & $-1.50$ & 0.161 & $-0.690$ & 6.214 & $-0.657$ \\
4 & $1.25$ & 0.141 & $0.569$ & 6.386 & $0.534$ \\
5 & $1.00$ & 0.129 & $0.452$ & 6.520 & $0.420$ \\
6 & $1.75$ & 0.125 & $0.789$ & 6.050 & $0.761$ \\
7 & $3.50$ & 0.129 & $1.581$ & 3.937 & $1.890$ \\
8 & $-1.75$ & 0.903 & $-2.366$ & 0.450 & $-8.367$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For branch~8 exactly, $h_{88} = 1/8 + 196/252 = 1/8 + 7/9 = 65/72$, so $1 - h_{88} = 7/72$, and
\begin{align*}
r_8^2 &= \frac{1.75^2}{5.625 \times 7/72} = \frac{3.0625}{0.546875} = 5.6, \\
S^2_{(8)} &= \frac{33.75 - 3.0625/(7/72)}{5} = \frac{33.75 - 31.5}{5} = 0.45, \\
t_8 &= \frac{-1.75}{\sqrt{0.45 \times 7/72}} = \frac{-1.75}{0.2092} = -8.367 .
\end{align*}
Branch~8 has by far the highest leverage, $0.903$ against the cut-off $2p/n = 0.5$. Its raw
residual, $-1.75$, is smaller than those of branches~1 and~7, and a plot of residuals against
fitted values would not single it out. Its R-student residual tells a different story:
$|t_8| = 8.37$ exceeds the Bonferroni value $t_{0.05/16,\,5} = 4.53$.

\emph{Influence measures.} With $p = 2$, $n = 8$, the cut-offs are $D_i > 1$,
$|\mathrm{DFFITS}_i| > 2\sqrt{2/8} = 1$, $|\mathrm{DFBETAS}_{j,i}| > 2/\sqrt8 = 0.707$ and
COVRATIO outside $(0.25, 1.75)$. For branch~8, by Theorems~\ref{thm:cook}, \ref{thm:dffits}
and~\ref{thm:covratio},
\begin{align*}
D_8 &= \frac{5.6}{2}\cdot\frac{65/72}{7/72} = 2.8 \times \frac{65}{7} = 26, \\
\mathrm{DFFITS}_8 &= -8.367\sqrt{65/7} = -8.367 \times 3.047 = -25.5, \\
\mathrm{COVRATIO}_8 &= \left(\frac{0.45}{5.625}\right)^2\frac{72}{7} = 0.0064 \times 10.29 = 0.066 .
\end{align*}
For all eight branches:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrr}
Branch & $D_i$ & DFFITS & DFBETAS$_0$ & DFBETAS$_1$ & COVRATIO \\
\hline
1 & 0.298 & $-0.870$ & $-0.866$ & $0.579$ & 0.799 \\
2 & 0.040 & $-0.265$ & $-0.260$ & $0.154$ & 1.578 \\
3 & 0.046 & $-0.287$ & $-0.272$ & $0.136$ & 1.454 \\
4 & 0.027 & $0.216$ & $0.192$ & $-0.073$ & 1.500 \\
5 & 0.015 & $0.162$ & $0.129$ & $-0.028$ & 1.543 \\
6 & 0.044 & $0.288$ & $0.196$ & $0.000$ & 1.322 \\
7 & 0.185 & $0.727$ & $0.396$ & $0.128$ & 0.563 \\
8 & 26.0 & $-25.5$ & $10.80$ & $-23.66$ & 0.066 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Branch~8 is flagged by every measure, and overwhelmingly: $D_8 = 26$ is twenty-six times the
cut-off, and its DFBETAS for the slope says that deleting it changes $\hat\beta_1$ by almost
$24$ standard errors. The only other flag is a mild one, branch~1's DFBETAS for the intercept
($-0.87$); branch~1 is the point at the far left, and has the largest residual among the
others.

\emph{The fit without branch~8.} The other seven give $\bar x = 4$, $\bar y = 7$,
$S_{xx} = 28$, $S_{xy} = 35$, so
\[
\hat y = 2 + 1.25x ,
\]
with $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 2.25$ on $5$ degrees of freedom (which is $5S^2_{(8)}$, as it should be)
and $R^2 = 0.951$. The slope is five times as large. This agrees with the deletion
formula: in simple regression, deleting observation $i$ changes the slope by
$(x_i - \bar x)e_i/\bigl(S_{xx}(1 - h_{ii})\bigr)$ (Problem~\ref{prob:slope-change}), here
\[
\frac{14 \times (-1.75)}{252 \times 7/72} = \frac{-24.5}{24.5} = -1 ,
\]
and indeed $0.25 - 1.25 = -1$.

The two lines are drawn in Figure~\ref{fig:shops}. The seven ordinary branches lie close to
a line of slope $1.25$; the warehouse store, far to the right, sells much less per square
metre, and on its own it flattens the fitted line until it fits none of the branches well.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.32cm,y=0.2cm]
\draw[-Stealth] (0,0) -- (22,0) node[right] {\small $x$};
\draw[-Stealth] (0,0) -- (0,29) node[above] {\small $y$};
\foreach \t in {5,10,15,20} \draw[-] (\t,0.3) -- (\t,-0.3) node[below] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\foreach \t in {5,10,15,20,25} \draw[-] (0.15,\t) -- (-0.15,\t) node[left] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\draw[-,thick,red!70!black] (0,5.75) -- (21,11.0) node[right,font=\small] {$\hat y = 5.75 + 0.25x$};
\draw[-,thick,dashed,blue] (0,2) -- (21,28.25) node[right,font=\small] {$\hat y = 2 + 1.25x$};
\foreach \p in {(1,3),(2,5),(3,5),(4,8),(5,8),(6,9),(7,11)} \fill[blue] \p circle (2.4pt);
\fill[red!70!black] (20,9) circle (3pt) node[below right,font=\small] {branch 8};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The branch data of Example~\ref{exa:shops}. Solid: the fit to all eight branches.
Dashed: the fit without branch~8.}
\label{fig:shops}
\end{figure}

\subsection{What to do about influential observations}
Influence diagnostics locate the points that drive the fit; they do not say what to do with
them. The first step is always to check the data: a point that is both influential and wrong
should be corrected or removed. Beyond that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item A sound influential point may show that the model is inadequate. In
Example~\ref{exa:shops}, the warehouse store may genuinely behave differently, and the
right model for the chain may need a regressor for the type of store, or a curve that
flattens at large areas. One store at $x = 20$ cannot tell these apart.
\item If the point is sound but different in kind, the honest course may be to restrict the
model to the range where it is supported, here branches of up to $700$\,m$^2$, and say so.
\item Never delete a point simply because it is influential. Report the fit with and without
it. If the conclusions agree, the point does not matter; if they differ, as here, the
data cannot settle the question and more observations are needed in the gap.
\end{enumerate}
A design lesson follows too. A single point far from the others is a weakness in the data, and
when the $x$ values can be chosen they should be spread so that no one observation carries
the estimate. Methods of \emph{robust regression}, which limit the weight any one point can
have, exist for data in which some influential points are unavoidable.

\begin{remark}[Masking]
All the measures of this section delete one observation at a time. If two bad points sit
together, deleting either leaves the other to hold the fit in place, and neither looks
influential: each \emph{masks} the other (Problem~\ref{prob:masking}). Plots of the data, and
of the leverages, remain essential, and groups of suspect points can be deleted together.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:crop-infl}
For the crop data of Example~\ref{exa:crop}, using the residuals and leverages of
Example~\ref{exa:crop-scaled}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item compute Cook's distance, DFFITS and COVRATIO for each plot;
\item which plots have high leverage? Which are influential? Explain the difference for plot~6.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) Here $p = 3$, $n = 6$, $n - p = 3$, and from Example~\ref{exa:crop-scaled} we have $r_i$, $t_i$,
$S^2_{(i)}$ and $h_{ii}$. For plot~2, exactly, $r_2^2 = 189/104$ and $h_{22}/(1 - h_{22}) = 37/26$, so
\[
D_2 = \frac{189}{3 \times 104}\cdot\frac{37}{26} = \frac{63}{104}\cdot\frac{37}{26} = \frac{2331}{2704} = 0.862 ,
\]
\[
\mathrm{DFFITS}_2 = 1.753\sqrt{37/26} = 1.753 \times 1.193 = 2.091 ,
\]
\[
\mathrm{COVRATIO}_2 = \left(\frac{0.788}{1.333}\right)^3\frac{63}{26} = 0.2068 \times 2.423 = 0.501 .
\]
In full:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrr}
Plot & $h_{ii}$ & $D_i$ & DFFITS & COVRATIO \\
\hline
1 & 0.421 & 0.313 & $-1.050$ & 1.070 \\
2 & 0.587 & 0.862 & $2.091$ & 0.501 \\
3 & 0.397 & 0.273 & $0.965$ & 1.123 \\
4 & 0.230 & 0.097 & $-0.536$ & 1.350 \\
5 & 0.587 & 0 & 0 & 8.178 \\
6 & 0.778 & 0 & 0 & 15.19 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For plots~5 and~6, $e_i = 0$, so $S^2_{(i)} = 4/2 = 2$ and
$\mathrm{COVRATIO}_6 = (2/1.333)^3/(2/9) = 3.375 \times 4.5 = 15.19$.

(b) The leverage cut-off is $2p/n = 1$, which no point can exceed: with $p = 3$ and only
$n = 6$, every point carries a large share of the fit, and the rule is useless. Relative to
the average $p/n = 0.5$, plots~2, 5 and~6 stand out. No $D_i$ exceeds $1$, but plot~2 comes
close, and $\mathrm{DFFITS}_2 = 2.09$ exceeds $2\sqrt{3/6} = 1.41$. Plot~2 is the most
influential observation.

Plot~6 has the highest leverage of all but no influence on the estimates: the plane passes
exactly through it, so deleting it changes nothing ($D_6 = 0$). Its COVRATIO of $15.2$ shows
what it does do: without it the generalised variance of the estimates would be fifteen times
larger. It is a high-leverage point that lies on the trend, panel~2 of
Figure~\ref{fig:influence-types}, and it is the most valuable observation in the data.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:slope-change}
In simple linear regression, show that deleting observation $i$ changes the estimates by
\begin{align*}
\hat\beta_1 - \hat\beta_{1(i)} &= \frac{(x_i - \bar x)\,e_i}{S_{xx}(1 - h_{ii})} , \\
\hat\beta_0 - \hat\beta_{0(i)} &= \frac{e_i}{1 - h_{ii}}\left(\frac{1}{n} - \frac{\bar x(x_i - \bar x)}{S_{xx}}\right) .
\end{align*}
Deduce that an observation at $x_i = \bar x$ cannot change the slope, however large its
residual.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:deletion}(ii), $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{(i)} = \mathbf{C}\mathbf{x}_i e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$.
From Example~\ref{exa:slr-matrix}, with $\sum x_j^2 = S_{xx} + n\bar x^2$,
\[
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{x}_i = \frac{1}{nS_{xx}}\begin{bmatrix} \sum x_j^2 - n\bar x x_i \\ -n\bar x + n x_i \end{bmatrix}
= \begin{bmatrix} \dfrac{1}{n} - \dfrac{\bar x(x_i - \bar x)}{S_{xx}} \\[2mm] \dfrac{x_i - \bar x}{S_{xx}} \end{bmatrix} ,
\]
since $\sum x_j^2 - n\bar x x_i = S_{xx} - n\bar x(x_i - \bar x)$. Multiply by $e_i/(1 - h_{ii})$.
At $x_i = \bar x$ the slope change is $0$; the point moves the line up or down, parallel to
itself, by $e_i/(n(1 - h_{ii})) = e_i/(n - 1)$, since $h_{ii} = 1/n$ there. This is panel~1 of
Figure~\ref{fig:influence-types}. The further $x_i$ is from $\bar x$, the larger both factors
$x_i - \bar x$ and $1/(1 - h_{ii})$ become.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:dffits-cook}
Show that $\mathrm{DFFITS}_i^2 = p\,D_i\,MS_{\mathrm{Res}}/S^2_{(i)}$. Then, for the mistyped
yield data of Example~\ref{exa:yield-outlier}, compute $D_4$ and $\mathrm{DFFITS}_4$
and explain why the two measures disagree about observation~4.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
By Theorems~\ref{thm:cook} and~\ref{thm:dffits},
\[
\frac{\mathrm{DFFITS}_i^2}{p\,D_i} = \frac{t_i^2\,h_{ii}/(1 - h_{ii})}{r_i^2\,h_{ii}/(1 - h_{ii})}
= \frac{t_i^2}{r_i^2} = \frac{e_i^2/\bigl(S^2_{(i)}(1 - h_{ii})\bigr)}{e_i^2/\bigl(MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(1 - h_{ii})\bigr)} = \frac{MS_{\mathrm{Res}}}{S^2_{(i)}} .
\]
For observation~4 of Example~\ref{exa:yield-outlier}, $r_4 = 1.989$, $t_4 = 16.39$ and
$h_{44} = 0.181$, so $h_{44}/(1 - h_{44}) = 0.221$, and with $p = 2$,
\[
D_4 = \frac{1.989^2}{2} \times 0.221 = 0.437, \qquad
\mathrm{DFFITS}_4 = 16.39 \times \sqrt{0.221} = 7.70 .
\]
Cook's distance is well below $1$; DFFITS is nearly seven times its cut-off
$2\sqrt{2/6} = 1.15$. They disagree because $D_i$ is built on the studentized residual, which
here cannot exceed $\sqrt{n - p} = 2$, while DFFITS is built on R-student, which has no such
bound. The ratio $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}/S^2_{(4)} = 25/0.368 = 68$ is exactly the factor by which the
bad value has inflated the residual mean square. Observation~4 is near the middle of the
$x$-range, so it has little leverage and moves the slope only moderately, which is what $D_4$
reports; but it is a gross outlier, which is what DFFITS reports.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:masking}
Suppose a ninth branch, identical to branch~8 ($x = 20$, $y = 9$), is added to the data of
Example~\ref{exa:shops}. The refitted line is $\hat y = 5.94 + 0.199x$, and for each of the two
large stores $h_{ii} = 0.474$, $r_i = -0.565$, $D_i = 0.144$, $\mathrm{DFFITS}_i = -0.508$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Which cut-offs do the two stores exceed?
\item Explain why deleting one of them changes so little, and what would show the
problem.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) With $n = 9$, $p = 2$: $2p/n = 0.444$, so both have (just) high leverage; but
$D_i = 0.144 < 1$ and $|\mathrm{DFFITS}_i| = 0.508 < 2\sqrt{2/9} = 0.943$. On the deletion
measures neither is influential.

(b) Deleting one large store leaves the other at the same point, and it holds the line almost
exactly where it was: the fit to the remaining eight is the fit of Example~\ref{exa:shops},
$\hat y = 5.75 + 0.25x$, little different from $5.94 + 0.199x$. Each store masks the other.
Deleting both together gives $\hat y = 2 + 1.25x$, the dramatic change of the example. The
problem is plain in a scatter plot, and in the leverages: two points share a leverage of
almost $0.95$ out of a total of $p = 2$. Deleting the pair, and computing the analogue of
Cook's distance for the pair, would reveal it.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:leverage-design}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item In simple linear regression with an intercept, show that $\sum_i (h_{ii} - 1/n) = 1$,
and hence that at most one observation can have $h_{ii} - 1/n \ge 1/2$.
\item Five values of $x$ are to be chosen in $[0, 4]$. Compare the leverages of the designs
$0, 1, 2, 3, 4$ and $0, 0, 0, 0, 4$. Which is safer against a single faulty observation, and why?
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) By Example~\ref{exa:slr-leverage}, $h_{ii} - 1/n = (x_i - \bar x)^2/S_{xx}$, and these add
to $S_{xx}/S_{xx} = 1$. They are all non-negative, so two of them cannot both be at least $1/2$
unless they are exactly $1/2$ each and the rest are zero.

(b) For $0, 1, 2, 3, 4$: $\bar x = 2$, $S_{xx} = 10$, and $h_{ii} = 0.2 + (x_i - 2)^2/10$, which is
$0.6, 0.3, 0.2, 0.3, 0.6$. For $0, 0, 0, 0, 4$: $\bar x = 0.8$, $S_{xx} = 4(0.64) + 3.2^2 = 12.8$, and
$h_{ii} = 0.2 + 0.64/12.8 = 0.25$ for the four points at $0$, but
$h_{55} = 0.2 + 10.24/12.8 = 1$ for the point at $4$. In the second design the single
observation at $x = 4$ alone determines the slope: the line must pass through it
(Problem~\ref{prob:hii-one}), its residual is always zero, and an error in it cannot be
detected at all. The first design spreads the leverage, so every observation is checked by the
others. (The second design does have a smaller variance for $\hat\beta_1$,
$\sigma^2/12.8$ against $\sigma^2/10$, but only by trusting one observation completely; it also
gives no way of seeing whether the relationship is a straight line.)
\end{solution}

\section{Transformation \& weighting to correct model inadequacies}
The residual analysis of Section~4 diagnoses two common failures: a variance that is not
constant, and a relationship that is not a straight line. This section gives the two
standard remedies. The first is to \emph{transform} the response, a regressor, or both, so
that on the new scale the assumptions hold. The second, for non-constant variance whose form
is known, is to keep the original scale and fit by \emph{weighted} least squares, giving each
observation a say in proportion to its precision.

\subsection{Variance-stabilizing transformations}
Non-constant variance usually arises because the variance of $y$ is a function of its mean.
Counts have variance equal to their mean; measurements subject to a percentage error have a
standard deviation proportional to the mean; proportions have variance
$\pi(1 - \pi)/n$. In each case the remedy is a function $g$ such that $g(y)$ has
(approximately) constant variance. The tool for finding it is the following approximation.

\begin{lem}[Delta method]\label{lem:delta}
Let $y$ have mean $\mu$ and small variance $\sigma^2(\mu)$, and let $g$ be differentiable at
$\mu$. Then, approximately,
\[
\operatorname{Var}\bigl(g(y)\bigr) \approx g'(\mu)^2\,\sigma^2(\mu) .
\]
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Derivation]
By Taylor's theorem, $g(y) = g(\mu) + g'(\mu)(y - \mu) + R$, where the remainder $R$ is of
order $(y - \mu)^2$. When $y$ stays close to $\mu$ the remainder is negligible, and
$g(y) \approx g(\mu) + g'(\mu)(y - \mu)$. The right side is a linear function of $y$, with
variance $g'(\mu)^2\operatorname{Var}(y)$.
\end{proof}

To make $\operatorname{Var}(g(y))$ constant we need $g'(\mu) \propto 1/\sigma(\mu)$, that is,
\[
g(\mu) = \int \frac{d\mu}{\sigma(\mu)} .
\]

\begin{thm}\label{thm:stabilize}
If the standard deviation of $y$ is proportional to a power of its mean, $\sigma(\mu) \propto \mu^a$,
then $g(y) = y^{1 - a}$ for $a \neq 1$, and $g(y) = \ln y$ for $a = 1$, has approximately constant
variance. If $y$ is a binomial proportion, with $\sigma^2(\pi) = \pi(1 - \pi)/n$, then
$g(y) = \arcsin\sqrt{y}$ has approximately constant variance $1/(4n)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
With $\sigma(\mu) = c\mu^a$, $\int \mu^{-a}\,d\mu = \mu^{1 - a}/(1 - a)$ for $a \neq 1$ and
$\ln\mu$ for $a = 1$; constant multiples do not matter. Check by Lemma~\ref{lem:delta}: for
$g(y) = y^{1-a}$, $g'(\mu)^2\sigma^2(\mu) = (1 - a)^2\mu^{-2a}c^2\mu^{2a} = (1 - a)^2c^2$, and for
$g = \ln$, $\mu^{-2}c^2\mu^2 = c^2$, both free of $\mu$.

For a proportion, let $g(\pi) = \arcsin\sqrt\pi$. By the chain rule
\[
g'(\pi) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - \pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\sqrt\pi} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(1 - \pi)}} ,
\]
so $g'(\pi)^2\sigma^2(\pi) = \dfrac{1}{4\pi(1 - \pi)}\cdot\dfrac{\pi(1 - \pi)}{n} = \dfrac{1}{4n}$.
\end{proof}

The common cases are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
Relation of $\sigma^2$ to $E(y)$ & Typical data & Transformation \\
\hline
$\sigma^2 \propto$ constant & & none \\
$\sigma^2 \propto E(y)$ & counts (Poisson) & $\sqrt{y}$ \\
$\sigma^2 \propto E(y)(1 - E(y))$ & proportions & $\arcsin\sqrt{y}$ \\
$\sigma^2 \propto [E(y)]^2$ & percentage errors & $\ln y$ \\
$\sigma^2 \propto [E(y)]^3$ & & $y^{-1/2}$ \\
$\sigma^2 \propto [E(y)]^4$ & & $y^{-1}$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The strength of the transformation increases down the table. When the response varies over
less than a factor of about two or three, the transformations hardly change anything; when
$y_{\max}/y_{\min}$ is large, they matter a great deal. The form of $\sigma^2(\mu)$ may be known
from the nature of the data, or it may be suggested by the funnel in a plot of residuals
against fitted values (Figure~\ref{fig:resid-patterns}).

\begin{remark}
A model fitted to $g(y)$ predicts $g(y)$. Converting back to the original scale needs care.
Because $g$ is monotone, an interval for $g(y)$ converts to an interval for $y$ by applying
$g^{-1}$ to its end points, and a prediction interval keeps its probability. But
$g^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{g(y)}\bigr)$ is not an estimate of the \emph{mean} of $y$: for
$g = \ln$, $E(\ln y) \neq \ln E(y)$. If $\ln y$ is normal with mean $m$ and variance $s^2$,
then $e^m$ is the \emph{median} of $y$, and its mean is $e^{m + s^2/2}$, somewhat larger.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Transformations to linearize the model}
A curved relationship can often be made straight. The models in Figure~\ref{fig:linearizable}
are non-linear in $x$, and some are non-linear in the parameters, but each becomes a straight
line after transforming $y$, $x$ or both.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\py/\col/\num/\name/\eqn/\todo/\curve in {
 0/0/blue/1/{Exponential}/{$y = \beta_0 e^{\beta_1 x}$}/{plot $\ln y$ against $x$}/{plot[domain=0.1:3.8,samples=40,smooth] (\x,{0.3*exp(0.55*\x)})},
 5.3/0/orange/2/{Power}/{$y = \beta_0 x^{\beta_1}$}/{plot $\ln y$ against $\ln x$}/{plot[domain=0.05:3.9,samples=40,smooth] (\x,{1.2*sqrt(\x)})},
 0/-4.8/green/3/{Reciprocal}/{$y = \beta_0 + \beta_1/x$}/{plot $y$ against $1/x$}/{plot[domain=0.4:3.9,samples=40,smooth] (\x,{2.6-0.7/\x})},
 5.3/-4.8/red/4/{Logarithmic}/{$y = \beta_0 + \beta_1\ln x$}/{plot $y$ against $\ln x$}/{plot[domain=0.3:3.9,samples=40,smooth] (\x,{1.0+0.75*ln(\x)})}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,\py)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-0.4,-1.35) rectangle (4.6,3.2);
  \node[circle,fill=\col!60!black,text=white,inner sep=1.5pt,font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (-0.05,2.85) {\num};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.2,2.85) {\name};
  \node[font=\scriptsize,text=\col!50!black] at (2.1,-0.45) {\eqn};
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (4.2,0);
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (0,2.5);
  \draw[-,thick,\col!60!black] \curve;
  \node[font=\scriptsize,text=\col!50!black] at (2.1,-0.95) {\todo};
 \end{scope}
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Four curves that become straight lines after a transformation. Each panel shows the
model, the shape of $y$ against $x$ for a typical choice of parameters, and the plot that is
linear.}
\label{fig:linearizable}
\end{figure}

For the exponential model, $\ln y = \ln\beta_0 + \beta_1 x$, a straight line in $x$ with
intercept $\beta_0' = \ln\beta_0$. For the power model, $\ln y = \ln\beta_0 + \beta_1\ln x$.
The reciprocal and logarithmic models are already linear in the parameters, and need only the
regressor $x$ to be replaced by $x' = 1/x$ or $x' = \ln x$. A reciprocal model has a horizontal
asymptote, $y \to \beta_0$ as $x \to \infty$, and suits a response that rises and levels off.

The error term needs thought. Fitting $\ln y = \beta_0' + \beta_1 x + \varepsilon'$ by least squares
assumes that the errors are additive, constant-variance and normal \emph{on the log scale}, so
that the original model is
\[
y = \beta_0 e^{\beta_1 x}\varepsilon, \qquad \varepsilon = e^{\varepsilon'} ,
\]
with a \emph{multiplicative} error. That is often realistic: a growth process with errors of a
roughly constant percentage. If instead the errors are additive on the original scale,
$y = \beta_0 e^{\beta_1 x} + \varepsilon$, then taking logs destroys the constant variance, and the
model should be fitted by non-linear least squares, a subject of a later section. The residual
plots of the transformed fit show which description fits the data.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:catalyst-recip}
In Example~\ref{exa:catalyst-lof} the straight line failed the lack-of-fit test, the yield
rising with catalyst concentration and then levelling off. Fit $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1(1/x) + \varepsilon$
and test it for lack of fit.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Let $z = 1/x$, which takes the values $1$, $0.5$, $0.3333$, $0.25$, $0.2$, each twice. Then
$\bar z = 2(2.2833)/10 = 0.45667$, and
\[
S_{zz} = \sum(z_i - \bar z)^2 = 0.84178, \qquad
S_{zy} = \sum(z_i - \bar z)y_i = -6.7800 ,
\]
so $\hat\beta_1 = -6.7800/0.84178 = -8.0544$ and $\hat\beta_0 = 8.7 + 8.0544 \times 0.45667 = 12.378$:
\[
\hat y = 12.378 - \frac{8.054}{x} .
\]
The fitted values at $x = 1, \dots, 5$ are $4.32$, $8.35$, $9.69$, $10.36$, $10.77$, against group means
$4.5$, $7.5$, $10.5$, $10.5$, $10.5$. Now $SS_R = \hat\beta_1 S_{zy} = 54.61$, so
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 60.1 - 54.61 = 5.49$, and with $SS_{\mathrm{PE}} = 2.5$ as before (it does not
depend on the model), $SS_{\mathrm{LOF}} = 2.99$ on $3$ degrees of freedom:
\[
