removes the near-singularity. Ridge regression is also least squares with a penalty: $\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R$
minimises $\|\mathbf{y}^* - \mathbf{Wb}\|^2 + k\|\mathbf{b}\|^2$ (Problem~\ref{prob:ridge-penalty}), so it
shrinks the coefficients towards zero, counteracting the tendency of least squares to make them too
long (Theorem~\ref{thm:length}).

The properties of the ridge estimator are clearest in the coordinates of the eigenvectors. Write
$\mathbf{R} = \mathbf{P}\boldsymbol\Lambda\mathbf{P}^\top$ and $\boldsymbol\alpha = \mathbf{P}^\top\mathbf{b}$, the
coefficients along the eigenvector directions, with estimates $\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha} = \mathbf{P}^\top\hat{\mathbf{b}}$.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:ridge}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The components of $\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}$ are uncorrelated, with $E(\hat\alpha_j) = \alpha_j$ and
$\operatorname{Var}(\hat\alpha_j) = \sigma_*^2/\lambda_j$.
\item The ridge estimator shrinks each component: $\hat\alpha_{R,j} = \dfrac{\lambda_j}{\lambda_j + k}\hat\alpha_j$.
\item Its total mean squared error is
\[
\mathrm{MSE}(k) = E\|\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R - \mathbf{b}\|^2 = \sum_j\frac{\sigma_*^2\lambda_j + k^2\alpha_j^2}{(\lambda_j + k)^2} .
\]
\item (Hoerl and Kennard) $\mathrm{MSE}(k)$ is strictly decreasing for $0 \le k < \sigma_*^2/\max_j\alpha_j^2$.
Hence there is always a $k > 0$ for which the ridge estimator has smaller total mean squared error than
least squares.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(i) $\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}) = \mathbf{P}^\top(\sigma_*^2\mathbf{R}^{-1})\mathbf{P} = \sigma_*^2\mathbf{P}^\top\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol\Lambda^{-1}\mathbf{P}^\top\mathbf{P} = \sigma_*^2\boldsymbol\Lambda^{-1}$,
a diagonal matrix; and $E(\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}) = \mathbf{P}^\top\mathbf{b} = \boldsymbol\alpha$.

(ii) By the normal equations $\mathbf{r}_y = \mathbf{R}\hat{\mathbf{b}}$, so
$\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R = (\mathbf{R} + k\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{R}\hat{\mathbf{b}} = \mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol\Lambda + k\mathbf{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol\Lambda\mathbf{P}^\top\hat{\mathbf{b}}$,
using $\mathbf{R} + k\mathbf{I} = \mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol\Lambda + k\mathbf{I})\mathbf{P}^\top$. Multiply on the left by $\mathbf{P}^\top$.

(iii) $\mathbf{P}$ is orthogonal, so $\|\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R - \mathbf{b}\|^2 = \|\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}_R - \boldsymbol\alpha\|^2$, and each
component's mean squared error is its variance plus its squared bias. By (i) and (ii), with
$c_j = \lambda_j/(\lambda_j + k)$, the variance is $c_j^2\sigma_*^2/\lambda_j = \sigma_*^2\lambda_j/(\lambda_j + k)^2$ and the
bias is $(c_j - 1)\alpha_j = -k\alpha_j/(\lambda_j + k)$.

(iv) Differentiate one term of (iii) with respect to $k$:
\begin{align*}
\frac{d}{dk}\,\frac{\sigma_*^2\lambda_j + k^2\alpha_j^2}{(\lambda_j + k)^2}
&= \frac{2k\alpha_j^2(\lambda_j + k) - 2(\sigma_*^2\lambda_j + k^2\alpha_j^2)}{(\lambda_j + k)^3} \\
&= \frac{2\lambda_j(k\alpha_j^2 - \sigma_*^2)}{(\lambda_j + k)^3} .
\end{align*}
If $k < \sigma_*^2/\alpha_j^2$ for every $j$, each derivative is negative, so $\mathrm{MSE}(k)$ is strictly decreasing
on that interval, which starts at $k = 0$.
\end{proof}

The theorem shows where the gain comes from. Least squares estimates the component along a small
eigenvalue with variance $\sigma_*^2/\lambda_j$, which is huge. Ridge shrinks that component by the factor
$\lambda_j/(\lambda_j + k)$, which is far below $1$ when $\lambda_j$ is small compared with $k$, while leaving the
well-estimated components (large $\lambda_j$) almost untouched. A little bias buys a large cut in variance.
The catch is that the best $k$ depends on the unknown $\boldsymbol\alpha$ and $\sigma_*^2$.

\subhead{Choosing $k$}
Two common methods are:
\begin{itemize}
\item the \textbf{ridge trace}: plot the coefficients $\hat b_{R,j}$ against $k$, and choose the smallest $k$
at which they have stabilised, with sensible signs and sizes, and with the residual mean square not much
larger than at $k = 0$;
\item the formula of Hoerl, Kennard and Baldwin (1975),
\[
\hat k = \frac{q\,\hat\sigma_*^2}{\hat{\mathbf{b}}^\top\hat{\mathbf{b}}} ,
\]
where $q$ is the number of regressors. It is the $k$ that minimises the mean squared error of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge}(iii) when all the $\alpha_j$ are equal (Problem~\ref{prob:hkb}).
\end{itemize}
Either way $k$ is chosen from the data, so the usual tests and intervals do not apply to the ridge
estimates; ridge regression is a tool for estimation and prediction, not for testing.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:appliances-ridge}
Apply ridge regression to the appliance data of Example~\ref{exa:appliances}.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
In correlation form, $r_{12} = 0.98563$, $r_{1y} = 0.95182$ and $r_{2y} = 0.93455$, and the
least-squares standardized coefficients are $\hat b_1 = 1.0760$ and $\hat b_2 = -0.1260$. (Check:
$\hat\beta_1 = 1.0760\sqrt{163.5/65.5} = 1.70$.) For a given $k$,
\[
\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R = \frac{1}{(1 + k)^2 - r_{12}^2}\begin{bmatrix} (1 + k)r_{1y} - r_{12}r_{2y} \\ (1 + k)r_{2y} - r_{12}r_{1y} \end{bmatrix} .
\]
For instance at $k = 0.05$, $(1.05)^2 - 0.97147 = 0.13103$, and
\[
\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R = \frac{1}{0.13103}\begin{bmatrix} 0.99941 - 0.92112 \\ 0.98128 - 0.93815 \end{bmatrix}
= \begin{bmatrix} 0.5975 \\ 0.3292 \end{bmatrix} .
\]
Converting each to the original scale with $\hat\beta_j = \hat b_j\sqrt{SS_T/S_{jj}}$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|cc|cc}
$k$ & $\hat b_{R,1}$ & $\hat b_{R,2}$ & $\hat\beta_{R,1}$ & $\hat\beta_{R,2}$ \\
\hline
0 & 1.076 & $-0.126$ & 1.700 & $-0.200$ \\
0.005 & 0.920 & 0.028 & 1.453 & 0.044 \\
0.01 & 0.827 & 0.118 & 1.307 & 0.188 \\
0.02 & 0.722 & 0.219 & 1.140 & 0.348 \\
0.05 & 0.598 & 0.329 & 0.944 & 0.523 \\
0.1 & 0.528 & 0.377 & 0.834 & 0.598 \\
0.2 & 0.472 & 0.391 & 0.746 & 0.621 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The ridge trace, Figure~\ref{fig:ridge-trace}, shows the two coefficients moving rapidly towards each
other for small $k$, the sign of $\hat b_2$ turning positive by $k = 0.005$, and both settling down by
about $k = 0.05$. For the Hoerl--Kennard--Baldwin value, $\hat\sigma_*^2 = MS_{\mathrm{Res}}/SS_T = 3.06/163.5 = 0.01872$
and $\hat{\mathbf{b}}^\top\hat{\mathbf{b}} = 1.0760^2 + 0.1260^2 = 1.1736$, so
\[
\hat k = \frac{2 \times 0.01872}{1.1736} = 0.032 ,
\]
giving $\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R = (0.654,\ 0.281)$ and, on the original scale, with
$\hat\beta_{R,0} = \bar y - \hat\beta_{R,1}\bar x_1 - \hat\beta_{R,2}\bar x_2$,
\[
\hat y_R = 4.00 + 1.03x_1 + 0.45x_2 .
\]
Both coefficients are now positive and of sensible size: about one unit of use per appliance, and a
further half unit for each one used daily. The fit has hardly suffered: $R^2$ falls from $0.906$ to
$0.901$.

The theory explains the result. The eigenvalues are $1.9856$ and $0.0144$, and the least-squares components
along the eigenvectors are $\hat\alpha_1 = (1.0760 - 0.1260)/\sqrt2 = 0.672$ and
$\hat\alpha_2 = (1.0760 + 0.1260)/\sqrt2 = 0.850$, the latter with standard error
$\sqrt{0.01872/0.0144} = 1.14$, larger than the estimate itself. At $k = 0.032$ ridge multiplies $\hat\alpha_1$ by
$1.9856/2.0176 = 0.98$, leaving it almost unchanged, and $\hat\alpha_2$ by $0.0144/0.0464 = 0.31$, removing most
of the noise in the poorly determined direction. If the estimates were the true values,
Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge}(iii) would give $\mathrm{MSE}(0) = 1.31$ and $\mathrm{MSE}(0.032) = 0.48$, a reduction by more than
half.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=20cm,y=3.2cm]
\draw[-Stealth] (0,0) -- (0.32,0) node[right] {\small $k$};
\draw[-Stealth] (0,-0.25) -- (0,1.25) node[above] {\small $\hat b_{R,j}$};
\foreach \t in {0.1,0.2,0.3} \draw[-] (\t,0.02) -- (\t,-0.02) node[below] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\foreach \t in {0.5,1} \draw[-] (0.003,\t) -- (-0.003,\t) node[left] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\draw[-] (0.003,0) -- (-0.003,0) node[left] {\scriptsize $0$};
\draw[-,dashed,gray] (0.032,-0.25) -- (0.032,1.2);
\node[right,font=\scriptsize,gray!70!black] at (0.032,1.12) {$k = 0.032$};
\draw[-,thick,blue] plot[smooth] coordinates {(0.0000,1.076) (0.0025,0.986) (0.0050,0.920) (0.0075,0.868) (0.0100,0.827) (0.0150,0.765) (0.0200,0.722) (0.0300,0.663) (0.0400,0.624) (0.0500,0.598) (0.0600,0.577) (0.0800,0.548) (0.1000,0.528) (0.1200,0.512) (0.1500,0.494) (0.2000,0.472) (0.2500,0.455) (0.3000,0.440)};
\draw[-,thick,red!70!black] plot[smooth] coordinates {(0.0000,-0.126) (0.0025,-0.038) (0.0050,0.028) (0.0075,0.078) (0.0100,0.118) (0.0150,0.177) (0.0200,0.219) (0.0300,0.273) (0.0400,0.307) (0.0500,0.329) (0.0600,0.345) (0.0800,0.365) (0.1000,0.377) (0.1200,0.384) (0.1500,0.389) (0.2000,0.391) (0.2500,0.389) (0.3000,0.385)};
\node[blue,right,font=\small] at (0.3,0.47) {$\hat b_{R,1}$};
\node[red!70!black,right,font=\small] at (0.3,0.36) {$\hat b_{R,2}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The ridge trace for the appliance data: the standardized ridge coefficients against $k$.}
\label{fig:ridge-trace}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[Principal components]
\emph{Principal-components regression} works directly with the components $\alpha_j$. Since the components along small
eigenvalues are almost impossible to estimate, set them to zero and keep only the others. In
Example~\ref{exa:appliances} this would keep only $\hat\alpha_1 = 0.672$, giving
\[
\hat{\mathbf{b}} = 0.672 \times \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}(1, 1) = (0.475,\ 0.475) ,
\]
equal standardized coefficients for the two
regressors, which is the same as regressing on their (standardized) sum. Ridge regression shrinks the
poorly determined components smoothly; principal-components regression removes them altogether.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:two-reg-corr}
For a model with two regressors, show that
\[
\mathbf{R}^{-1} = \frac{1}{1 - r_{12}^2}\begin{bmatrix} 1 & -r_{12} \\ -r_{12} & 1 \end{bmatrix} ,
\]
and hence that the correlation between $\hat\beta_1$ and $\hat\beta_2$ is $-r_{12}$. Explain why, in
Example~\ref{exa:appliances}, an estimate of $\beta_1$ that is too large tends to go with an estimate of
$\beta_2$ that is too small.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The inverse of $\begin{bmatrix} 1 & r \\ r & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ is its adjugate divided by its determinant
$1 - r^2$. Then $\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\mathbf{b}}) = \sigma_*^2\mathbf{R}^{-1}$ gives
$\operatorname{Corr}(\hat b_1, \hat b_2) = (-r\sigma_*^2/(1 - r^2))\big/(\sigma_*^2/(1 - r^2)) = -r$, and since
$\hat\beta_j$ is a positive multiple of $\hat b_j$, the same holds for $\hat\beta_1$, $\hat\beta_2$. In the
example the correlation is $-0.986$: the data determine $\beta_1 + \beta_2$ (the effect of moving along the
line $x_1 \approx x_2$) well, so an error upwards in one estimate must be offset by an error downwards in the
other. That is how $\hat\beta_1 = 1.7$ and $\hat\beta_2 = -0.2$ arise from true values that are probably both
positive: their sum, $1.5$, is close to the simple-regression slopes $1.50$ and $1.48$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:centring}
For the quadratic model $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2x^2 + \varepsilon$ with $x = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item find the correlation between $x$ and $x^2$, and the VIFs;
\item repeat with $x$ replaced by $x - 3$, and explain why centring helps;
\item show that the two models give the same fitted values.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $x$: $1, \dots, 5$, $\bar x = 3$, $S_{xx} = 10$. $x^2$: $1, 4, 9, 16, 25$, mean $11$, with deviations
$-10, -7, -2, 5, 14$, so $S_{x^2x^2} = 100 + 49 + 4 + 25 + 196 = 374$, and
$S_{x,x^2} = -2(-10) - 1(-7) + 0 + 1(5) + 2(14) = 60$. So
$r = 60/\sqrt{10 \times 374} = 0.981$, and both VIFs are $1/(1 - 0.9626) = 26.7$.

(b) With $u = x - 3$: $u = -2, -1, 0, 1, 2$ and $u^2 = 4, 1, 0, 1, 4$. Then
$S_{u,u^2} = \sum u_i \cdot u_i^2 = \sum u_i^3 = -8 - 1 + 0 + 1 + 8 = 0$, so $r = 0$ and both VIFs are $1$.
Over $1 \le x \le 5$, $x^2$ is an increasing function of $x$ and nearly linear in it, hence the high
correlation. After centring, $u^2$ is symmetric about $u = 0$ while $u$ is antisymmetric, and for points
placed symmetrically the two are exactly uncorrelated.

(c) $\beta_0 + \beta_1u + \beta_2u^2 = (\beta_0 - 3\beta_1 + 9\beta_2) + (\beta_1 - 6\beta_2)x + \beta_2x^2$, so the two
models span the same column space (the functions $1, x, x^2$), and least squares gives the same projection
$\hat{\mathbf{y}}$. Only the meaning, and the precision, of the individual coefficients change: in the
centred model $\beta_1$ is the slope at $x = 3$, the middle of the data.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:ridge-penalty}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that $\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R$ minimises $Q(\mathbf{b}) = \|\mathbf{y}^* - \mathbf{Wb}\|^2 + k\|\mathbf{b}\|^2$.
\item Show that $\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R$ is the ordinary least-squares estimate for the data $\mathbf{y}^*$, $\mathbf{W}$
augmented with $k$ extra observations: response $0$ and regressor rows $\sqrt{k}\,\mathbf{I}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $Q(\mathbf{b}) = \mathbf{y}^{*\top}\mathbf{y}^* - 2\mathbf{b}^\top\mathbf{W}^\top\mathbf{y}^* + \mathbf{b}^\top(\mathbf{W}^\top\mathbf{W} + k\mathbf{I})\mathbf{b}$.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:diff} the gradient is $-2\mathbf{r}_y + 2(\mathbf{R} + k\mathbf{I})\mathbf{b}$, zero at $\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R$.
The matrix $\mathbf{R} + k\mathbf{I}$ is positive definite, so, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:ls-matrix},
$Q(\mathbf{b}) = Q(\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R) + (\mathbf{b} - \hat{\mathbf{b}}_R)^\top(\mathbf{R} + k\mathbf{I})(\mathbf{b} - \hat{\mathbf{b}}_R)$, which is
minimised only at $\hat{\mathbf{b}}_R$.

(b) The augmented regressor matrix and response are
\[
\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{W} \\ \sqrt{k}\,\mathbf{I} \end{bmatrix} , \qquad \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{y}^* \\ \mathbf{0} \end{bmatrix} ,
\]
and the least-squares criterion is $\|\mathbf{y}^* - \mathbf{Wb}\|^2 + \|\mathbf{0} - \sqrt{k}\,\mathbf{b}\|^2 = Q(\mathbf{b})$. Equivalently its normal equations are
$(\mathbf{W}^\top\mathbf{W} + k\mathbf{I})\mathbf{b} = \mathbf{W}^\top\mathbf{y}^*$. The pseudo-observations say ``each coefficient is
probably near zero'', and they pull the estimates towards zero most strongly in the directions the real
data leave undetermined.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:vif-from-r2}
In a regression with four regressors, regressing each on the other three gave
$R_1^2 = 0.30$, $R_2^2 = 0.95$, $R_3^2 = 0.96$ and $R_4^2 = 0.50$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Compute the VIFs and say which coefficients are affected.
\item By what factor is the standard error of $\hat\beta_3$ larger than it would be if $x_3$ were
uncorrelated with the others?
\item Suggest what to examine next.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $\mathrm{VIF}_j = 1/(1 - R_j^2)$: $1.43$, $20$, $25$, $2$. The coefficients of $x_2$ and $x_3$ are badly
affected; those of $x_1$ and $x_4$ are not.

(b) The standard error is multiplied by $\sqrt{\mathrm{VIF}_3} = 5$.

(c) Since only $x_2$ and $x_3$ have large VIFs, the likeliest explanation is a single near dependence
between them, possibly involving the others weakly. The correlation $r_{23}$ and the eigenvalues and
eigenvectors of $\mathbf{R}$ would confirm this: one small eigenvalue, with eigenvector concentrated on $x_2$ and
$x_3$. Then ask whether the two measure the same thing (combine them), whether one can be dropped, or whether
the aim is prediction, in which case the collinearity may not matter.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:length}
For the appliance data, $\hat\sigma_*^2 = 0.01872$. Use Theorem~\ref{thm:length} to estimate how much longer, in
squared length, the least-squares vector $\hat{\mathbf{b}}$ is expected to be than the true $\mathbf{b}$, and compare
with an orthogonal design with the same $n$ and $\sigma_*^2$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
$\sum 1/\lambda_j = 1/1.9856 + 1/0.0144 = 0.504 + 69.6 = 70.1$, so
$E\|\hat{\mathbf{b}}\|^2 - \|\mathbf{b}\|^2 = 0.01872 \times 70.1 = 1.31$. The observed $\|\hat{\mathbf{b}}\|^2 = 1.174$ is smaller than
this expected excess alone, so much of its length may well be noise. With uncorrelated regressors $\mathbf{R} = \mathbf{I}$, both
eigenvalues are $1$, and the excess would be $0.01872 \times 2 = 0.037$: thirty-five times smaller, the
average of the two VIFs.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:hkb}
Suppose that in Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge}(iii) all the components are equal in size,
$\alpha_j^2 = a^2$ for each of the $q$ components. Show that $\mathrm{MSE}(k)$ is minimised at $k = \sigma_*^2/a^2$, and deduce the
Hoerl--Kennard--Baldwin formula.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
By the derivative in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge}(iv), each term has derivative
$2\lambda_j(ka^2 - \sigma_*^2)/(\lambda_j + k)^3$, which is negative for $k < \sigma_*^2/a^2$ and positive for
$k > \sigma_*^2/a^2$, whatever $\lambda_j$. So every term, and hence the sum, is minimised at
$k = \sigma_*^2/a^2$. Since $\|\mathbf{b}\|^2 = \|\boldsymbol\alpha\|^2 = qa^2$, this is
$k = q\sigma_*^2/\|\mathbf{b}\|^2$. Replacing $\sigma_*^2$ and $\mathbf{b}$ by their least-squares estimates gives
$\hat k = q\hat\sigma_*^2/\hat{\mathbf{b}}^\top\hat{\mathbf{b}}$.
\end{solution}

\section{Dummy variables}
The regressors so far have been quantitative: amounts of fertiliser, temperatures, distances. Many
variables that affect a response are \emph{qualitative}: the variety of a crop, the type of school, the
province, whether a household is rural or urban. They have no natural numerical scale, but they can
still enter a regression through \textbf{indicator variables}, also called \textbf{dummy variables}, which
take the values $0$ and $1$ to record which category an observation belongs to. With them, one
regression model can compare groups, test whether a relationship is the same in each, and fit
relationships whose shape changes at a known point.

\subsection{A qualitative regressor with two levels}
Suppose the response $y$ depends on a quantitative regressor $x$ and on which of two groups, $A$ or
$B$, an observation comes from. Define
\[
z = \begin{cases} 0 & \text{for an observation from group } A, \\ 1 & \text{for an observation from group } B. \end{cases}
\]
Three models are natural, and Figure~\ref{fig:dummy-models} shows them.

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Coincident lines}, $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \varepsilon$: the group makes no difference.
\item \emph{Parallel lines}, $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2z + \varepsilon$. For group $A$ ($z = 0$) the mean is
$\beta_0 + \beta_1x$; for group $B$ ($z = 1$) it is $(\beta_0 + \beta_2) + \beta_1x$. The lines have the same slope,
and $\beta_2$ is the vertical distance between them: the difference between the groups at any fixed $x$.
\item \emph{Separate lines}, $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2z + \beta_3xz + \varepsilon$. For group $A$ the mean is
$\beta_0 + \beta_1x$; for group $B$ it is $(\beta_0 + \beta_2) + (\beta_1 + \beta_3)x$. Now $\beta_3$ is the difference in
slopes. The product $xz$ is an \textbf{interaction} term: the effect of $x$ depends on the group, and the
difference between the groups depends on $x$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\col/\num/\name/\eqn/\lineA/\lineB in {
 0/blue/1/{Coincident}/{$\beta_2 = \beta_3 = 0$}/{(0.2,0.5) -- (4.0,2.4)}/{(0.2,0.5) -- (4.0,2.4)},
 4.9/green/2/{Parallel}/{$\beta_3 = 0$}/{(0.2,0.3) -- (4.0,1.9)}/{(0.2,1.2) -- (4.0,2.8)},
 9.8/red/3/{Separate}/{all $\beta_j$ free}/{(0.2,0.4) -- (4.0,1.6)}/{(0.2,0.8) -- (4.0,3.0)}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,0)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-0.35,-1.0) rectangle (4.4,3.75);
  \node[circle,fill=\col!60!black,text=white,inner sep=1.5pt,font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (0,3.4) {\num};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.25,3.4) {\name};
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (4.2,0);
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (0,3.0);
  \draw[-,thick,black] \lineA;
  \draw[-,thick,dashed,\col!60!black] \lineB;
  \node[font=\scriptsize,text=\col!50!black] at (2.05,-0.55) {\eqn};
 \end{scope}
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Three models for one quantitative regressor $x$ and a two-level factor. Solid: group $A$
($z = 0$). Dashed: group $B$ ($z = 1$). In panel~1 the two lines are the same line.}
\label{fig:dummy-models}
\end{figure}

One regressor $z$ is enough for two groups. A second indicator, $z' = 1 - z$ for group $A$, would be a
mistake.

\begin{thm}[The dummy-variable trap]\label{thm:dummy-trap}
If a factor has $a$ levels and the model contains an intercept and an indicator for every one of the
$a$ levels, then $\mathbf{X}$ does not have full column rank, and $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}$ is singular.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Every observation belongs to exactly one level, so the $a$ indicator columns add up to the column of ones.
That is a linear dependence among the columns of $\mathbf{X}$, so $\mathbf{Xv} = \mathbf{0}$ for the non-zero
$\mathbf{v} = (1, -1, \dots, -1, 0, \dots, 0)^\top$, and then $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{0}$.
\end{proof}

So a factor with $a$ levels is represented by $a - 1$ indicators. The level with no indicator is the
\textbf{reference level}, and each indicator's coefficient is the difference between its level and the
reference. Which level is the reference is a matter of convenience: the fitted values are the same
whichever is chosen (Problem~\ref{prob:recode}).

\subsection{Fitting and testing}
The models are fitted by ordinary least squares, with $z$ and $xz$ treated as regressors like any other.
Two results explain what the fits are.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:separate-lines}
Fitting the separate-lines model gives the same fitted values as fitting a straight line to each group
on its own. If the separate fits are $\hat y = \hat a_0 + \hat a_1x$ for group $A$ and $\hat y = \hat b_0 + \hat b_1x$ for
group $B$, then
\[
\hat\beta_0 = \hat a_0, \quad \hat\beta_1 = \hat a_1, \quad \hat\beta_2 = \hat b_0 - \hat a_0, \quad \hat\beta_3 = \hat b_1 - \hat a_1 ,
\]
and $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = SS_{\mathrm{Res},A} + SS_{\mathrm{Res},B}$, on $n - 4$ degrees of freedom.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The columns $\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{x}, \mathbf{z}, \mathbf{xz}$ span the same space as $\mathbf{1} - \mathbf{z}$, $(\mathbf{1} - \mathbf{z})\mathbf{x}$,
$\mathbf{z}$, $\mathbf{zx}$ (products taken entry by entry), since each set is a combination of the other. In the
second set, the first two columns are zero on group $B$ and the last two are zero on group $A$. Order the
observations with group $A$ first; then the second matrix is block diagonal,
\[
\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{X}_A & \mathbf{0} \\ \mathbf{0} & \mathbf{X}_B \end{bmatrix} ,
\]
where $\mathbf{X}_A$ and $\mathbf{X}_B$ are the simple-regression matrices of the two groups. The sum of squares
$\|\mathbf{y}_A - \mathbf{X}_A\mathbf{a}\|^2 + \|\mathbf{y}_B - \mathbf{X}_B\mathbf{b}\|^2$ separates, and is minimised by minimising each part,
that is, by the separate least-squares lines. The fitted values and residuals are those of the separate fits.
Matching the means of the two parametrisations at $z = 0$ and at $z = 1$ gives the coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}\label{thm:parallel-lines}
In the parallel-lines model, the common slope is the \emph{pooled} within-group slope,
\[
\hat\beta_1 = \frac{S_{xy,A} + S_{xy,B}}{S_{xx,A} + S_{xx,B}} ,
\]
where $S_{xx,A}$, $S_{xy,A}$ are the corrected sums of squares and products within group $A$, and similarly for
$B$. The fitted lines pass through the group means $(\bar x_A, \bar y_A)$ and $(\bar x_B, \bar y_B)$, so
\[
\hat\beta_0 = \bar y_A - \hat\beta_1\bar x_A , \qquad
\hat\beta_2 = (\bar y_B - \bar y_A) - \hat\beta_1(\bar x_B - \bar x_A) .
\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Apply the added-variable theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable}) with $x$ as the added regressor and
$\mathbf{1}$, $\mathbf{z}$ as the others. Regressing $x$ on $\mathbf{1}$ and $\mathbf{z}$ fits a separate mean to each group
(the same argument as in Theorem~\ref{thm:separate-lines}, with no slope), so the residual is
$u_i = x_i - \bar x_A$ for group $A$ and $x_i - \bar x_B$ for group $B$. Then
\[
\hat\beta_1 = \frac{\sum u_iy_i}{\sum u_i^2} = \frac{\sum_A(x_i - \bar x_A)y_i + \sum_B(x_i - \bar x_B)y_i}{\sum_A(x_i - \bar x_A)^2 + \sum_B(x_i - \bar x_B)^2} ,
\]
which is the stated formula. The normal equation for the intercept column, $\sum e_i = 0$, and the one for
$\mathbf{z}$, $\sum_B e_i = 0$, together give $\sum_A e_i = 0$ and $\sum_B e_i = 0$: within each group the residuals add to
zero, so each line passes through its group's mean point.
\end{proof}

When the groups have the same mean of $x$, as in the next example, $\hat\beta_2 = \bar y_B - \bar y_A$; otherwise the
difference in mean response is adjusted for the difference in $x$. This adjusted comparison is the classical
\textbf{analysis of covariance}, and $x$ is then called a \emph{covariate}.

\subhead{The tests}
Each simplification of the separate-lines model is a hypothesis about $\beta_2$ and $\beta_3$, tested by the
extra-sum-of-squares principle (Subsection~\ref{ssec:extra-ss}) with the separate-lines model as the full model.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Parallelism}, $H_0\colon \beta_3 = 0$: compare the parallel-lines model with the separate lines, a
partial $F$ on $1$ and $n - 4$ degrees of freedom (equivalently the $t$-test for $\beta_3$).
\item \emph{Coincidence}, $H_0\colon \beta_2 = \beta_3 = 0$: compare one common line with the separate lines, a partial
$F$ on $2$ and $n - 4$ degrees of freedom.
\item \emph{Equal intercepts given parallelism}, $H_0\colon \beta_2 = 0$ in the parallel-lines model: the $t$-test for
$\beta_2$, on $n - 3$ degrees of freedom. This is the usual comparison of the two groups after allowing for $x$.
\end{itemize}
The parallelism test is done first: if the slopes differ, the difference between the groups depends on $x$, and
a single number $\beta_2$ does not describe it.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:varieties}
Two maize varieties, $A$ and $B$, were each grown on five plots with $x = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5$ bags of fertiliser per
hectare, giving yields $y$ (bags per hectare):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|ccccc}
$x$ & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 \\
\hline
Variety $A$ & 11 & 13 & 15 & 19 & 19 \\
Variety $B$ & 14 & 16 & 20 & 20 & 22 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Compare the varieties, allowing for fertiliser.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{Separate lines.} In each group $\bar x = 3$ and $S_{xx} = 10$. For $A$, $\bar y_A = 77/5 = 15.4$ and
$S_{xy,A} = -2(11) - 1(13) + 0 + 1(19) + 2(19) = 22$; for $B$, $\bar y_B = 92/5 = 18.4$ and
$S_{xy,B} = -2(14) - 1(16) + 0 + 1(20) + 2(22) = 20$. So
\begin{align*}
A:\quad \hat y &= 15.4 - 2.2 \times 3 + 2.2x = 8.8 + 2.2x , \\
B:\quad \hat y &= 18.4 - 2.0 \times 3 + 2.0x = 12.4 + 2.0x .
\end{align*}
For $A$ the fitted values are $11$, $13.2$, $15.4$, $17.6$, $19.8$, so the residuals are
$0$, $-0.2$, $-0.4$, $1.4$, $-0.8$, with $SS_{\mathrm{Res},A} = 2.8$. For $B$ the fitted values are
$14.4$, $16.4$, $18.4$, $20.4$, $22.4$ and the residuals $-0.4$, $-0.4$, $1.6$, $-0.4$, $-0.4$, with $SS_{\mathrm{Res},B} = 3.2$.
By Theorem~\ref{thm:separate-lines} the separate-lines model is
\[
\hat y = 8.8 + 2.2x + 3.6z - 0.2xz ,
\]
with $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 6.0$ on $10 - 4 = 6$ degrees of freedom, so $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 1.0$.

\emph{Parallel lines.} By Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel-lines}, $\hat\beta_1 = (22 + 20)/(10 + 10) = 2.1$,
$\hat\beta_0 = 15.4 - 2.1 \times 3 = 9.1$, and since $\bar x_A = \bar x_B$, $\hat\beta_2 = 18.4 - 15.4 = 3.0$:
\[
\hat y = 9.1 + 2.1x + 3.0z .
\]
Its residual sum of squares is $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 6.2$ on $7$ degrees of freedom (the two pooled
within-group sums of squares, $\sum_A(y - \bar y_A)^2 + \sum_B(y - \bar y_B)^2 = 51.2 + 43.2 = 94.4$, less
$\hat\beta_1(S_{xy,A} + S_{xy,B}) = 2.1 \times 42 = 88.2$).

\emph{One common line.} Ignoring the variety, $\bar y = 16.9$, $S_{xx} = 20$, $S_{xy} = 42$ (the groups have the same
$x$ values), so $\hat y = 16.9 - 6.3 + 2.1x = 10.6 + 2.1x$. Here $SS_T = 116.9$ and
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 116.9 - 2.1 \times 42 = 28.7$ on $8$ degrees of freedom.

\emph{Tests}, with $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 1.0$ from the separate-lines model:
\begin{align*}
\text{parallelism:}\quad F &= \frac{6.2 - 6.0}{1.0} = 0.2 < F_{0.05,1,6} = 5.99 , \\
\text{coincidence:}\quad F &= \frac{(28.7 - 6.0)/2}{1.0} = 11.35 > F_{0.01,2,6} = 10.92 .
\end{align*}
The slopes do not differ significantly, but the lines are not the same. In the parallel-lines model,
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_2) = \sigma^2\left(\frac{1}{5} + \frac{1}{5}\right) = 0.4\sigma^2
\]
(here $\hat\beta_2 = \bar y_B - \bar y_A$, a difference of two independent means of five), and with
$MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 6.2/7 = 0.886$, $\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_2) = \sqrt{0.886 \times 0.4} = 0.595$ and
$t = 3.0/0.595 = 5.04$ on $7$ degrees of freedom, highly significant. A $95\%$ confidence interval for the
difference is $3.0 \pm 2.365 \times 0.595 = 3.0 \pm 1.41$, that is $(1.6,\ 4.4)$ bags per hectare.

\emph{Conclusion.} Both varieties respond to fertiliser at the same rate, about $2.1$ bags of maize per bag of
fertiliser, and variety $B$ yields about $3$ bags per hectare more than $A$ at every fertiliser level.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.4cm,y=0.3cm]
\draw[-Stealth] (0,9) -- (5.8,9) node[right] {\small $x$};
\draw[-Stealth] (0,9) -- (0,24.5) node[above] {\small $y$};
\foreach \t in {1,...,5} \draw[-] (\t,9.25) -- (\t,8.75) node[below] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\foreach \t in {10,15,20} \draw[-] (0.05,\t) -- (-0.05,\t) node[left] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\draw[-,thick] (0.6,10.36) -- (5.4,20.44) node[right,font=\small] {$A$: $9.1 + 2.1x$};
\draw[-,thick,dashed,red!70!black] (0.6,13.36) -- (5.4,23.44) node[right,font=\small] {$B$: $12.1 + 2.1x$};
\foreach \p in {(1,11),(2,13),(3,15),(4,19),(5,19)} \fill \p circle (2.4pt);
\foreach \p in {(1,14),(2,16),(3,20),(4,20),(5,22)} \draw[red!70!black,thick] \p circle (2.6pt);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The maize data of Example~\ref{exa:varieties}, with the fitted parallel lines. Filled circles:
variety $A$; open circles: variety $B$.}
\label{fig:varieties}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Factors with more than two levels}
A factor with $a$ levels needs $a - 1$ indicators. With three levels $A$, $B$, $C$ and reference level $A$, let
$z_1 = 1$ for level $B$ and $z_2 = 1$ for level $C$, both $0$ otherwise:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|cc}
Level & $z_1$ & $z_2$ \\
\hline
$A$ & 0 & 0 \\
$B$ & 1 & 0 \\
$C$ & 0 & 1 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2z_1 + \beta_3z_2 + \varepsilon$, the three groups have parallel lines with intercepts
$\beta_0$, $\beta_0 + \beta_2$ and $\beta_0 + \beta_3$, so $\beta_2$ and $\beta_3$ compare $B$ and $C$ with $A$; $B$ is compared with $C$
through $\beta_2 - \beta_3$. Whether the factor matters at all is the test of $H_0\colon \beta_2 = \beta_3 = 0$, a partial $F$
on $2$ degrees of freedom; testing $\beta_2$ and $\beta_3$ separately does not answer that question.

It would be wrong to code the three levels as one regressor taking the values $1$, $2$, $3$. That forces the
effect of moving from $A$ to $B$ to equal the effect of moving from $B$ to $C$, an assumption with no basis for
categories that have no order.

\begin{thm}[Regression and one-way analysis of variance]\label{thm:oneway}
Suppose the only regressors are the $a - 1$ indicators of a factor, with $n_i$ observations at level $i$. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the fitted value of every observation is the mean $\bar y_i$ of its level; $\hat\beta_0$ is the reference-level
mean, and the coefficient of each indicator is the difference between its level's mean and the
reference-level mean;
\item $SS_R = \sum_i n_i(\bar y_i - \bar y)^2$ and $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = \sum_i\sum_j(y_{ij} - \bar y_i)^2$, the between-groups and
within-groups sums of squares, and the $F$-test for significance of regression is the one-way analysis of
variance $F$-test of equal level means.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(i) The intercept and the $a - 1$ indicators span the same space as the $a$ indicators of all the levels (the
reference indicator is the ones column minus the others). That space consists of the vectors that are constant
within each level, and, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:lof-test}, projecting onto it replaces each $y_{ij}$
by its level mean. Matching $\beta_0 + \beta_j$ with $\bar y_j$ and $\beta_0$ with the reference mean gives the coefficients.

(ii) $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = \sum(y_{ij} - \hat y_{ij})^2$ with $\hat y_{ij} = \bar y_i$, and
$SS_R = \sum(\hat y_{ij} - \bar y)^2 = \sum_i n_i(\bar y_i - \bar y)^2$. The regression has $a - 1$ degrees of freedom and
the residual $n - a$, the same as the analysis of variance, so the two $F$ statistics coincide.
\end{proof}

So the one-way analysis of variance is a special case of regression, and Theorem~\ref{thm:means-wls} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:lof-identity} were already using the same structure. Adding a quantitative regressor $x$ to
the indicators gives the analysis of covariance.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:methods}
Twelve learners were taught a topic by one of three methods, four by each, and their test scores were:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|cccc}
Method $A$ & 5 & 7 & 6 & 6 \\
Method $B$ & 9 & 8 & 10 & 9 \\
Method $C$ & 7 & 8 & 6 & 7 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit a regression with indicators for $B$ and $C$, and test whether the method matters.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The level means are $\bar y_A = 6$, $\bar y_B = 9$, $\bar y_C = 7$ and $\bar y = 88/12 = 7.333$. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:oneway},
\[
\hat y = 6 + 3z_1 + 1z_2 .
\]
Method $B$ scores $3$ marks more than method $A$ on average, method $C$ $1$ mark more. Then
\begin{align*}
SS_R &= 4\bigl[(6 - 7.333)^2 + (9 - 7.333)^2 + (7 - 7.333)^2\bigr] \\
&= 4(1.778 + 2.778 + 0.111) = 18.67 , \\
SS_{\mathrm{Res}} &= (1 + 1 + 0 + 0) + (0 + 1 + 1 + 0) + (0 + 1 + 1 + 0) = 6 ,
\end{align*}
on $2$ and $9$ degrees of freedom, so
\[
F_0 = \frac{18.67/2}{6/9} = \frac{9.333}{0.667} = 14.0 > F_{0.05,2,9} = 4.26 .
\]
The method matters. The same table is the one-way analysis of variance of the three groups. With
$MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 0.667$ and four learners per group, each comparison with $A$ has standard error
$\sqrt{0.667(1/4 + 1/4)} = 0.577$, so $\hat\beta_1 = 3$ gives $t = 5.2$ and $\hat\beta_2 = 1$ gives $t = 1.73$: $B$ is clearly better
than $A$, while the evidence for $C$ over $A$ is weak.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Piecewise linear regression}
Indicators also let the \emph{shape} of a relationship change at a known value of $x$. Suppose the slope
changes at $x = t$, but the relationship stays continuous. Let
\[
(x - t)_+ = \begin{cases} 0 , & x \le t , \\ x - t , & x > t , \end{cases}
\]
which is $(x - t)z$ with $z$ the indicator of $x > t$. The model
\[
y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2(x - t)_+ + \varepsilon
\]
has mean $\beta_0 + \beta_1x$ for $x \le t$ and $\beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2(x - t) = (\beta_0 - \beta_2t) + (\beta_1 + \beta_2)x$ for $x > t$. The
two pieces meet at $x = t$, and the slope changes by $\beta_2$ there. The point $t$ is called a \emph{knot}, and the
fitted curve is a \emph{linear spline}. Testing $H_0\colon \beta_2 = 0$ tests whether the slope changes. If the
relationship may also jump at $t$, add $z$ itself as a regressor. (When the knot is unknown, the model is
non-linear in $t$; that case belongs to the section on non-linear estimation.)

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:recode}
In Example~\ref{exa:varieties}, suppose the indicator is defined the other way round, $w = 1$ for variety $A$ and
$0$ for variety $B$. Write down the fitted parallel-lines model without refitting, and show in general that
the fitted values do not depend on which level is the reference.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
$w = 1 - z$, so $9.1 + 2.1x + 3.0z = 9.1 + 2.1x + 3.0(1 - w) = 12.1 + 2.1x - 3.0w$. In general, the columns
$\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{x}, \mathbf{z}$ and $\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{x}, \mathbf{1} - \mathbf{z}$ span the same space, and the least-squares fitted
values are the projection of $\mathbf{y}$ onto that space, whatever basis describes it. Only the meaning of the
coefficients changes: the intercept becomes the mean for the new reference level, and the indicator
coefficient changes sign.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:cell-means}
For a factor with $a$ levels, show that the model $y = \gamma_1z_1 + \cdots + \gamma_az_a + \varepsilon$, with an indicator for
\emph{every} level and \emph{no} intercept, has full column rank, and that $\hat\gamma_i = \bar y_i$. How is it related to
the model with an intercept and $a - 1$ indicators?
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The columns of the $a$ indicators are non-zero and have no $1$ in a common row, so they are orthogonal and
hence linearly independent. $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X} = \operatorname{diag}(n_1, \dots, n_a)$ and $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y}$ has entries
$\sum_j y_{ij} = n_i\bar y_i$, so $\hat\gamma_i = \bar y_i$. It spans the same column space as the model with an
intercept and $a - 1$ indicators (Theorem~\ref{thm:oneway}), so it gives the same fit, reparametrised:
$\gamma_A = \beta_0$, and $\gamma_i = \beta_0 + \beta_i$ for the other levels. This \emph{cell-means} form avoids the dummy-variable
trap by dropping the intercept instead of an indicator. Note that its $R^2$, computed by software as for a model
without an intercept, is not comparable with the usual $R^2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:interaction-read}
A model for weekly earnings $y$ (kwacha) of traders, with $x$ years of experience and $z = 1$ for traders in a
city market and $0$ for a rural market, was fitted as
\[
\hat y = 420 + 35x + 150z + 20xz .
\]
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Write down the fitted lines for the two markets.
\item What is the estimated difference between city and rural earnings for a trader with $5$ years'
experience? With none?
\item Why would it be misleading to report ``city traders earn $150$ kwacha more''?
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) Rural ($z = 0$): $\hat y = 420 + 35x$. City ($z = 1$): $\hat y = 570 + 55x$.

(b) The difference is $150 + 20x$: $250$ kwacha at $x = 5$, and $150$ at $x = 0$.

(c) With an interaction, $\beta_2 = 150$ is the difference only at $x = 0$, for traders with no experience. The gap
grows by $20$ kwacha with each year of experience, so there is no single difference to report; it must be
given as a function of $x$, or at stated values of $x$ that are typical of the data.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:spline}
The output $y$ of a process was measured at eight settings, and the engineers believe the response steepens
beyond $x = 4$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|cccccccc}
$x$ & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 & 8 \\
\hline
$y$ & 3 & 4 & 6 & 6 & 9 & 11 & 15 & 17 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2(x - 4)_+ + \varepsilon$ and test whether the slope changes. The straight line
$y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \varepsilon$ has $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 8.821$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The values of $(x - 4)_+$ are $0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4$. Then
\[
\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X} = \begin{bmatrix} 8 & 36 & 10 \\ 36 & 204 & 70 \\ 10 & 70 & 30 \end{bmatrix} ,
\qquad
\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y} = \begin{bmatrix} 71 \\ 405 \\ 144 \end{bmatrix} ,
\]
with $\det(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}) = 880$. By cofactors, as in Example~\ref{exa:crop-fit},
\[
(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1} = \frac{1}{880}\begin{bmatrix} 1220 & -380 & 480 \\ -380 & 140 & -200 \\ 480 & -200 & 336 \end{bmatrix} ,
\]
and
\[
\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = \frac{1}{880}\begin{bmatrix} 1220(71) - 380(405) + 480(144) \\ -380(71) + 140(405) - 200(144) \\ 480(71) - 200(405) + 336(144) \end{bmatrix}
= \frac{1}{880}\begin{bmatrix} 1840 \\ 920 \\ 1464 \end{bmatrix}
= \begin{bmatrix} 2.091 \\ 1.045 \\ 1.664 \end{bmatrix} .
\]
So the slope is $1.045$ up to $x = 4$ and $1.045 + 1.664 = 2.709$ beyond it. The residual sum of squares is
$1.573$ on $5$ degrees of freedom, and
\[
F = \frac{8.821 - 1.573}{1.573/5} = \frac{7.248}{0.3145} = 23.0 > F_{0.05,1,5} = 6.61 .
\]
The slope does change at $x = 4$: the response rises nearly three times as fast beyond it.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:three-level-x}
A model is to compare three schools, allowing for each learner's previous score $x$, with possibly different
slopes in each school. How many parameters does it have, and what are the regressors? Which test examines
whether the slopes are equal, and on how many degrees of freedom?
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
With indicators $z_1$, $z_2$ for two of the schools, the regressors are $x$, $z_1$, $z_2$, $xz_1$ and $xz_2$, and with the
intercept there are $6$ parameters: an intercept and slope for each school. Equal slopes is
$H_0\colon \beta_{xz_1} = \beta_{xz_2} = 0$, a partial $F$ comparing this model with the parallel-lines model in $x$, $z_1$, $z_2$, on
$2$ and $n - 6$ degrees of freedom.
\end{solution}

\section{Polynomial regression}
Many relationships are curved: yield rises with fertiliser and then falls off, cost per unit falls with
output and then rises, a growth curve bends. When the curvature is smooth and the theory gives no
particular non-linear form, a polynomial in the regressors is the natural first model to try. A
polynomial such as
\[
y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2x^2 + \varepsilon
\]
is curved in $x$ but \emph{linear in the parameters}: it is the multiple regression model with regressors
$x_1 = x$ and $x_2 = x^2$. So everything from Sections 3 to 9 applies: least squares, the tests, the
residual analysis. This section is about what is special to polynomials: how to choose the order, the
ill-conditioning of the powers of $x$ and how to remove it, orthogonal polynomials, piecewise
polynomials, models in several regressors, and the stationary points of a fitted surface.

\subsection{Polynomial models in one variable}
The polynomial model of order $k$ in one variable is
\[
y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2x^2 + \dots + \beta_kx^k + \varepsilon ,
\]
and the commonest cases are the quadratic ($k = 2$) and the cubic ($k = 3$). In the quadratic, $\beta_1$ is no longer
``the slope'': the slope of the mean, $\beta_1 + 2\beta_2x$, changes with $x$. The parameter $\beta_2$ measures the
curvature. The mean function is concave when $\beta_2 < 0$ and convex when $\beta_2 > 0$, and it turns at
$x = -\beta_1/(2\beta_2)$.

\subhead{Considerations in fitting a polynomial}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item \emph{Keep the order low.} A polynomial of high enough order fits any data, as the next result shows,
and a perfect fit describes the noise as well as the trend. Transformations (Section~6) may straighten
the relationship with fewer parameters; orders above $3$ are seldom justified.
\item \emph{Build the model systematically.} Either raise the order one term at a time until the highest term is
not significant (forward), or start from a deliberately high order and remove the highest term while it is
not significant (backward). The two need not stop at the same order.
\item \emph{Do not extrapolate.} Beyond the range of the data, a polynomial can turn in directions the process
never takes: a fitted quadratic yield curve, carried far enough, predicts negative yields.
\item \emph{Expect ill-conditioning.} The powers $x, x^2, x^3, \dots$ are highly correlated when the range of $x$ is
narrow relative to its distance from zero, and the problem worsens with each power. Centring $x$ removes
much of it (Theorem~\ref{thm:centred-poly}).
\item \emph{Keep the hierarchy.} A model containing $x^k$ should contain all the lower powers. A model such as
$y = \beta_0 + \beta_2x^2 + \varepsilon$ changes its meaning when the origin of $x$ is moved
(Theorem~\ref{thm:poly-invariance}).
\end{enumerate}
Figure~\ref{fig:poly-cautions} illustrates the first, third and fourth points.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\py/\col/\num/\name in {
 0/4.75/blue/1/{Order too high},
 6.9/4.75/red/2/{Extrapolation},
 0/0/orange/3/{$x$ against $x^2$: $r = 0.98$},
 6.9/0/green/4/{Centred: $r = 0$}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,\py)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-0.3,-0.45) rectangle (6.3,3.85);

  \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.25,3.5) {\name};
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (6.0,0);
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (0,3.1);
  \ifnum\num=1

\caption{Cautions with polynomials. (1) The sixth-degree polynomial through seven points (solid) against a
quadratic (dashed). (2) A quadratic carried beyond the data. (3) For $x = 1, \dots, 7$ the columns $x$ and $x^2$
are almost collinear. (4) After centring, $u$ and $u^2$ are uncorrelated.}

\begin{prop}\label{prop:interpolate}
If the $n$ observations have distinct values $x_1, \dots, x_n$, the polynomial of order $n - 1$ fits them exactly:
every residual is zero.
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
The model matrix is the $n \times n$ Vandermonde matrix with rows $(1, x_i, x_i^2, \dots, x_i^{n-1})$. If
$\mathbf{Xc} = \mathbf{0}$, the polynomial $c_0 + c_1x + \dots + c_{n-1}x^{n-1}$, of degree at most $n - 1$, has the $n$ distinct roots
$x_1, \dots, x_n$, so it is the zero polynomial and $\mathbf{c} = \mathbf{0}$. So $\mathbf{X}$ is non-singular, the equations
$\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf{y}$ have a solution, and least squares finds it, with $\mathbf{e} = \mathbf{0}$.
\end{proof}

So $R^2 = 1$ is always within reach, and it means nothing: the fit has no degrees of freedom left with which
to estimate $\sigma^2$, and the curve between the data points (panel~1 of Figure~\ref{fig:poly-cautions}) is not
believable.

\subhead{Centring and the hierarchy principle}
The next result says that moving the origin of $x$ or changing its units leaves a full polynomial fit
unchanged.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:poly-invariance}
Let $z = (x - c)/d$ with $d \ne 0$. The polynomial models of order $k$ in $x$ and in $z$,
\[
y = \sum_{j=0}^k \beta_jx^j + \varepsilon \quad\text{and}\quad y = \sum_{j=0}^k \gamma_jz^j + \varepsilon ,
\]
have the same fitted values, residuals and sums of squares, and the highest coefficients are related by
$\gamma_k = d^k\beta_k$. A model that leaves out some lower power does not have this property in general.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
By the binomial theorem,
\[
z^j = d^{-j}(x - c)^j = d^{-j}\sum_{i=0}^j \binom{j}{i}(-c)^{j-i}x^i ,
\]
so each column $z^j$ is a combination of $1, x, \dots, x^j$, and $\mathbf{X}_z = \mathbf{X}_x\mathbf{T}$ with $\mathbf{T}$ upper triangular. The
diagonal entries of $\mathbf{T}$ are $d^{-j} \ne 0$, so $\mathbf{T}$ is non-singular. Then the hat matrices agree:
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{H}_z &= \mathbf{X}_x\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{T}^\top\mathbf{X}_x^\top\mathbf{X}_x\mathbf{T})^{-1}\mathbf{T}^\top\mathbf{X}_x^\top \\
&= \mathbf{X}_x\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}^{-1}(\mathbf{X}_x^\top\mathbf{X}_x)^{-1}(\mathbf{T}^\top)^{-1}\mathbf{T}^\top\mathbf{X}_x^\top = \mathbf{H}_x ,
\end{align*}
so $\hat{\mathbf{y}} = \mathbf{Hy}$, $\mathbf{e}$ and every sum of squares are the same for both.
Matching the coefficients of $x^k$ in $\sum_j\gamma_jz^j = \sum_j\beta_jx^j$, only $z^k$ contains $x^k$, with coefficient
$d^{-k}$, so $\gamma_kd^{-k} = \beta_k$.

For the last statement, take $y = \beta_0 + \beta_2x^2 + \varepsilon$. In terms of $x$, the variable $z^2 = (x^2 - 2cx + c^2)/d^2$
contains $x$ when $c \ne 0$, so the column space of $(\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{z}^2)$ contains a vector with a non-zero $x$-component,
which $(\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{x}^2)$ does not. The two models are different models.
\end{proof}

So for a full polynomial, the choice of origin and scale is free, and the test of the highest-order term is the
same whatever the coding, since $\gamma_k$ and $\beta_k$ differ only by the known factor $d^k$. The lower coefficients do change: in the
quadratic, $\beta_1$ is the slope at $x = 0$ and $\gamma_1$ the slope at $x = c$ (times $d$). Testing $\beta_1 = 0$ in a quadratic
asks whether the slope is zero at the origin, which depends on where the origin is; that is why
removing a lower-order term while keeping a higher one is rarely sensible. The freedom to move the origin
is what makes centring possible.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:centred-poly}
Suppose the values of $x$ are symmetric about $\bar x$, so that the deviations $u_i = x_i - \bar x$ are the same
set of numbers as $-u_i$. Then $\sum_i u_i^m = 0$ for every odd $m$. In particular, in the quadratic in $u$
the column $\mathbf{u}$ is orthogonal to both $\mathbf{1}$ and $\mathbf{u}^2$, and $\operatorname{corr}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{u}^2) = 0$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Pairing each $u_i$ with the observation whose deviation is $-u_i$, the odd powers cancel in pairs, and a
deviation equal to $0$ contributes nothing. Then $\mathbf{1}^\top\mathbf{u} = \sum u_i = 0$, and $\mathbf{u}^\top\mathbf{u}^2 = \sum u_i^3 = 0$. The sample
covariance of $u$ and $u^2$ is $\frac{1}{n}\sum u_i u_i^2 - \bar u\,\overline{u^2} = 0 - 0$, so the correlation is zero.
\end{proof}

For $x = 1, \dots, 7$, $\operatorname{corr}(x, x^2) = 0.977$, so in the uncentred quadratic both regressors have
$\mathrm{VIF} = 1/(1 - 0.977^2) = 22.3$; after centring at $\bar x = 4$, both VIFs are $1$. When the design is not symmetric,
centring does not make the correlation exactly zero, but it usually reduces it greatly
(Problem~\ref{prob:centring}).

\begin{exa}\label{exa:irrigation}
A farmer applied seven levels of irrigation to tomato plots and recorded the yields.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|ccccccc}
Irrigation, $x$ & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 \\
\hline
Yield, $y$ & 8 & 16 & 23 & 26 & 27 & 29 & 26 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The units of $x$ are 10~mm of water a week, and $y$ is in kilograms per plot. Fit a quadratic, test the
quadratic term, and find the fitted yield and its turning point.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Centre at $\bar x = 4$, so $u = x - 4$ takes the values $-3, \dots, 3$. The needed sums are
\begin{gather*}
\textstyle\sum u = 0, \quad \sum u^2 = 28, \quad \sum u^3 = 0, \quad \sum u^4 = 196, \\
\textstyle\sum y = 155, \quad \sum uy = 84, \quad \sum u^2y = 536 .
\end{gather*}
The normal equations for $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1u + \beta_2u^2 + \varepsilon$ are
\[
\begin{pmatrix} 7 & 0 & 28 \\ 0 & 28 & 0 \\ 28 & 0 & 196 \end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix} \hat\beta_0 \\ \hat\beta_1 \\ \hat\beta_2 \end{pmatrix}
= \begin{pmatrix} 155 \\ 84 \\ 536 \end{pmatrix} .
\]
The middle equation, uncoupled from the others by Theorem~\ref{thm:centred-poly}, gives $\hat\beta_1 = 84/28 = 3$. The
other two have determinant $7(196) - 28^2 = 588$, so
\begin{align*}
\hat\beta_0 &= \frac{196(155) - 28(536)}{588} = \frac{15372}{588} = 26.143 , \\
\hat\beta_2 &= \frac{7(536) - 28(155)}{588} = \frac{-588}{588} = -1 .
\end{align*}
The fitted model is
\[
\hat y = 26.143 + 3u - u^2 = -1.857 + 11x - x^2 ,
\]
the second form by substituting $u = x - 4$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:poly-invariance}: the fits are the same). The fitted
values are $8.14, 16.14, 22.14, 26.14, 28.14, 28.14, 26.14$, and the residuals are
\[
-0.14, \quad -0.14, \quad 0.86, \quad -0.14, \quad -1.14, \quad 0.86, \quad -0.14 ,
\]
so $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 2.857$ on $7 - 3 = 4$ degrees of freedom and $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 0.714$. With $\sum y^2 = 3771$,
$SS_T = 3771 - 155^2/7 = 338.857$.

The quadratic term. The $(3,3)$ element of $(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}$ is $7/588 = 1/84$, so
\[
\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_2) = \sqrt{0.714/84} = 0.0922, \qquad t_0 = \frac{-1}{0.0922} = -10.84 ,
\]
and $|t_0| > t_{0.025,4} = 2.776$. The curvature is real: a straight line would not do.

The turning point. The fitted yield is greatest where $3 - 2u = 0$, at $u = 1.5$, or $x = 5.5$, where
$\hat y = 26.143 + 4.5 - 2.25 = 28.39$~kg. Its standard error is found in Example~\ref{exa:irrigation-max}.
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}[The turning point of a quadratic]\label{thm:turning}
In the fitted quadratic $\hat y = \hat\beta_0 + \hat\beta_1u + \hat\beta_2u^2$, the turning point $\hat u_0 = -\hat\beta_1/(2\hat\beta_2)$ has
approximate variance
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Var}(\hat u_0) \approx \frac{1}{4\beta_2^2}\Bigl[&\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_1) + 4u_0\operatorname{Cov}(\hat\beta_1, \hat\beta_2) \\
&+ 4u_0^2\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_2)\Bigr] ,
\end{align*}
estimated by putting $\hat\beta_2$, $\hat u_0$ and the estimated variances and covariance in place of the unknowns.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
This is the delta method (Lemma~\ref{lem:delta}) with two variables. Let $g(a, b) = -a/(2b)$. To first order
around $(\beta_1, \beta_2)$,
\[
g(\hat\beta_1, \hat\beta_2) \approx g(\beta_1, \beta_2) + g_a(\hat\beta_1 - \beta_1) + g_b(\hat\beta_2 - \beta_2) ,
\]
with $g_a = -1/(2\beta_2)$ and $g_b = \beta_1/(2\beta_2^2) = -u_0/\beta_2$. The variance of the linear function on the right is
$g_a^2\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_1) + 2g_ag_b\operatorname{Cov}(\hat\beta_1, \hat\beta_2) + g_b^2\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_2)$. Since
$g_a^2 = 1/(4\beta_2^2)$, $2g_ag_b = u_0/\beta_2^2$ and $g_b^2 = u_0^2/\beta_2^2$, taking out the factor $1/(4\beta_2^2)$ gives the formula.
\end{proof}

When the design is symmetric and $u$ is centred, $\operatorname{Cov}(\hat\beta_1, \hat\beta_2) = 0$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:centred-poly}, and the middle
term disappears.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:irrigation-max}
Use the irrigation data of Example~\ref{exa:irrigation} to find an approximate $95\%$ confidence
interval for the level of irrigation that gives the greatest mean yield.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
From $(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}$, $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_1) = \sigma^2/28$, $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_2) = \sigma^2/84$ and the covariance is zero. With
$\hat\beta_2 = -1$, $\hat u_0 = 1.5$ and Theorem~\ref{thm:turning},
\begin{align*}
\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat u_0) &= \frac{MS_{\mathrm{Res}}}{4}\Bigl[\frac{1}{28} + \frac{4(1.5)^2}{84}\Bigr] \\
&= \frac{0.714}{4}\Bigl[\frac{3}{84} + \frac{9}{84}\Bigr] = \frac{0.714}{28} = 0.0255 ,
\end{align*}
so $\operatorname{se}(\hat u_0) = 0.160$. With $t_{0.025,4} = 2.776$ the interval for $u_0$ is $1.5 \pm 0.44$, and adding $4$,
\[
5.06 \le x_0 \le 5.94 .
\]
The best irrigation level is between about $51$ and $59$~mm a week. Figure~\ref{fig:irrigation} shows the fit.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=1.25,yscale=0.14]
\fill[green!12] (5.06,0) rectangle (5.94,32);
\draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (8,0) node[right,black,font=\small] {$x$};
\draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (0,34) node[above,black,font=\small] {yield $y$};
\foreach \t in {1,...,7} \draw[gray] (\t,0) -- (\t,-0.6) node[below,black,font=\scriptsize] {\t};
\foreach \t in {10,20,30} \draw[gray] (0,\t) -- (-0.1,\t) node[left,black,font=\scriptsize] {\t};
\draw[-,thick,blue!60!black] plot[domain=0.8:7.3,samples=40] (\x,{-1.857+11*\x-\x*\x});
\draw[-,dashed,green!40!black] (5.5,0) -- (5.5,28.39);
\fill[green!40!black] (5.5,28.39) circle (0.06 and 0.55);
\node[font=\scriptsize,text=green!30!black,anchor=south] at (5.5,29.2) {$\hat y = 28.4$};
\node[font=\scriptsize,text=green!30!black,align=center] at (5.5,-5.6) {$95\%$ interval for $x_0$};
\foreach \a/\b in {1/8,2/16,3/23,4/26,5/27,6/29,7/26}
 \fill[black] (\a,\b) circle (0.07 and 0.6);
\node[font=\scriptsize,text=blue!50!black,anchor=west] at (0.6,31) {$\hat y = -1.857 + 11x - x^2$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The irrigation data with the fitted quadratic. The turning point $x_0 = 5.5$ is marked, and the shaded
band is its approximate $95\%$ confidence interval $(5.06, 5.94)$.}
\label{fig:irrigation}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Orthogonal polynomials}
The centred quadratic still had $\mathbf{1}$ and $\mathbf{u}^2$ correlated, and adding a cubic term would change every
lower coefficient. Both problems disappear if the powers are replaced by polynomials that are
\emph{orthogonal} over the observed $x$ values. Let
\[
y_i = \alpha_0P_0(x_i) + \alpha_1P_1(x_i) + \dots + \alpha_kP_k(x_i) + \varepsilon_i ,
\]
where $P_j$ is a polynomial of degree exactly $j$, $P_0 = 1$, and
\[
\sum_{i=1}^n P_j(x_i)P_l(x_i) = 0 \qquad (j \ne l) .
\]
The $P_j$ can be built by the Gram--Schmidt process: $P_j$ is $x^j$ minus its projection on $P_0, \dots, P_{j-1}$. Since
each $P_j$ has degree exactly $j$, the columns $P_0, \dots, P_k$ span the same space as $1, x, \dots, x^k$, so by the argument of
Theorem~\ref{thm:poly-invariance} the fitted values are those of the ordinary polynomial.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:orthogonal-columns}
If the columns $\mathbf{p}_0, \dots, \mathbf{p}_k$ of $\mathbf{X}$ are mutually orthogonal, then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\hat\alpha_j = \mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{y}/\mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{p}_j$, which does not depend on which other columns are in the model;
\item $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\alpha_j) = \sigma^2/\mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{p}_j$, and the $\hat\alpha_j$ are uncorrelated;
\item the regression sum of squares splits as $SS_R = \sum_{j \ge 1}SS(\alpha_j)$ with
$SS(\alpha_j) = (\mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{y})^2/\mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{p}_j$, and $SS(\alpha_j)$ is also the extra sum of squares for adding
$\mathbf{p}_j$ to a model containing any of the other columns.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
$\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X} = \operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{p}_0^\top\mathbf{p}_0, \dots, \mathbf{p}_k^\top\mathbf{p}_k)$, so
$\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y}$ has the components in (i), each involving only its own column,
and $\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}) = \sigma^2(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}$ is diagonal, which is (ii). For (iii), with $\mathbf{p}_0 = \mathbf{1}$,
\[
SS_R = \hat{\boldsymbol\alpha}^\top\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y} - n\bar y^2 = \sum_{j=0}^k \hat\alpha_j\mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{y} - \frac{(\mathbf{1}^\top\mathbf{y})^2}{n}
= \sum_{j=1}^k \frac{(\mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{y})^2}{\mathbf{p}_j^\top\mathbf{p}_j} ,
\]
because the $j = 0$ term is $(\mathbf{1}^\top\mathbf{y})^2/n$. The same calculation for any subset of the columns gives the sum of the
same terms over the subset, so adding $\mathbf{p}_j$ always adds exactly $SS(\alpha_j)$.
\end{proof}

So with orthogonal polynomials, the order can be raised one term at a time without refitting, and the sequence
of tests in consideration (2) above reads straight off one table.

\subhead{Equally spaced values}
When $x_i = a + (i - 1)d$, $i = 1, \dots, n$, write $u_i = (x_i - \bar x)/d$, which takes the values $-(n - 1)/2, \dots, (n - 1)/2$ in steps of
one. The first two orthogonal polynomials are
\[
P_1 = u , \qquad P_2 = u^2 - \frac{n^2 - 1}{12} .
\]
To check this: the $u_i$ are symmetric about $0$, so $\sum u_i = \sum u_i^3 = 0$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:centred-poly}), which gives
$\sum P_1 = 0$ and $\sum P_1P_2 = \sum u^3 - \frac{n^2 - 1}{12}\sum u = 0$. For $\sum P_2 = 0$ we need
$\sum u_i^2 = n(n^2 - 1)/12$. With $u_i = i - (n + 1)/2$,
\begin{align*}
\sum u_i^2 &= \sum i^2 - n\Bigl(\frac{n + 1}{2}\Bigr)^2 = \frac{n(n + 1)(2n + 1)}{6} - \frac{n(n + 1)^2}{4} \\
&= \frac{n(n + 1)\bigl[2(2n + 1) - 3(n + 1)\bigr]}{12} = \frac{n(n + 1)(n - 1)}{12} .
\end{align*}
Higher polynomials are found the same way. The polynomials are usually scaled by constants $\lambda_j$ so
that their values are small integers. For $n = 7$, $u = -3, \dots, 3$, $P_2 = u^2 - 4$ and $P_3 = (u^3 - 7u)/6$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrrr|r}
$u$ & $-3$ & $-2$ & $-1$ & $0$ & $1$ & $2$ & $3$ & $\sum P_j^2$ \\
\hline
$P_1$ & $-3$ & $-2$ & $-1$ & $0$ & $1$ & $2$ & $3$ & 28 \\
$P_2$ & $5$ & $0$ & $-3$ & $-4$ & $-3$ & $0$ & $5$ & 84 \\
$P_3$ & $-1$ & $1$ & $1$ & $0$ & $-1$ & $-1$ & $1$ & 6 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Each row sums to zero, and the products of any two rows sum to zero, as can be checked directly.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:irrigation-orth}
Fit polynomials up to the cubic to the irrigation data of Example~\ref{exa:irrigation}, using orthogonal polynomials,
and decide the order.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
With $y = 8, 16, 23, 26, 27, 29, 26$ and the table above,
\begin{align*}
\textstyle\sum P_1y &= -24 - 32 - 23 + 0 + 27 + 58 + 78 = 84 , \\
\textstyle\sum P_2y &= 40 + 0 - 69 - 104 - 81 + 0 + 130 = -84 , \\
\textstyle\sum P_3y &= -8 + 16 + 23 + 0 - 27 - 29 + 26 = 1 .
\end{align*}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:orthogonal-columns}, $\hat\alpha_0 = \bar y = 22.143$, $\hat\alpha_1 = 84/28 = 3$,
$\hat\alpha_2 = -84/84 = -1$ and $\hat\alpha_3 = 1/6 = 0.167$, and these values do not change as terms are added or removed. The
sums of squares are
\begin{gather*}
SS(\alpha_1) = \frac{84^2}{28} = 252, \qquad SS(\alpha_2) = \frac{84^2}{84} = 84, \\
SS(\alpha_3) = \frac{1^2}{6} = 0.167 .
\end{gather*}
From $SS_T = 338.857$, the residual for the cubic model is $338.857 - 252 - 84 - 0.167 = 2.690$ on $3$ degrees of
freedom.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
Source & SS & df & MS & $F_0$ \\
\hline
Linear & 252.000 & 1 & 252.000 & 281.0 \\
Quadratic & 84.000 & 1 & 84.000 & 93.7 \\
Cubic & 0.167 & 1 & 0.167 & 0.19 \\
Residual & 2.690 & 3 & 0.897 & \\
\hline
Total & 338.857 & 6 & & \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The cubic term is far from significant ($F_{0.05,1,3} = 10.13$), so the quadratic is enough. Refitting the quadratic only
moves the cubic sum of squares into the residual, $2.690 + 0.167 = 2.857$ on $4$ degrees of freedom, and the quadratic
test becomes $F_0 = 84/0.714 = 117.6$, which is $t_0^2 = 10.84^2$ from Example~\ref{exa:irrigation}. The fitted quadratic is
\[
\hat y = 22.143 + 3P_1 - P_2 = 22.143 + 3u - (u^2 - 4) = 26.143 + 3u - u^2 ,
\]
the same as before.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Piecewise polynomials: splines}
A single low-order polynomial may fit some ranges of $x$ well and others badly, and raising its order brings
the problems of Figure~\ref{fig:poly-cautions}. An alternative is to divide the range of $x$ at points
$t_1 < t_2 < \dots < t_h$, called \emph{knots}, and fit a low-order polynomial on each piece, joined smoothly at the knots.
This was done with straight lines in Section~9 (piecewise linear regression). The usual choice is cubic pieces.

A \textbf{cubic spline} with knots $t_1, \dots, t_h$ is a function that is a cubic polynomial on each interval between
consecutive knots (and beyond the end knots), and that is continuous with continuous first and second
derivatives everywhere. Joining the pieces this smoothly makes the joins invisible to the eye.

\begin{thm}[Truncated power basis]\label{thm:spline-basis}
A function $s$ is a cubic spline with knots $t_1 < \dots < t_h$ if and only if
\[
s(x) = \beta_{0} + \beta_{1}x + \beta_{2}x^2 + \beta_{3}x^3 + \sum_{i=1}^h \gamma_i(x - t_i)_+^3
\]
for some constants, where $(x - t)_+ = \max(x - t, 0)$. So the cubic splines with $h$ given knots form a
linear model with $h + 4$ parameters.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
\emph{If.} $(x - t)_+^3$ is $0$ for $x \le t$ and the cubic $(x - t)^3$ for $x > t$. Its first and second derivatives are
$3(x - t)_+^2$ and $6(x - t)_+$, which are continuous, with value $0$ at $x = t$ from both sides. So each term is a
piecewise cubic with two continuous derivatives, and so is the sum.

\emph{Only if.} Let $s$ be a cubic spline, and let $c_0$ be the cubic that equals $s$ on $x \le t_1$, and $c_i$ the cubic that
equals $s$ on $(t_i, t_{i+1}]$ (with $t_{h+1} = \infty$). At $t_i$, $s$ and its first two derivatives are continuous, so the cubic
$c_i - c_{i-1}$ and its first two derivatives vanish at $t_i$. Expanded about $t_i$, a cubic is
$a_0 + a_1(x - t_i) + a_2(x - t_i)^2 + a_3(x - t_i)^3$, and the conditions force $a_0 = a_1 = a_2 = 0$, so
$c_i - c_{i-1} = \gamma_i(x - t_i)^3$ for some $\gamma_i$. Then for $x$ in the $i$th interval,
\[
s(x) = c_0(x) + \sum_{l=1}^i \gamma_l(x - t_l)^3 = c_0(x) + \sum_{l=1}^h \gamma_l(x - t_l)_+^3 ,
\]
since the terms with $t_l \ge x$ are zero. This has the stated form, with $c_0$ supplying the $\beta$'s.
\end{proof}

So once the knots are chosen, a spline is fitted by ordinary least squares on the $h + 4$ columns
$1, x, x^2, x^3, (x - t_1)_+^3, \dots, (x - t_h)_+^3$, and the extra-sum-of-squares test of $\gamma_1 = \dots = \gamma_h = 0$ asks whether a
single cubic would do. The coefficient $\gamma_i$ is the jump in $s'''/6$ at $t_i$ (Problem~\ref{prob:spline-jump}). Some
practical points:
\begin{itemize}
\item Use few knots: three to five are usually ample. Put them where the curve is expected to change its
behaviour, or, lacking that knowledge, at quantiles of the $x$ values so that each piece has about the same
number of observations.
\item If the curve may jump at $t_i$, add $(x - t_i)_+^0$ (the indicator of $x > t_i$); for a jump in slope only, add $(x - t_i)_+$; and so
on. The spline then has fewer continuity conditions.
\item The truncated power columns are badly conditioned when there are many knots. Software uses an
equivalent basis, the \emph{B-splines}, which spans the same space (so gives the same fit) with much better
conditioning.
\item When the knot positions themselves must be estimated, the model is non-linear in them.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
