first and second derivatives. It has $2 + 4 = 6$ parameters.}
\label{fig:spline}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Polynomial models in two or more variables}
With two regressors, the \textbf{second-order model} is
\[
y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_1 + \beta_2x_2 + \beta_{11}x_1^2 + \beta_{22}x_2^2 + \beta_{12}x_1x_2 + \varepsilon ,
\]
and with $k$ regressors it has the $k$ linear terms, the $k$ pure quadratic terms and the $k(k - 1)/2$ cross-products. The
cross-product $x_1x_2$ is an interaction, as in Section~9: the effect of $x_1$ depends on the level of $x_2$. Such a model
describes a curved \emph{response surface}, and it is the basis of \emph{response surface methodology}, in which
an industrial process is moved towards the settings of its variables that give the best response. The
variables are usually coded, $x_j = (\xi_j - c_j)/d_j$ for natural variable $\xi_j$, so that the design points are at $\pm 1$ and
$0$.

In matrix form the fitted second-order model is
\[
\hat y = \hat\beta_0 + \mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{b} + \mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{B}\mathbf{x} ,
\]
where $\mathbf{b} = (\hat\beta_1, \dots, \hat\beta_k)^\top$ and $\mathbf{B}$ is the symmetric $k \times k$ matrix with $\hat\beta_{jj}$ on the diagonal and $\frac12\hat\beta_{jl}$
in positions $(j, l)$ and $(l, j)$. (For $k = 2$, $\mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{Bx} = \hat\beta_{11}x_1^2 + \hat\beta_{22}x_2^2 + \hat\beta_{12}x_1x_2$.)

\begin{thm}[Stationary point and canonical form]\label{thm:canonical}
If $\mathbf{B}$ is non-singular, the fitted surface has a single stationary point,
\[
\mathbf{x}_s = -\tfrac12\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{b} , \qquad \hat y_s = \hat\beta_0 + \tfrac12\mathbf{x}_s^\top\mathbf{b} .
\]
Let $\mathbf{B} = \mathbf{P}\boldsymbol\Lambda\mathbf{P}^\top$ with $\mathbf{P}$ orthogonal and $\boldsymbol\Lambda = \operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k)$, and let
$\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{P}^\top(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_s)$. Then
\[
\hat y = \hat y_s + \lambda_1w_1^2 + \dots + \lambda_kw_k^2 .
\]
The stationary point is a maximum if every $\lambda_i < 0$, a minimum if every $\lambda_i > 0$, and a saddle point if the $\lambda_i$
have both signs.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The gradient of $\hat y$ is $\mathbf{b} + 2\mathbf{Bx}$, which is zero only at $\mathbf{x}_s$. Write $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_s + \mathbf{z}$. Then
\[
\hat y = \hat\beta_0 + \mathbf{x}_s^\top\mathbf{b} + \mathbf{x}_s^\top\mathbf{Bx}_s + \mathbf{z}^\top(\mathbf{b} + 2\mathbf{Bx}_s) + \mathbf{z}^\top\mathbf{Bz} .
\]
The middle term is zero, and $\mathbf{Bx}_s = -\frac12\mathbf{b}$ turns the constant into
$\hat\beta_0 + \mathbf{x}_s^\top\mathbf{b} - \frac12\mathbf{x}_s^\top\mathbf{b} = \hat y_s$. With $\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{P}^\top\mathbf{z}$, so $\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{Pw}$,
$\mathbf{z}^\top\mathbf{Bz} = \mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{P}^\top\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol\Lambda\mathbf{P}^\top\mathbf{P}\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{w}^\top\boldsymbol\Lambda\mathbf{w} = \sum\lambda_iw_i^2$. If every $\lambda_i < 0$, then
$\hat y < \hat y_s$ for every $\mathbf{w} \ne \mathbf{0}$, a maximum; similarly for a minimum. If $\lambda_1 > 0 > \lambda_2$, then $\hat y$ rises from $\hat y_s$
along the $w_1$ axis and falls along the $w_2$ axis.
\end{proof}

The $w_i$ are the \emph{canonical variables}: new axes, centred at $\mathbf{x}_s$ and along the eigenvectors of $\mathbf{B}$. The
eigenvalues say how fast the response changes along each axis. A $\lambda_i$ near zero means the surface is nearly
flat along $w_i$, a \emph{ridge}: many combinations of the variables give almost the best response, which in
practice is useful, since the cheapest of them can be chosen. If the stationary point lies far outside the
region of the data, it is an extrapolation and should not be trusted (consideration~(3)). Figure~\ref{fig:canonical}
shows the main shapes.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\col/\num/\name/\eqn in {
 0/blue/1/{Maximum}/{$\lambda_1, \lambda_2 < 0$},
 4.0/red/2/{Saddle}/{$\lambda_1 > 0 > \lambda_2$},
 8.0/green/3/{Ridge}/{$\lambda_2 < 0$, $\lambda_1 \approx 0$},
 12.0/orange/4/{Rising ridge}/{$\lambda_1 \approx 0$, $\mathbf{x}_s$ remote}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,0)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-0.25,-0.95) rectangle (3.65,3.85);
  \node[circle,fill=\col!60!black,text=white,inner sep=1.5pt,font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (0.05,3.5) {\num};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.3,3.5) {\name};
  \draw[gray!70] (0,0) rectangle (3.4,3.1);
  \begin{scope}
   \clip (0,0) rectangle (3.4,3.1);
   \ifnum\num=1
    \foreach \r in {0.35,0.7,1.05,1.4}
     \draw[blue!60!black,rotate around={45:(1.7,1.55)}] (1.7,1.55) ellipse ({2*\r} and {\r});
    \fill[blue!60!black] (1.7,1.55) circle (1.8pt);
   \fi
   \ifnum\num=2
    \foreach \a in {0.35,0.75,1.15} {
     \draw[red!60!black,rotate around={45:(1.7,1.55)}] plot[domain=-1.6:1.6,samples=25] ({1.7+\a*cosh(\x)},{1.55+\a*sinh(\x)});
     \draw[red!60!black,rotate around={45:(1.7,1.55)}] plot[domain=-1.6:1.6,samples=25] ({1.7-\a*cosh(\x)},{1.55+\a*sinh(\x)});
     \draw[red!60!black,dashed,rotate around={45:(1.7,1.55)}] plot[domain=-1.6:1.6,samples=25] ({1.7+\a*sinh(\x)},{1.55+\a*cosh(\x)});
     \draw[red!60!black,dashed,rotate around={45:(1.7,1.55)}] plot[domain=-1.6:1.6,samples=25] ({1.7+\a*sinh(\x)},{1.55-\a*cosh(\x)});
    }
    \fill[red!60!black] (1.7,1.55) circle (1.8pt);
   \fi
   \ifnum\num=3
    \foreach \d in {-1.2,-0.8,-0.4,0.4,0.8,1.2}
     \draw[green!50!black,rotate around={45:(1.7,1.55)}] (-1,{1.55+\d}) -- (4.4,{1.55+\d});
    \draw[green!50!black,very thick,rotate around={45:(1.7,1.55)}] (-1,1.55) -- (4.4,1.55);
   \fi
   \ifnum\num=4
    \foreach \c in {-1.6,-0.8,0,0.8,1.6}
     \draw[orange!70!black] plot[domain=-2:2,samples=25] ({1.7+\x},{0.4+0.5*\c+0.55*\x*\x});
    \draw[-Stealth,thick,orange!70!black] (1.7,0.2) -- (1.7,2.9);
   \fi
  \end{scope}
  \node[font=\scriptsize,text=\col!50!black] at (1.7,-0.5) {\eqn};
 \end{scope}
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Contours of second-order surfaces in two coded variables. (1) A maximum, elongated along the
axis with the smaller $|\lambda_i|$. (2) A saddle; solid and dashed contours lie above and below $\hat y_s$. (3) A
stationary ridge: the response is almost constant along the thick line. (4) A rising ridge: the response
keeps increasing in the direction of the arrow, and the stationary point lies far outside the region.}
\label{fig:canonical}
\end{figure}

\begin{exa}\label{exa:process}
The yield $y$ (\%) of a chemical process was measured at the nine combinations of three temperatures and
three reaction times, coded as $x_1 = (\text{temperature} - 150)/10$ in $^\circ$C and $x_2 = (\text{time} - 60)/15$ in minutes, so
that each coded variable takes the values $-1, 0, 1$. The yields were:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|ccc}
& $x_2 = -1$ & $x_2 = 0$ & $x_2 = 1$ \\
\hline
$x_1 = -1$ & 72 & 74 & 63 \\
$x_1 = 0$ & 72 & 80 & 78 \\
$x_1 = 1$ & 65 & 76 & 80 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit the second-order model, test it, and find and describe the stationary point.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{Fitting.} On this $3 \times 3$ grid, replace $x_j^2$ by $q_j = 3x_j^2 - 2$, which takes the values $1, -2, 1$ at
$x_j = -1, 0, 1$ (the quadratic orthogonal polynomial for three levels). Every one of the columns
$\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{x}_1, \mathbf{x}_2, \mathbf{x}_1\mathbf{x}_2, \mathbf{q}_1, \mathbf{q}_2$ sums to zero except the first, and every product of two of them
sums to zero over the grid. For example, $\sum q_1q_2 = (1 - 2 + 1)^2 = 0$, and $\sum x_1q_1 = 3(-1 + 0 + 1) = 0$. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:orthogonal-columns}, each coefficient is a ratio of sums. The row totals are $209, 230, 221$ and the
column totals are $209, 230, 221$, so
\begin{gather*}
\textstyle\sum x_1y = 221 - 209 = 12, \qquad \sum x_2y = 221 - 209 = 12, \\
\textstyle\sum x_1x_2y = (80 + 72) - (63 + 65) = 24, \\
\textstyle\sum q_1y = \sum q_2y = 209 + 221 - 2(230) = -30 .
\end{gather*}
The sums of squares of the columns are $6, 6, 4, 18, 18$, so
\[
\hat\beta_1 = \frac{12}{6} = 2, \quad \hat\beta_2 = 2, \quad \hat\beta_{12} = \frac{24}{4} = 6 ,
\]
and the coefficient of each $q_j$ is $-30/18 = -\frac53$.
Since $q_j = 3x_j^2 - 2$, a coefficient $-\frac53$ on $q_j$ is $\hat\beta_{jj} = 3(-\frac53) = -5$ on $x_j^2$, plus a constant $(-\frac53)(-2) = \frac{10}{3}$. With
$\bar y = 660/9 = 73.333$, the intercept is $\hat\beta_0 = 73.333 + \frac{10}{3} + \frac{10}{3} = 80$, and
\[
\hat y = 80 + 2x_1 + 2x_2 - 5x_1^2 - 5x_2^2 + 6x_1x_2 .
\]

\emph{Testing.} The fitted values are $72, 73, 64$; $73, 80, 77$; $64, 77, 80$ (row by row), so the residuals are
$0, 1, -1$; $-1, 0, 1$; $1, -1, 0$, and $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 6$ on $9 - 6 = 3$ degrees of freedom: $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 2$. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:orthogonal-columns}(iii),
\begin{align*}
SS_R &= \frac{12^2}{6} + \frac{12^2}{6} + \frac{24^2}{4} + \frac{30^2}{18} + \frac{30^2}{18} \\
&= 24 + 24 + 144 + 50 + 50 = 292
\end{align*}
on $5$ degrees of freedom, so $F_0 = (292/5)/2 = 29.2 > F_{0.05,5,3} = 9.01$. The standard errors are $\sqrt{2/6} = 0.577$ for
$\hat\beta_1, \hat\beta_2$, $\sqrt{2/4} = 0.707$ for $\hat\beta_{12}$, and $3\sqrt{2/18} = 1$ for $\hat\beta_{11}, \hat\beta_{22}$, giving $t$ values $3.46$, $3.46$, $8.49$, $-5$
and $-5$, all beyond $t_{0.025,3} = 3.182$. Every term is needed.

\emph{The stationary point.} Here $\mathbf{b} = (2, 2)^\top$ and
\[
\mathbf{B} = \begin{pmatrix} -5 & 3 \\ 3 & -5 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad
\mathbf{B}^{-1} = \frac{1}{16}\begin{pmatrix} -5 & -3 \\ -3 & -5 \end{pmatrix} .
\]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:canonical},
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{x}_s &= -\frac12\cdot\frac{1}{16}\begin{pmatrix} -16 \\ -16 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.5 \\ 0.5 \end{pmatrix} , \\
\hat y_s &= 80 + \tfrac12(0.5 \times 2 + 0.5 \times 2) = 81 .
\end{align*}
As a check, $\hat y$ at $(0.5, 0.5)$ is $80 + 1 + 1 - 1.25 - 1.25 + 1.5 = 81$. The eigenvalues of $\mathbf{B}$ are $-5 + 3 = -2$, with
eigenvector $(1, 1)/\sqrt2$, and $-5 - 3 = -8$, with eigenvector $(1, -1)/\sqrt2$. So
\[
\hat y = 81 - 2w_1^2 - 8w_2^2 ,
\]
where
\[
w_1 = \frac{(x_1 - 0.5) + (x_2 - 0.5)}{\sqrt2}, \qquad w_2 = \frac{(x_1 - 0.5) - (x_2 - 0.5)}{\sqrt2} .
\]
Both eigenvalues are negative, so the stationary point is a maximum, inside the region of the data: a
temperature of $150 + 0.5(10) = 155\,^\circ$C and a time of $60 + 0.5(15) = 67.5$ minutes, with predicted yield $81\%$. The
contours are ellipses with their long axis along $w_1$, the direction in which temperature and time rise
together. The yield falls four times as fast along $w_2$ as along $w_1$, so if the process must be run at a
different setting, it should be moved along the diagonal, raising or lowering temperature and time
together, not one without the other.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Nonparametric regression}
A polynomial imposes one global form on the whole range of $x$. Nonparametric regression drops the global
form: it estimates the mean at each point $x_0$ from the observations near $x_0$ only. Let $K$ be a
\emph{kernel}, a non-negative function that is largest at $0$ and decreases away from it, and let $h > 0$ be the
\emph{bandwidth}. The weight of observation $i$ at $x_0$ is
\[
w_i(x_0) = K\Bigl(\frac{x_i - x_0}{h}\Bigr) ,
\]
and the \textbf{kernel estimate} (the Nadaraya--Watson estimate) of the mean at $x_0$ is the weighted average
\[
\tilde y(x_0) = \frac{\sum_i w_i(x_0)\,y_i}{\sum_i w_i(x_0)} .
\]
A common choice is the triangular kernel $K(t) = \max(1 - |t|, 0)$, which gives weight only to the observations
within $h$ of $x_0$.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:local-fit}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The kernel estimate $\tilde y(x_0)$ is the weighted least squares estimate of a constant: it minimises
$\sum_i w_i(x_0)(y_i - a)^2$ over $a$.
\item The \emph{local linear} estimate, $\tilde y(x_0) = \hat a$ where $(\hat a, \hat b)$ minimises
$\sum_i w_i(x_0)\bigl(y_i - a - b(x_i - x_0)\bigr)^2$, reproduces any straight line exactly: if $y_i = c + dx_i$ for every $i$,
then $\tilde y(x_0) = c + dx_0$. The kernel estimate does not, in general.
\item Both estimates are linear in $\mathbf{y}$: the vector of estimates at the observed $x_i$ is $\tilde{\mathbf{y}} = \mathbf{Sy}$ for a
matrix $\mathbf{S}$ that depends only on the $x_i$, $K$ and $h$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(i) The derivative of $\sum w_i(y_i - a)^2$ is $-2\sum w_i(y_i - a)$, zero at $a = \sum w_iy_i/\sum w_i$, and the second derivative
$2\sum w_i$ is positive.

(ii) The local linear estimate is weighted least squares for the model with columns $\mathbf{1}$ and $\mathbf{x} - x_0\mathbf{1}$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:wls}), with $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{Wy}$. If $\mathbf{y} = (c + dx_0)\mathbf{1} + d(\mathbf{x} - x_0\mathbf{1})$,
then $\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\theta$ with $\boldsymbol\theta = (c + dx_0, d)^\top$, so $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX}\boldsymbol\theta = \boldsymbol\theta$, and $\hat a = c + dx_0$.
For the kernel estimate, take $x_0$ at the smallest $x_i$ and $d > 0$: every observation with positive weight has
$y_i \ge c + dx_0$, with strict inequality for those to the right of $x_0$, so the weighted average exceeds $c + dx_0$ as soon
as any of them has positive weight.

(iii) In both cases $\tilde y(x_0)$ is a fixed linear combination of the $y_i$: $\sum_i w_i(x_0)y_i/\sum_i w_i(x_0)$, or the first
row of $(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}$ applied to $\mathbf{y}$. Stacking these rows for $x_0 = x_1, \dots, x_n$ gives $\mathbf{S}$.
\end{proof}

The matrix $\mathbf{S}$ plays the part of the hat matrix $\mathbf{H}$. For a linear model, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:trace},
\[
\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{H} = \operatorname{tr}\bigl((\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}\bigr) = \operatorname{tr}\mathbf{I}_p = p ,
\]
the number of parameters, so $\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}$ is called the \emph{effective number of parameters} of the smoother. The
bandwidth controls the trade-off: a small $h$ follows the data closely (small bias, large variance, large
$\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}$), a large $h$ averages over many points (large bias at peaks and troughs, small variance). Part (ii)
explains why local linear fitting is preferred near the ends of the data, where the kernel estimate is
pulled towards the interior. Fitting a local line with tricube weights, and down-weighting
observations with large residuals, is the \emph{loess} method used by most software. Nonparametric fits are
useful for exploring the shape of a relationship and for checking a parametric model, but they give no
formula, and they are unreliable near the ends of the data and wherever the data are sparse.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:irrigation-kernel}
For the irrigation data of Example~\ref{exa:irrigation}, use the triangular kernel with $h = 2$ to estimate the
mean yield at $x_0 = 4$ and at $x_0 = 1$, by the kernel and the local linear methods, and find
$\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}$ for the kernel estimate.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
At $x_0 = 4$ the weights $1 - |x_i - 4|/2$ are $\frac12, 1, \frac12$ at $x = 3, 4, 5$ and zero elsewhere, so
\[
\tilde y(4) = \frac{\frac12(23) + 26 + \frac12(27)}{2} = 25.5 .
\]
The weights are symmetric about $x_0$, so $\sum w_i(x_i - x_0) = 0$; the local linear normal equations then decouple, and
the local linear estimate is also $25.5$. The quadratic fit gave $26.14$ here: averaging across the peak of a concave curve
pulls the smooth estimate down, the bias of a large bandwidth.

At $x_0 = 1$ the weights are $1$ at $x = 1$ and $\frac12$ at $x = 2$. The kernel estimate is
\[
\tilde y(1) = \frac{8 + \frac12(16)}{1.5} = 10.67 ,
\]
well above the observation $8$ and the quadratic fit $8.14$, because every neighbour lies to the right, uphill. The local
linear fit uses the same two points; a line through two points fits them exactly, so $\tilde y(1) = 8$, with local
slope $8$. This is Theorem~\ref{thm:local-fit}(ii) at work.

For $\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}$ we need the weight of $y_i$ in its own estimate. At $x_0 = 1$ and $x_0 = 7$ it is $1/1.5 = \frac23$; at each
interior point the weights are $\frac12, 1, \frac12$ with total $2$, so it is $\frac12$. Then
\[
\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S} = 2\Bigl(\frac23\Bigr) + 5\Bigl(\frac12\Bigr) = 3.83 ,
\]
a little more than the three parameters of the quadratic.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:hierarchy}
Consider the model $y = \beta_0 + \beta_2x^2 + \varepsilon$, without a linear term.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Rewrite it in terms of $z = x - c$, and show that it becomes a quadratic in $z$ whose coefficients satisfy a
constraint.
\item Explain why testing $H_0\colon \beta_1 = 0$ in the full quadratic $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x + \beta_2x^2 + \varepsilon$ is usually not a sensible
way to simplify the model.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) With $x = z + c$, $\beta_0 + \beta_2(z + c)^2 = (\beta_0 + \beta_2c^2) + 2\beta_2c\,z + \beta_2z^2$. This is $\gamma_0 + \gamma_1z + \gamma_2z^2$ with the
constraint $\gamma_1 = 2c\gamma_2$: the turning point is forced to be at $z = -\gamma_1/(2\gamma_2) = -c$, that is, at $x = 0$. Dropping the
linear term is the statement that the curve turns exactly at the origin of $x$, which is a property of the
coding, not of the data, unless $x = 0$ has a special meaning.

(b) In the full quadratic, $\beta_1 + 2\beta_2x$ is the slope, so $\beta_1$ is the slope at $x = 0$. Whether that is zero depends on
where the origin is: by Theorem~\ref{thm:poly-invariance}, changing the origin changes $\beta_1$ but not the fit. A test of
$\beta_1 = 0$ asks whether the turning point is at $x = 0$, which is seldom a question of interest. The hierarchy
principle keeps all lower-order terms.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:orth5}
For $x = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5$ and $y = 1, 5, 8, 7, 6$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item find the orthogonal polynomials $P_1$ and $P_2$ (scaled to integer values);
\item fit the quadratic by orthogonal polynomials, give the analysis of variance, and test the quadratic term;
\item write the fitted model in terms of $x$ and find the turning point.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) Here $n = 5$, $u = x - 3$ takes the values $-2, \dots, 2$, and $(n^2 - 1)/12 = 2$, so $P_1 = u = -2, -1, 0, 1, 2$ and
$P_2 = u^2 - 2 = 2, -1, -2, -1, 2$, with $\sum P_1^2 = 10$ and $\sum P_2^2 = 14$.

(b) $\sum P_1y = -2 - 5 + 0 + 7 + 12 = 12$ and $\sum P_2y = 2 - 5 - 16 - 7 + 12 = -14$, so $\hat\alpha_1 = 1.2$ and $\hat\alpha_2 = -1$, and
$\bar y = 27/5 = 5.4$. Then $SS(\alpha_1) = 144/10 = 14.4$ and $SS(\alpha_2) = 196/14 = 14$. With $\sum y^2 = 175$,
$SS_T = 175 - 27^2/5 = 29.2$, so $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 29.2 - 14.4 - 14 = 0.8$ on $2$ degrees of freedom, $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 0.4$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
Source & SS & df & MS & $F_0$ \\
\hline
Linear & 14.4 & 1 & 14.4 & 36 \\
Quadratic & 14.0 & 1 & 14.0 & 35 \\
Residual & 0.8 & 2 & 0.4 & \\
\hline
Total & 29.2 & 4 & & \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$F_0 = 35 > F_{0.05,1,2} = 18.51$, so the quadratic term is significant.

(c) $\hat y = 5.4 + 1.2u - (u^2 - 2) = 7.4 + 1.2u - u^2$, and with $u = x - 3$,
$\hat y = 7.4 + 1.2x - 3.6 - (x^2 - 6x + 9) = -5.2 + 7.2x - x^2$. The turning point is at $u = 0.6$, $x = 3.6$, where
$\hat y = 7.4 + 0.72 - 0.36 = 7.76$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:even-odd}
Suppose the $x$ values are symmetric about $\bar x$, and fit the cubic $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1u + \beta_2u^2 + \beta_3u^3 + \varepsilon$ with
$u = x - \bar x$. Show that, after reordering the columns as $(\mathbf{1}, \mathbf{u}^2 \mid \mathbf{u}, \mathbf{u}^3)$, $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}$ is block diagonal, and
deduce that adding $u^3$ to the centred quadratic changes $\hat\beta_1$ but not $\hat\beta_0$ or $\hat\beta_2$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The entries of $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}$ are the sums $\sum u_i^{a + b}$ for columns $u^a$ and $u^b$. An even column ($a = 0, 2$) with an odd
column ($b = 1, 3$) has $a + b$ odd, and the sum is zero by Theorem~\ref{thm:centred-poly}. So
$\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X} = \operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{even}}, \mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{odd}})$, and its inverse is block diagonal too. The normal equations
split into two separate systems: $(\hat\beta_0, \hat\beta_2)$ from the even columns alone and $(\hat\beta_1, \hat\beta_3)$ from the odd columns
alone. Adding $u^3$ enlarges only the odd system, so $\hat\beta_0$ and $\hat\beta_2$ are unchanged, while $\hat\beta_1$ changes because
$\sum u^4 \ne 0$ couples $\mathbf{u}$ and $\mathbf{u}^3$. For the irrigation data, adding $\hat\alpha_3P_3 = \frac16(u^3 - 7u)/6$ to the
quadratic of Example~\ref{exa:irrigation-orth} gives the cubic $\hat y = 26.143 + 2.806u - u^2 + 0.0278u^3$: the even
coefficients $26.143$ and $-1$ are unchanged, and $\hat\beta_1$ moves from $3$ to $3 - 7/36 = 2.806$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:saddle}
A fitted second-order model in two coded variables is
\[
\hat y = 50 + 2x_1 - 2x_2 + x_1^2 + x_2^2 + 4x_1x_2 .
\]
Find the stationary point, write the surface in canonical form, and describe it.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Here $\mathbf{b} = (2, -2)^\top$ and $\mathbf{B} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, with $\det\mathbf{B} = -3$ and
$\mathbf{B}^{-1} = -\frac13\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -2 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$. Then $\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{b} = -\frac13(2 + 4, -4 - 2)^\top = (-2, 2)^\top$, so
$\mathbf{x}_s = -\frac12(-2, 2)^\top = (1, -1)^\top$ and $\hat y_s = 50 + \frac12(2 + 2) = 52$. The eigenvalues of $\mathbf{B}$ are $1 + 2 = 3$, with
eigenvector $(1, 1)/\sqrt2$, and $1 - 2 = -1$, with eigenvector $(1, -1)/\sqrt2$, so
\[
\hat y = 52 + 3w_1^2 - w_2^2 .
\]
The eigenvalues have opposite signs: $(1, -1)$ is a saddle point, neither a maximum nor a minimum. The
response increases fastest away from it along $w_1$, the direction in which $x_1$ and $x_2$ change together, and
decreases along $w_2$. To increase the response, move along $w_1$, keeping in mind that the model is only
trustworthy inside the region of the data.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:spline-jump}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item In the truncated power representation of Theorem~\ref{thm:spline-basis}, show that $s'''$ jumps by $6\gamma_i$ at $t_i$.
\item Count the parameters of a cubic spline with $h$ knots in another way: start from $h + 1$ unrelated cubics and
subtract the number of continuity conditions.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) The terms $\beta_0 + \dots + \beta_3x^3$ have constant third derivative $6\beta_3$, and $(x - t_l)_+^3$ has third derivative
$6$ for $x > t_l$ and $0$ for $x < t_l$. So $s'''(x) = 6\beta_3 + 6\sum_{t_l < x}\gamma_l$, a step function that rises by $6\gamma_i$ as $x$ crosses $t_i$.

(b) $h + 1$ separate cubics have $4(h + 1)$ parameters. At each of the $h$ knots, the value and the first and second
derivatives must match: $3h$ conditions. That leaves $4(h + 1) - 3h = h + 4$, in agreement with the theorem.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:moving-average}
With the \emph{uniform} kernel, $K(t) = 1$ for $|t| \le 1$ and $0$ otherwise, and $h = 1$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item show that for equally spaced $x$ with unit spacing, the kernel estimate at an interior $x_i$ is the moving
average $(y_{i-1} + y_i + y_{i+1})/3$;
\item compute the estimates at $x = 1, 4, 7$ for the irrigation data and $\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}$;
\item compare $\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}$ with the triangular kernel with $h = 2$ (Example~\ref{exa:irrigation-kernel}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) The observations with $|x_j - x_i| \le 1$ are $x_{i-1}, x_i, x_{i+1}$, each with weight $1$, so the weighted average is their
plain average.

(b) $\tilde y(4) = (23 + 26 + 27)/3 = 25.33$. At the ends only two observations are within $1$:
$\tilde y(1) = (8 + 16)/2 = 12$ and $\tilde y(7) = (29 + 26)/2 = 27.5$. The weight of $y_i$ in its own estimate is $\frac12$ at the two
ends and $\frac13$ at the five interior points, so $\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S} = 2(\frac12) + 5(\frac13) = 2.67$.

(c) Both kernels reach the same neighbours, but the uniform kernel gives them full weight, so it smooths more:
$2.67$ effective parameters against $3.83$. The end estimate $12$ is even further from $y_1 = 8$ than the triangular
kernel's $10.67$.
\end{solution}

\section{Generalized linear models}
Every model so far has had a response that is, at least approximately, normal with constant variance.
Many responses are not. Whether an insect dies, whether a borrower defaults, whether a patient recovers:
these are \emph{binary} responses, $0$ or $1$. The number of faults on a machine, of accidents at a junction,
of customers in an hour: these are \emph{counts}. For such responses the mean is restricted (a probability
lies between $0$ and $1$, a mean count is positive), and the variance depends on the mean. A
\textbf{generalized linear model} (GLM) keeps the linear predictor $\mathbf{x}^\top\boldsymbol\beta$ of regression but
connects it to the mean through a \emph{link function}, and replaces the normal distribution by one suited to
the response. This section treats the two most used cases, logistic regression for binary data and
Poisson regression for counts, and then the general theory that covers both, and the normal linear model
as well.

\subsection{Binary responses and the logistic model}
Let $y_i = 1$ if observation $i$ is a ``success'' and $0$ otherwise, with $P(y_i = 1) = \pi_i$. Then
$E(y_i) = \pi_i$ and $\operatorname{Var}(y_i) = \pi_i(1 - \pi_i)$. Fitting $y_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_i + \varepsilon_i$ by least squares, the
\emph{linear probability model}, runs into three problems, shown in Figure~\ref{fig:why-logistic}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item the fitted line leaves $[0, 1]$, giving impossible ``probabilities'';
\item the variance $\pi_i(1 - \pi_i)$ changes with the mean, so the constant-variance assumption fails;
\item the errors take only two values for each $x$, so they are far from normal.
\end{enumerate}
The \textbf{logistic regression model} avoids the first two problems by modelling a transformation of $\pi$:
\[
\pi = \frac{e^{\eta}}{1 + e^{\eta}} = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\eta}}, \qquad \eta = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_1 + \dots + \beta_kx_k = \mathbf{x}^\top\boldsymbol\beta .
\]
Solving for $\eta$,
\[
\eta = \log\frac{\pi}{1 - \pi} ,
\]
the \textbf{logit} or \emph{log-odds} of $\pi$. Whatever the value of $\eta$, $\pi$ lies strictly between $0$ and $1$, and as $\eta$
increases, $\pi$ traces an S-shaped curve. The variance is dealt with by fitting by maximum likelihood, which
uses the binomial distribution directly, instead of least squares.

\subhead{Odds and odds ratios}
The \emph{odds} of success are $\pi/(1 - \pi)$: odds of $3$ mean success is three times as likely as failure, $\pi = 0.75$.
The logistic model says that the log-odds are linear in the regressors, so when $x_j$ increases by one unit and
the others are held fixed, the log-odds increase by $\beta_j$ and the odds are \emph{multiplied} by
\[
\frac{\text{odds at } x_j + 1}{\text{odds at } x_j} = \frac{e^{\eta + \beta_j}}{e^{\eta}} = e^{\beta_j} ,
\]
the \textbf{odds ratio}. It is the same at every value of $x_j$, which is what makes it the natural summary of a
logistic regression. An odds ratio above $1$ means $x_j$ raises the probability of success; below $1$, lowers it.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\col/\num/\name/\eqn in {
 0/red/1/{Straight line}/{$\hat\pi = -0.1 + 0.2x$},
 5.1/orange/2/{Variance}/{$\operatorname{Var}(y) = \pi(1 - \pi)$},
 10.2/blue/3/{Logistic}/{$\operatorname{logit}\hat\pi = -3.04 + 1.01x$}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,0)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-0.4,-1.2) rectangle (4.5,3.95);
  \node[circle,fill=\col!60!black,text=white,inner sep=1.5pt,font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (-0.05,3.6) {\num};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.2,3.6) {\name};
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (4.2,0);
  \draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (0,2.95);
  \ifnum\num=2
   \draw[-,thick,orange!70!black] plot[domain=0:1,samples=40] ({3.8*\x},{0.3+9.2*\x*(1-\x)});
   \draw[-,dashed,gray] (1.9,0) -- (1.9,2.6);

   \node[font=\scriptsize,anchor=north] at (1.9,0) {$0.5$};
   \node[font=\scriptsize,anchor=north] at (3.8,0) {$1$};

  \else
   \draw[-,dashed,gray] (0,0.4) -- (4.0,0.4);

   \node[font=\scriptsize,anchor=east] at (0,2.6) {$1$};

  \fi

\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Why binary data need a different model. (1) Least squares on the insecticide data of
Example~\ref{exa:dose} gives a line that predicts $-0.1$ at dose $0$ and $1.1$ at dose $6$. (2) The variance of a binary
response depends on its mean. (3) The fitted logistic curve stays between $0$ and $1$.}
\label{fig:why-logistic}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Maximum likelihood estimation}
Data are often \emph{grouped}: at the $i$th setting $\mathbf{x}_i$ of the regressors there are $m_i$ trials, of which
$y_i$ are successes, so $y_i \sim \text{Bin}(m_i, \pi_i)$, $i = 1, \dots, n$, independently. (Ungrouped binary data are the case
$m_i = 1$.) The log-likelihood is, apart from a constant $\sum\log\binom{m_i}{y_i}$ that does not involve $\boldsymbol\beta$,
\begin{align*}
\ell(\boldsymbol\beta) &= \sum_{i=1}^n\bigl[y_i\log\pi_i + (m_i - y_i)\log(1 - \pi_i)\bigr] \\
&= \sum_{i=1}^n\Bigl[y_i\log\frac{\pi_i}{1 - \pi_i} + m_i\log(1 - \pi_i)\Bigr] .
\end{align*}
With $\eta_i = \log\{\pi_i/(1 - \pi_i)\} = \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta$ and $1 - \pi_i = 1/(1 + e^{\eta_i})$, this is
\[
\ell(\boldsymbol\beta) = \sum_{i=1}^n\bigl[y_i\eta_i - m_i\log(1 + e^{\eta_i})\bigr] .
\]

\begin{thm}\label{thm:logistic-score}
Let $\boldsymbol\mu$ have components $\mu_i = m_i\pi_i$, and let $\mathbf{W} = \operatorname{diag}(w_i)$ with $w_i = m_i\pi_i(1 - \pi_i)$. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the gradient (the \emph{score}) of $\ell$ is $\mathbf{U}(\boldsymbol\beta) = \mathbf{X}^\top(\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol\mu)$, so the maximum-likelihood estimate
satisfies the \emph{likelihood equations} $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\mu}$;
\item the matrix of second derivatives is $-\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX}$, which does not involve $\mathbf{y}$;
\item if $\mathbf{X}$ has full column rank, $\ell$ is strictly concave, so it has at most one maximum, and any solution
of the likelihood equations is the maximum-likelihood estimate.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(i) $\dfrac{d}{d\eta}\log(1 + e^{\eta}) = \dfrac{e^{\eta}}{1 + e^{\eta}} = \pi$, so $\partial\ell/\partial\eta_i = y_i - m_i\pi_i = y_i - \mu_i$, and since
$\partial\eta_i/\partial\beta_j = x_{ij}$, the chain rule gives $\partial\ell/\partial\beta_j = \sum_i x_{ij}(y_i - \mu_i)$.

(ii) $d\pi/d\eta = e^{\eta}/(1 + e^{\eta})^2 = \pi(1 - \pi)$, so $\partial\mu_i/\partial\beta_l = m_i\pi_i(1 - \pi_i)x_{il} = w_ix_{il}$, and
$\partial^2\ell/\partial\beta_j\partial\beta_l = -\sum_i x_{ij}w_ix_{il}$, the $(j, l)$ element of $-\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX}$.

(iii) For $\mathbf{v} \ne \mathbf{0}$, $\mathbf{v}^\top\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WXv} = \sum_i w_i(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\mathbf{v})^2 > 0$, because every $w_i > 0$ and $\mathbf{Xv} \ne \mathbf{0}$. So the
second-derivative matrix is negative definite everywhere, and $\ell$ is strictly concave: along any line it is
a function with negative second derivative, so a point with zero gradient is the unique maximum.
\end{proof}

When the model contains an intercept, the first likelihood equation reads $\sum y_i = \sum\hat\mu_i$: the fitted
number of successes equals the observed number, as the sum of the residuals is zero in least squares.

The likelihood equations are non-linear in $\boldsymbol\beta$, and in general they must be solved by iteration.
Newton's method turns out to be a sequence of weighted least squares fits.

\begin{thm}[Iteratively reweighted least squares]\label{thm:irls-logistic}
Newton's method for maximising $\ell$, started from $\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)}$, gives
\[
\boldsymbol\beta^{(t+1)} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{Wz} ,
\qquad z_i = \eta_i + \frac{y_i - \mu_i}{w_i} ,
\]
where $\eta_i$, $\mu_i$, $w_i$ are evaluated at $\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)}$. That is, each step is the weighted least squares
regression of the \emph{working response} $\mathbf{z}$ on $\mathbf{X}$ with weights $w_i$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Newton's method maximises the second-order Taylor approximation of $\ell$ at
$\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)}$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:logistic-score} this gives
\[
\boldsymbol\beta^{(t+1)} = \boldsymbol\beta^{(t)} + (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{U} .
\]
Since
$\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)} = \boldsymbol\eta$ and $\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol\mu = \mathbf{W}(\mathbf{z} - \boldsymbol\eta)$,
\begin{align*}
\boldsymbol\beta^{(t+1)} &= (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\bigl[\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX}\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)} + \mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{z} - \boldsymbol\eta)\bigr] \\
&= (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{Wz} .
\end{align*}
This is the weighted least squares estimate of Theorem~\ref{thm:wls} with response $\mathbf{z}$.
\end{proof}

The working response $z_i$ is a linearised version of the logit of $y_i/m_i$, and $w_i = m_i\pi_i(1 - \pi_i)$ is the
reciprocal of its approximate variance, so the weighting is exactly the remedy of Section~6 for unequal
variances, with the weights recomputed as the estimates improve. A common start is $\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf{0}$, which
gives $\pi_i = \frac12$. The iteration usually converges in a handful of steps. It fails only when the
estimate does not exist: if some combination of the regressors separates the successes from the failures
completely, the likelihood keeps increasing as the coefficients go to infinity.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:dose}
Batches of $20$ insects were exposed to five doses of an insecticide, and the numbers killed were:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|ccccc}
Dose, $x$ & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 \\
\hline
Killed, $y$ (of 20) & 2 & 6 & 10 & 14 & 18 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit the logistic regression of the probability of death on dose.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{First step.} At $\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf{0}$, every $\pi_i = \frac12$, $\mu_i = 10$ and $w_i = 20 \times \frac14 = 5$, so
$z_i = 0 + (y_i - 10)/5 = -1.6, -0.8, 0, 0.8, 1.6$. The weights are equal, so the weighted fit is the ordinary straight-line fit
of $z$ on $x$: with $\bar x = 3$ and $\sum(x_i - 3)^2 = 10$,
\begin{align*}
\beta_1^{(1)} &= \frac{\sum(x_i - 3)z_i}{10} = \frac{3.2 + 0.8 + 0 + 0.8 + 3.2}{10} = 0.8 , \\
\beta_0^{(1)} &= \bar z - 0.8\bar x = -2.4 .
\end{align*}

\emph{Further steps.} Recomputing $\pi_i$, $w_i$ and $z_i$ at each new estimate gives:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
Step $t$ & $\beta_0^{(t)}$ & $\beta_1^{(t)}$ & $\ell(\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)})$ \\
\hline
0 & 0 & 0 & $-69.315$ \\
1 & $-2.4000$ & $0.8000$ & $-52.063$ \\
2 & $-2.9666$ & $0.9889$ & $-51.475$ \\
3 & $-3.0351$ & $1.0117$ & $-51.469$ \\
4 & $-3.0360$ & $1.0120$ & $-51.469$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The log-likelihood rises at each step and the estimates settle after four steps:
\[
\operatorname{logit}\hat\pi = -3.036 + 1.012x .
\]
The fitted probabilities are $0.117, 0.267, 0.500, 0.733, 0.883$, so the fitted numbers killed are
$2.33, 5.33, 10.00, 14.67, 17.67$. They add to $50$, the observed total, as Theorem~\ref{thm:logistic-score} requires. The
odds ratio for one unit of dose is $e^{1.012} = 2.75$: each extra unit multiplies the odds of death by $2.75$.
Figure~\ref{fig:why-logistic}, panel~3, shows the fit.
\end{solution}

\subhead{A single indicator: the two-by-two table}
With one indicator regressor, the likelihood equations can be solved exactly.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:logit-indicator}
Suppose $z = 0$ for group $0$ and $z = 1$ for group $1$, with $y_0$ successes in $m_0$ trials in group $0$ and $y_1$ in $m_1$
in group $1$, and fit $\operatorname{logit}\pi = \beta_0 + \beta_1z$. Then $\hat\pi_0 = y_0/m_0$, $\hat\pi_1 = y_1/m_1$,
\[
\hat\beta_0 = \log\frac{y_0}{m_0 - y_0}, \qquad \hat\beta_1 = \log\frac{y_1(m_0 - y_0)}{y_0(m_1 - y_1)} ,
\]
so $e^{\hat\beta_1}$ is the sample odds ratio, and the variance estimate of Theorem~\ref{thm:wald} below is
\[
\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat\beta_1) = \frac{1}{y_0} + \frac{1}{m_0 - y_0} + \frac{1}{y_1} + \frac{1}{m_1 - y_1} .
\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The columns of $\mathbf{X}$ are $\mathbf{1}$ and $\mathbf{z}$, so the likelihood equations say the fitted successes equal the
observed ones in total and in group $1$, hence in each group: $m_0\hat\pi_0 = y_0$ and $m_1\hat\pi_1 = y_1$. Taking logits,
$\hat\beta_0 = \operatorname{logit}\hat\pi_0$ and $\hat\beta_0 + \hat\beta_1 = \operatorname{logit}\hat\pi_1$, which give the formulas. For the variance,
with $w_g = m_g\hat\pi_g(1 - \hat\pi_g)$,
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX} &= \begin{pmatrix} w_0 + w_1 & w_1 \\ w_1 & w_1 \end{pmatrix} , \\
\bigl[(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\bigr]_{22} &= \frac{w_0 + w_1}{w_0w_1} = \frac{1}{w_0} + \frac{1}{w_1} ,
\end{align*}
and $\dfrac{1}{w_g} = \dfrac{1}{m_g\hat\pi_g(1 - \hat\pi_g)} = \dfrac{1}{m_g\hat\pi_g} + \dfrac{1}{m_g(1 - \hat\pi_g)} = \dfrac{1}{y_g} + \dfrac{1}{m_g - y_g}$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Inference in logistic regression}
The exact distribution of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ is intractable, and inference rests on large-sample approximations.
The matrix $\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol\beta) = \mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX}$, minus the second-derivative matrix, is the \emph{information matrix}.

\begin{thm}[Large-sample distribution]\label{thm:wald}
For large samples, approximately,
\[
\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} \sim N\bigl(\boldsymbol\beta, (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\bigr) ,
\]
and the variance is estimated by $(\mathbf{X}^\top\hat{\mathbf{W}}\mathbf{X})^{-1}$, with $\hat{\mathbf{W}}$ evaluated at $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Derivation]
Expand the score about the true $\boldsymbol\beta$: $\mathbf{0} = \mathbf{U}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) \approx \mathbf{U}(\boldsymbol\beta) - \mathcal{I}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta)$, so
$\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta \approx \mathcal{I}^{-1}\mathbf{U}(\boldsymbol\beta)$. Now $\mathbf{U}(\boldsymbol\beta) = \mathbf{X}^\top(\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol\mu)$ has mean $\mathbf{0}$ and, since the $y_i$ are independent
with variances $w_i$, variance $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX} = \mathcal{I}$. It is a sum of many independent terms, so by the central limit
theorem it is approximately normal. Then $\mathcal{I}^{-1}\mathbf{U}$ is approximately normal with mean $\mathbf{0}$ and variance
$\mathcal{I}^{-1}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}^{-1} = \mathcal{I}^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

So the \textbf{Wald test} of $H_0\colon \beta_j = 0$ uses $Z_0 = \hat\beta_j/\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_j)$, compared with the standard normal, and an
approximate $100(1 - \alpha)\%$ confidence interval for $\beta_j$ is $\hat\beta_j \pm z_{\alpha/2}\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_j)$. Exponentiating its end
points gives an interval for the odds ratio $e^{\beta_j}$.

\subhead{Deviance and the likelihood ratio test}
The largest log-likelihood any model can reach is that of the \textbf{saturated model}, which has a separate
parameter for every group and so fits $\hat\pi_i = y_i/m_i$ exactly. The \textbf{deviance} of a model measures how far
it falls short:
\[
D = 2\bigl[\ell(\text{saturated}) - \ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})\bigr]
= 2\sum_{i=1}^n\Bigl[y_i\log\frac{y_i}{\hat\mu_i} + (m_i - y_i)\log\frac{m_i - y_i}{m_i - \hat\mu_i}\Bigr] ,
\]
the second form by substituting $\pi_i = y_i/m_i$ and $\pi_i = \hat\mu_i/m_i$ in the first expression for $\ell$; a term with
$y_i = 0$ or $y_i = m_i$ is taken as $0$. The deviance plays the part of the residual sum of squares: it is never negative,
and it is zero only for a perfect fit.

\begin{thm}[Likelihood ratio test]\label{thm:lr-test}
Let model $M_0$ be obtained from model $M_1$ by setting $q$ of its coefficients to zero. If $M_0$ is correct,
then for large samples, approximately,
\[
D(M_0) - D(M_1) = 2\bigl[\ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1) - \ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_0)\bigr] \sim \chi^2_q .
\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Derivation]
The saturated log-likelihood cancels in the difference, which gives the equality. For the distribution,
consider first the case where $M_0$ fixes all $p$ coefficients at their true value $\boldsymbol\beta$. Expanding $\ell$ about $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$,
where the gradient is zero,
\[
\ell(\boldsymbol\beta) \approx \ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) - \tfrac12(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta)^\top\mathcal{I}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta) .
\]
So $2[\ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) - \ell(\boldsymbol\beta)] \approx \mathbf{v}^\top\mathbf{v}$ with $\mathbf{v} = \mathcal{I}^{1/2}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta)$, which by Theorem~\ref{thm:wald} is
approximately $N(\mathbf{0}, \mathbf{I}_p)$. A sum of $p$ squared independent standard normals is $\chi^2_p$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:idempotent-chisq} with $\mathbf{A} = \mathbf{I}_p$). When $M_0$ fixes only $q$ coefficients, the same expansion applies to
both fits, and the $p - q$ coefficients estimated in both cancel, leaving $q$ squared approximately
independent standard normals.
\end{proof}

This is the analogue of the extra-sum-of-squares $F$-test. In particular, comparing a model with the
intercept-only model tests whether the regressors matter at all. The likelihood ratio test is generally more
reliable than the Wald test, which can behave badly when a coefficient is large.

\subhead{Goodness of fit}
When the $m_i$ are reasonably large, the saturated model is a sensible alternative, and the deviance of the
fitted model is approximately $\chi^2_{n-p}$ if the model is correct. A large deviance relative to $n - p$
signals lack of fit. A similar statistic is \textbf{Pearson's chi-square},
\[
X^2 = \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{(y_i - \hat\mu_i)^2}{m_i\hat\pi_i(1 - \hat\pi_i)} ,
\]
the sum of the squared \emph{Pearson residuals} $(y_i - \hat\mu_i)/\sqrt{m_i\hat\pi_i(1 - \hat\pi_i)}$. For ungrouped binary data ($m_i = 1$)
neither statistic has a $\chi^2$ distribution, and goodness of fit is checked by grouping the observations by their
fitted probabilities (the Hosmer--Lemeshow test) or by plotting residuals.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:dose-inference}
For the insecticide data of Example~\ref{exa:dose}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item test whether dose affects mortality, by the Wald and likelihood ratio tests;
\item find a $95\%$ confidence interval for the odds ratio;
\item check the fit;
\item estimate the dose that kills half the insects, the $\mathrm{ED}_{50}$, with its standard error.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
It is easiest to work with the centred dose $u = x - 3$, for which $\operatorname{logit}\hat\pi = \hat\alpha_0 + \hat\beta_1u$ with
$\hat\alpha_0 = -3.036 + 3(1.012) = 0$. The weights $w_i = 20\hat\pi_i(1 - \hat\pi_i)$ are
\[
2.062, \quad 3.910, \quad 5.000, \quad 3.910, \quad 2.062 ,
\]
symmetric about $u = 0$, so $\sum w_iu_i = 0$ and $\mathbf{X}^\top\hat{\mathbf{W}}\mathbf{X}$ is diagonal, with entries
$\sum w_i = 16.944$ and $\sum w_iu_i^2 = 2(4 \times 2.062 + 3.910) = 24.314$.

(a) $\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_1) = 1/\sqrt{24.314} = 0.2028$, and the Wald statistic is $Z_0 = 1.012/0.2028 = 4.99$, far beyond $1.96$.
For the likelihood ratio test, the intercept-only model fits $\hat\pi = 50/100 = 0.5$ everywhere, so $\hat\mu_i = 10$. Dose $3$ contributes nothing to its deviance, and doses $5$ and $4$ contribute the same as doses $1$ and $2$ (with $y$ and $20 - y$
exchanged), so
\begin{align*}
D(M_0) &= 2 \times 2\Bigl[\bigl(2\log\tfrac{2}{10} + 18\log\tfrac{18}{10}\bigr) + \bigl(6\log\tfrac{6}{10} + 14\log\tfrac{14}{10}\bigr)\Bigr] \\
&= 4(7.361 + 1.646) = 36.03 .
\end{align*} The fitted model has
\[
D(M_1) = 0.0566 + 0.1114 + 0 + 0.1114 + 0.0566 = 0.336 ,
\]
so $D(M_0) - D(M_1) = 35.69$, against $\chi^2_{0.05,1} = 3.84$. Both tests reject $H_0\colon \beta_1 = 0$ decisively.

(b) A $95\%$ interval for $\beta_1$ is $1.012 \pm 1.96(0.2028) = (0.615, 1.410)$, and for the odds ratio
$(e^{0.615}, e^{1.410}) = (1.85, 4.09)$.

(c) $D(M_1) = 0.336$ on $n - p = 5 - 2 = 3$ degrees of freedom, far below $\chi^2_{0.05,3} = 7.81$, and Pearson's $X^2 = 0.337$
agrees. The logistic model fits well.

(d) $\pi = \frac12$ where the logit is $0$: $\hat\alpha_0 + \hat\beta_1u = 0$, so $\hat u_{50} = -\hat\alpha_0/\hat\beta_1 = 0$ and $\widehat{\mathrm{ED}}_{50} = 3$. By the
delta method, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:turning}, with $\operatorname{Cov}(\hat\alpha_0, \hat\beta_1) = 0$ and $\hat\alpha_0 = 0$,
\[
\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat u_{50}) = \frac{\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat\alpha_0)}{\hat\beta_1^2} + \frac{\hat\alpha_0^2\,\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat\beta_1)}{\hat\beta_1^4}
= \frac{1/16.944}{1.012^2} + 0 = 0.0576 ,
\]
so $\operatorname{se}(\widehat{\mathrm{ED}}_{50}) = 0.240$, and an approximate $95\%$ interval is $3 \pm 0.47$, from $2.53$ to $3.47$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}\label{exa:vaccine}
In a study of a vaccine, $10$ of $100$ vaccinated people became infected, and $30$ of $100$ unvaccinated people. Fit
$\operatorname{logit}\pi = \beta_0 + \beta_1z$, where $z = 1$ for the vaccinated, and estimate the odds ratio.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:logit-indicator}, with the unvaccinated as group $0$,
\[
\hat\beta_0 = \log\frac{30}{70} = -0.847, \qquad \hat\beta_1 = \log\frac{10 \times 70}{30 \times 90} = \log 0.259 = -1.350 ,
\]
and
\[
\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_1) = \sqrt{\tfrac{1}{30} + \tfrac{1}{70} + \tfrac{1}{10} + \tfrac{1}{90}} = \sqrt{0.1587} = 0.398 .
\]
The Wald statistic is $-1.350/0.398 = -3.39$. A $95\%$ interval for $\beta_1$ is $-1.350 \pm 0.781$, so the odds ratio is
$0.26$, with interval $(0.12, 0.57)$. Vaccination cuts the odds of infection to about a quarter. (In terms of
probabilities, from $0.30$ to $0.10$: the odds ratio and the ratio of probabilities differ when the probabilities are
not small.)
\end{solution}

\subsection{The exponential family and generalized linear models}
Logistic regression is one member of a family. A random variable $y$ belongs to the \textbf{exponential
family} if its density or probability function has the form
\[
f(y; \theta, \phi) = \exp\Bigl\{\frac{y\theta - b(\theta)}{a(\phi)} + c(y, \phi)\Bigr\} ,
\]
where $\theta$ is the \emph{natural parameter}, $\phi$ a \emph{dispersion parameter}, and $a$, $b$, $c$ are known functions.

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Normal}, $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$: $\log f = \dfrac{y\mu - \mu^2/2}{\sigma^2} - \dfrac{y^2}{2\sigma^2} - \tfrac12\log(2\pi\sigma^2)$, so $\theta = \mu$,
$b(\theta) = \theta^2/2$, $a(\phi) = \sigma^2$.
\item \emph{Poisson}, mean $\mu$: $\log f = y\log\mu - \mu - \log y!$, so $\theta = \log\mu$, $b(\theta) = e^{\theta}$, $a(\phi) = 1$.
\item \emph{Bernoulli}, $P(y = 1) = \pi$: $\log f = y\log\frac{\pi}{1 - \pi} + \log(1 - \pi)$, so $\theta = \operatorname{logit}\pi$, $b(\theta) = \log(1 + e^{\theta})$,
$a(\phi) = 1$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{thm}\label{thm:expfam-moments}
If $y$ belongs to the exponential family, then
\[
E(y) = b'(\theta) , \qquad \operatorname{Var}(y) = a(\phi)\,b''(\theta) .
\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For every $\theta$, $\int f(y; \theta, \phi)\,dy = 1$ (a sum for a discrete $y$). Differentiating with respect to $\theta$ under the integral,
\[
0 = \int \frac{y - b'(\theta)}{a(\phi)}\,f\,dy = \frac{E(y) - b'(\theta)}{a(\phi)} ,
\]
so $E(y) = b'(\theta)$. Differentiating $\int (y - b'(\theta))f\,dy = 0$ once more,
\[
0 = \int\Bigl[-b''(\theta) + \frac{(y - b'(\theta))^2}{a(\phi)}\Bigr]f\,dy = -b''(\theta) + \frac{\operatorname{Var}(y)}{a(\phi)} ,
\]
which gives the variance.
\end{proof}

So the mean determines $\theta$, and the variance is a function of the mean: $\operatorname{Var}(y) = a(\phi)V(\mu)$ with
$V(\mu) = b''(\theta)$, the \emph{variance function}. For the normal $V(\mu) = 1$, for the Poisson $b''(\theta) = e^\theta = \mu$, and for the
Bernoulli $b''(\theta) = e^\theta/(1 + e^\theta)^2 = \pi(1 - \pi)$, as they should be.

A \textbf{generalized linear model} has three components:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item a \emph{random component}: $y_1, \dots, y_n$ independent, from the same exponential family, with means $\mu_i$;
\item a \emph{linear predictor}: $\eta_i = \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta$;
\item a \emph{link function}: a smooth monotone $g$ with $g(\mu_i) = \eta_i$.
\end{enumerate}
The link for which $g(\mu) = \theta$, so that the natural parameter is the linear predictor, is the \textbf{canonical link}:
the identity for the normal, the log for the Poisson, and the logit for the binomial. The normal linear model of
Sections 2 to 10 is the GLM with normal errors and identity link. Other links are possible: the probit,
$g(\pi) = \Phi^{-1}(\pi)$, gives an S-curve very like the logistic.

\begin{thm}[Fitting a GLM]\label{thm:irls}
Let $\mathbf{W} = \operatorname{diag}(w_i)$ with $w_i = 1/\bigl[V(\mu_i)g'(\mu_i)^2\bigr]$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The score is $\mathbf{U} = \frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol\mu)$, where $\mathbf{G} = \operatorname{diag}(g'(\mu_i))$, and its variance (the
information matrix) is $\mathcal{I} = \frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX}$.
\item Fisher scoring, $\boldsymbol\beta^{(t+1)} = \boldsymbol\beta^{(t)} + \mathcal{I}^{-1}\mathbf{U}$, is iteratively reweighted least squares:
\[
\boldsymbol\beta^{(t+1)} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{Wz}, \qquad z_i = \eta_i + (y_i - \mu_i)g'(\mu_i) .
\]
\item For the canonical link, $\mathbf{U} = \frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top(\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol\mu)$, and Fisher scoring is Newton's method.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(i) The log-likelihood of observation $i$ is $\ell_i = [y_i\theta_i - b(\theta_i)]/a(\phi) + c(y_i, \phi)$, so
$\partial\ell_i/\partial\theta_i = (y_i - \mu_i)/a(\phi)$. By the chain rule through $\theta_i \to \mu_i \to \eta_i \to \boldsymbol\beta$, with
$d\mu/d\theta = b''(\theta) = V(\mu)$ and $d\eta/d\mu = g'(\mu)$,
\[
\frac{\partial\ell_i}{\partial\beta_j} = \frac{y_i - \mu_i}{a(\phi)}\cdot\frac{1}{V(\mu_i)}\cdot\frac{1}{g'(\mu_i)}\cdot x_{ij}
= \frac{1}{a(\phi)}\,x_{ij}w_ig'(\mu_i)(y_i - \mu_i) .
\]
Summing over $i$ gives $\mathbf{U}$. The $y_i$ are independent with $\operatorname{Var}(y_i) = a(\phi)V(\mu_i)$, so
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\mathbf{U}) = \frac{1}{a(\phi)^2}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WG}\bigl[a(\phi)\operatorname{diag}(V(\mu_i))\bigr]\mathbf{GWX} = \frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX} ,
\]
because $w_ig'(\mu_i)^2V(\mu_i) = 1$.

(ii) With $\boldsymbol\eta = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)}$, $\mathcal{I}\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)} + \mathbf{U} = \frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\bigl[\boldsymbol\eta + \mathbf{G}(\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol\mu)\bigr] = \frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{Wz}$, and
multiplying by $\mathcal{I}^{-1} = a(\phi)(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}$ gives the formula; $a(\phi)$ cancels.

(iii) For the canonical link $\eta = \theta$, so $g'(\mu) = d\theta/d\mu = 1/V(\mu)$ and $w_ig'(\mu_i) = 1/[V(\mu_i)g'(\mu_i)] = 1$. So $\mathbf{U} = \mathbf{X}^\top(\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol\mu)/a(\phi)$, and its derivative is
\[
-\frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top\operatorname{diag}\Bigl(\frac{d\mu_i}{d\eta_i}\Bigr)\mathbf{X} = -\frac{1}{a(\phi)}\mathbf{X}^\top\operatorname{diag}\bigl(V(\mu_i)\bigr)\mathbf{X} .
\]
This does not involve $\mathbf{y}$, and it equals $-\mathcal{I}$, since $w_i = 1/[V(\mu_i)(1/V(\mu_i))^2] = V(\mu_i)$. So the Newton step and the scoring step coincide.
\end{proof}

For the binomial with the logit link, $w_i = m_i\pi_i(1 - \pi_i)$ and $z_i = \eta_i + (y_i - \mu_i)/w_i$, which is
Theorem~\ref{thm:irls-logistic}. For the normal with identity link, $\mathbf{W} = \mathbf{I}$ and $\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{y}$, so one step gives the
least-squares estimate (Problem~\ref{prob:glm-normal}). The results on the approximate normality of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ and on
the likelihood ratio test carry over, with $\mathcal{I}$ in place of $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX}$. The deviance is defined as before,
$D = 2a(\phi)[\ell(\text{saturated}) - \ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})]$; for the normal model it is $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\py/\col/\num/\name/\eqn in {
 0/2.4/blue/1/{Linear predictor}/{$\eta_i = \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta, \ \mu_i = g^{-1}(\eta_i)$},
 7.4/2.4/orange/2/{Working response}/{$z_i = \eta_i + (y_i - \mu_i)g'(\mu_i)$},
 7.4/0/green/3/{Weights}/{$w_i = 1/[V(\mu_i)g'(\mu_i)^2]$},
 0/0/red/4/{Weighted least squares}/{$\boldsymbol\beta = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{Wz}$}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,\py)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (0,0) rectangle (6.0,1.6);
  \node[circle,fill=\col!60!black,text=white,inner sep=1.5pt,font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (0.35,1.25) {\num};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\small\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.6,1.25) {\name};
  \node[font=\small] at (3.0,0.55) {\eqn};
 \end{scope}
}
\draw[-Stealth,very thick,gray] (6.15,3.2) -- (7.25,3.2);
\draw[-Stealth,very thick,gray] (10.4,2.3) -- (10.4,1.7);
\draw[-Stealth,very thick,gray] (7.25,0.8) -- (6.15,0.8);
\draw[-Stealth,very thick,gray] (3.0,1.7) -- (3.0,2.3);
\node[font=\scriptsize,text=gray!30!black,align=center] at (6.7,2.0) {repeat until\\$\boldsymbol\beta$ settles};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Iteratively reweighted least squares (Theorem~\ref{thm:irls}). Each cycle is one weighted least
squares fit of the working response; the weights are recomputed from the current fitted means.}
\label{fig:irls}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Poisson regression}
For counts, the natural model is $y_i \sim \text{Poisson}(\mu_i)$ with the canonical log link,
\[
\log\mu_i = \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta , \qquad \mu_i = e^{\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta} ,
\]
which keeps every mean positive. Increasing $x_j$ by one unit multiplies the mean by $e^{\beta_j}$, the \textbf{rate
ratio}. By Theorem~\ref{thm:irls}, the likelihood equations are $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\mu}$, the weights are $w_i = \mu_i$, and the
working response is $z_i = \eta_i + (y_i - \mu_i)/\mu_i$. The log-likelihood is $\sum[y_i\log\mu_i - \mu_i - \log y_i!]$, and the saturated model
has $\hat\mu_i = y_i$, so the deviance is
\[
D = 2\sum_{i=1}^n\Bigl[y_i\log\frac{y_i}{\hat\mu_i} - (y_i - \hat\mu_i)\Bigr] ,
\]
in which the second term adds to zero when the model has an intercept (Problem~\ref{prob:poisson-dev}).

\subhead{Exposure and offsets}
Counts are often collected over different amounts of exposure $t_i$: different lengths of time, numbers of
people or lengths of road. Then the model is for the \emph{rate} $\mu_i/t_i$, $\log(\mu_i/t_i) = \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta$, that is,
\[
\log\mu_i = \log t_i + \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta .
\]
The term $\log t_i$ is an \textbf{offset}: a regressor whose coefficient is fixed at $1$, not estimated.

\subhead{Overdispersion}
The Poisson model forces $\operatorname{Var}(y) = \mu$. Counts are often more variable than that, because of
differences between units that the regressors do not capture. Then the estimates $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ are still
reasonable, but the standard errors from $(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{WX})^{-1}$ are too small and the tests too optimistic.
Overdispersion shows itself as a deviance or Pearson $X^2 = \sum(y_i - \hat\mu_i)^2/\hat\mu_i$ much larger than $n - p$. A simple
remedy, the \emph{quasi-Poisson} model, assumes $\operatorname{Var}(y) = \phi\mu$, estimates $\hat\phi = X^2/(n - p)$, and multiplies every
standard error by $\sqrt{\hat\phi}$. The same applies to binomial data with $m_i > 1$.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:faults}
The numbers of faults recorded in a year on six machines of ages $0$ to $5$ years were:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|cccccc}
Age, $x$ & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 \\
\hline
Faults, $y$ & 2 & 3 & 6 & 7 & 11 & 15 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit a Poisson regression with log link, test the effect of age, and check the fit.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Starting from $\beta_0 = \log\bar y = \log(44/6) = 1.992$ and $\beta_1 = 0$, iteratively reweighted least squares gives
\[
(1.116, 0.351), \quad (0.870, 0.376), \quad (0.838, 0.381), \quad (0.837, 0.381) ,
\]
and then no further change:
\[
\log\hat\mu = 0.837 + 0.381x .
\]
The fitted counts are $2.31, 3.38, 4.95, 7.24, 10.60, 15.52$. They add to $44 = \sum y$, and
$\sum x\hat\mu = 155 = \sum xy$: the two likelihood equations hold.

The rate ratio is $e^{0.381} = 1.46$: each year of age multiplies the expected number of faults by about $1.46$.
From $(\mathbf{X}^\top\hat{\mathbf{W}}\mathbf{X})^{-1}$, $\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_1) = 0.100$, so $Z_0 = 3.81$, and a $95\%$ interval for the rate ratio is
$(e^{0.381 - 0.196}, e^{0.381 + 0.196}) = (1.20, 1.78)$.

The intercept-only model has $\hat\mu_i = 44/6$ and deviance $16.77$. The deviance contributions of the fitted model are
$0.043, 0.045, 0.209, 0.008, 0.015, 0.018$, so $D = 0.338$, and the likelihood ratio statistic is $16.77 - 0.34 = 16.43$,
against $\chi^2_{0.05,1} = 3.84$. Age matters. With $D = 0.338$ and $X^2 = 0.348$ on $6 - 2 = 4$ degrees of freedom, there is no
sign of lack of fit or of overdispersion; in fact the counts vary rather less than a Poisson model would
lead one to expect. A machine of age $6$ would be predicted to have $e^{0.837 + 6(0.381)} = 22.7$ faults in a year, an
extrapolation to be treated with caution. Figure~\ref{fig:faults} shows the fit.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=1.3,yscale=0.17]
\draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (6.2,0) node[right,black,font=\small] {age $x$};
\draw[-Stealth,gray] (0,0) -- (0,19) node[above,black,font=\small] {faults $y$};
\foreach \t in {0,...,5} \draw[gray] (\t,0) -- (\t,-0.5) node[below,black,font=\scriptsize] {\t};
\foreach \t in {5,10,15} \draw[gray] (0,\t) -- (-0.08,\t) node[left,black,font=\scriptsize] {\t};
\draw[-,thick,blue!60!black] plot[domain=0:5.5,samples=40] (\x,{exp(0.837+0.381*\x)});
\foreach \a/\b in {0/2,1/3,2/6,3/7,4/11,5/15}
 \fill[black] (\a,\b) circle (0.055 and 0.42);
\node[font=\scriptsize,text=blue!50!black,anchor=west] at (0.3,15) {$\hat\mu = e^{0.837 + 0.381x}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The fault counts of Example~\ref{exa:faults} with the fitted Poisson regression curve. On the log scale
the curve is a straight line; each year of age multiplies the mean by $1.46$.}
\label{fig:faults}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:ed90}
For the insecticide data of Example~\ref{exa:dose}, with $\operatorname{logit}\hat\pi = -3.036 + 1.012x$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item estimate the probability of death at dose $4.5$;
\item estimate the dose that kills $90\%$ of the insects.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $\hat\eta = -3.036 + 1.012(4.5) = 1.518$, so $\hat\pi = 1/(1 + e^{-1.518}) = 0.820$.

(b) $\pi = 0.9$ where $\eta = \log(0.9/0.1) = \log 9 = 2.197$, so $x = (2.197 + 3.036)/1.012 = 5.17$. This is just beyond the largest dose
used, so it is a mild extrapolation.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:poisson-indicator}
Five junctions of design $A$ had $40$ accidents in a year and five junctions of design $B$ had $20$. Fit the Poisson model
$\log\mu = \beta_0 + \beta_1z$, with $z = 1$ for design $B$, assuming every junction has the same mean within its design. Show
that $\hat\beta_1 = \log(\bar y_B/\bar y_A)$ and $\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat\beta_1) = 1/Y_A + 1/Y_B$, where $Y_A$, $Y_B$ are the group totals, and test
whether the designs differ.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The likelihood equations $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}^\top\hat{\boldsymbol\mu}$, with columns $\mathbf{1}$ and $\mathbf{z}$, say that the fitted totals match the
observed totals overall and in group $B$, hence in each group: $5e^{\hat\beta_0} = Y_A$ and $5e^{\hat\beta_0 + \hat\beta_1} = Y_B$. So
$e^{\hat\beta_0} = \bar y_A$ and $e^{\hat\beta_1} = \bar y_B/\bar y_A$. The weights are $w_i = \hat\mu_i$, so, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:logit-indicator},
\[
\mathbf{X}^\top\hat{\mathbf{W}}\mathbf{X} = \begin{pmatrix} Y_A + Y_B & Y_B \\ Y_B & Y_B \end{pmatrix} ,
\qquad \widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat\beta_1) = \frac{Y_A + Y_B}{Y_AY_B} = \frac{1}{Y_A} + \frac{1}{Y_B} .
\]
Here $\hat\beta_1 = \log(4/8) = -0.693$ and $\operatorname{se} = \sqrt{1/40 + 1/20} = 0.274$, so $Z_0 = -2.53$, beyond $-1.96$. Design $B$ halves the
accident rate, with a $95\%$ interval for the rate ratio of $(e^{-1.230}, e^{-0.156}) = (0.29, 0.86)$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:gamma}
The gamma distribution with mean $\mu$ and shape $\nu$ has density
$f(y) = \dfrac{1}{\Gamma(\nu)}\Bigl(\dfrac{\nu}{\mu}\Bigr)^\nu y^{\nu - 1}e^{-\nu y/\mu}$, $y > 0$. Show that it belongs to the exponential family, and find
$\theta$, $b(\theta)$, $a(\phi)$, the variance function and the canonical link.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\[
\log f = \nu\Bigl(-\frac{y}{\mu} - \log\mu\Bigr) + \nu\log\nu + (\nu - 1)\log y - \log\Gamma(\nu) .
\]
This has the form $[y\theta - b(\theta)]/a(\phi) + c(y, \phi)$ with $\theta = -1/\mu$, $b(\theta) = \log\mu = -\log(-\theta)$ and $a(\phi) = 1/\nu$. Then
$b'(\theta) = -1/\theta = \mu$, as it must be, and $b''(\theta) = 1/\theta^2 = \mu^2$, so $V(\mu) = \mu^2$ and $\operatorname{Var}(y) = \mu^2/\nu$: the standard deviation
is proportional to the mean, the situation of Section~6 in which the log transformation stabilises the variance.
The canonical link is $g(\mu) = -1/\mu$, usually used as $1/\mu$, the reciprocal link.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:poisson-dev}
Show that for a Poisson regression with log link and an intercept, $\sum(y_i - \hat\mu_i) = 0$, so that
$D = 2\sum y_i\log(y_i/\hat\mu_i)$. Check the deviance of Example~\ref{exa:faults} with this formula.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:irls}(iii), the likelihood equation for the column of ones is $\sum y_i = \sum\hat\mu_i$, so the term
$\sum(y_i - \hat\mu_i)$ vanishes. For the faults data,
\begin{align*}
D &= 2\Bigl[2\log\tfrac{2}{2.309} + 3\log\tfrac{3}{3.380} + 6\log\tfrac{6}{4.948} \\
&\qquad + 7\log\tfrac{7}{7.243} + 11\log\tfrac{11}{10.602} + 15\log\tfrac{15}{15.518}\Bigr] \\
&= 2\bigl[-0.2877 - 0.3581 + 1.1565 - 0.2386 + 0.4059 - 0.5092\bigr] = 0.338 ,
\end{align*}
as before.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:overdispersion}
A Poisson regression with $p = 3$ coefficients was fitted to counts from $n = 9$ hospitals. A coefficient had
$\hat\beta_j = 0.42$ with $\operatorname{se} = 0.15$, and Pearson's $X^2 = 18.6$. Is there evidence of overdispersion? Re-assess the
coefficient using the quasi-Poisson model.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
$X^2 = 18.6$ on $n - p = 6$ degrees of freedom exceeds $\chi^2_{0.05,6} = 12.59$, and $\hat\phi = 18.6/6 = 3.1$: the counts vary about three
times as much as a Poisson model allows. The corrected standard error is $0.15\sqrt{3.1} = 0.264$, so $Z_0$ falls from
$0.42/0.15 = 2.8$ to $0.42/0.264 = 1.59$, no longer significant at the $5\%$ level. The apparent effect was largely
a consequence of understating the variability.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:glm-normal}
Consider the normal distribution with identity link. Show that one step of
iteratively reweighted least squares, from any starting value, gives the least-squares estimate. Show also that the
deviance is $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Here $V(\mu) = 1$ and $g'(\mu) = 1$, so $w_i = 1$ and $z_i = \eta_i + (y_i - \mu_i) = y_i$, since $\eta_i = \mu_i$. The step is
$(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y}$, whatever $\boldsymbol\beta^{(t)}$ was. For the deviance, $\ell = -\sum(y_i - \mu_i)^2/(2\sigma^2) + \text{const}$; the
saturated model has $\mu_i = y_i$ and $\ell = \text{const}$, so
$D = 2\sigma^2[\ell(\text{saturated}) - \ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})] = \sum(y_i - \hat\mu_i)^2 = SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$. The likelihood ratio test is then
based on differences of residual sums of squares, as in the extra-sum-of-squares principle.
\end{solution}

\section{Non linear estimation}
Every model so far has been linear in its parameters, even when, like a polynomial, it was curved in $x$.
Many models from science and engineering are not. A drug's concentration in the blood decays as
$\theta_1e^{-\theta_2x}$; the rate of an enzyme reaction follows $\theta_1x/(\theta_2 + x)$; a population grows towards a ceiling along an
S-shaped curve. The parameters have physical meanings: a half-life, a maximum rate, a ceiling. This
section treats the \textbf{non-linear regression model}
\[
y_i = f(\mathbf{x}_i, \boldsymbol\theta) + \varepsilon_i , \qquad i = 1, \dots, n ,
\]
where $f$ is a known function, non-linear in the $p$ parameters $\boldsymbol\theta = (\theta_1, \dots, \theta_p)^\top$, and the $\varepsilon_i$ are
independent with mean $0$ and variance $\sigma^2$. Least squares still defines the estimates, but they can no longer
be written down: they must be found by iteration, and their properties hold only approximately.

\subsection{Non-linear models}
A model is \textbf{linear} in the parameters if each derivative $\partial f/\partial\theta_j$ is free of $\boldsymbol\theta$. For
$f = \theta_1 + \theta_2x + \theta_3x^2$ the derivatives are $1$, $x$, $x^2$; for $f = \theta_1e^{-\theta_2x}$ they are $e^{-\theta_2x}$ and $-\theta_1xe^{-\theta_2x}$, which
involve the parameters, so the model is non-linear. Figure~\ref{fig:nl-models} shows four common models.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \px/\py/\col/\num/\name/\eqn in {
 0/5.0/blue/1/{Exponential decay}/{$\theta_1e^{-\theta_2x}$},
 7.6/5.0/green/2/{Michaelis--Menten}/{$\theta_1x/(\theta_2 + x)$},
 0/0/orange/3/{Asymptotic regression}/{$\theta_1 - \theta_2e^{-\theta_3x}$},
 7.6/0/red/4/{Logistic growth}/{$\theta_1/(1 + \theta_2e^{-\theta_3x})$}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,\py)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-1.05,-0.95) rectangle (6.3,3.95);
